A monoidal category of dependently sorted algebraic theories I: syntax
Daniel Almeida

Abstract
This is the first of a pair of papers where we construct and investigate a closed monoidal structure on the category of generalized algebraic theories (in the sense of Cartmell). In the present text, as a starting point, we define the tensor product , 𝔸 ⊗ 𝔹 \mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B} , between two generalized algebraic theories 𝔸 \mathbb{A} and 𝔹 \mathbb{B} . This is done syntactically via an algorithm that uses the axioms of 𝔸 \mathbb{A} and 𝔹 \mathbb{B} in a recursive manner to produce those of 𝔸 ⊗ 𝔹 \mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B} . We provide examples of known structures that are recovered by our construction, such as tensor products of Lawvere theories, “cellular” products of dependent type signatures, and theories of diagrams and of displayed structures. It will be verified in the second volume that, as suggested by these special cases, the category of family-valued models Mod ​ ( 𝔸 ⊗ 𝔹 , Fam ) \text{Mod}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B},\text{Fam}) is isomorphic to Mod ​ ( 𝔸 , Mod ​ ( 𝔹 ) ) \text{Mod}(\mathbb{A},\textbf{Mod}(\mathbb{B})) and to Mod ​ ( 𝔹 , Mod ​ ( 𝔸 ) ) \text{Mod}(\mathbb{B},\textbf{Mod}(\mathbb{A})) for certain contextual categories Mod ​ ( 𝔸 ) \textbf{Mod}(\mathbb{A}) and Mod ​ ( 𝔹 ) \textbf{Mod}(\mathbb{B}) whose underlying categories are equivalent to Mod ​ ( 𝔸 , Fam ) \text{Mod}(\mathbb{A},\text{Fam}) and to Mod ​ ( 𝔹 , Fam ) \text{Mod}(\mathbb{B},\text{Fam}) , respectively. Moreover, the cellular structure of the tensor product is obtained by combining, via a pushout-product operation, those of the two theories. We also construct a functor ⊗ 𝔸 , 𝔹 : 𝒞 ( 𝔸 ) × 𝒞 ( 𝔹 ) → 𝒞 ( 𝔸 ⊗ 𝔹 ) \otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}) comparing the associated contextual categories, and describe isomorphisms of the forms ( 𝔸 ⊗ 𝔹 ) ⊗ ℂ ≅ 𝔸 ⊗ ( 𝔹 ⊗ ℂ ) (\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})\otimes\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{A}\otimes(\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{C}) and 𝔸 ⊗ 𝔹 ≅ 𝔹 ⊗ 𝔸 \mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A} . In the sequel paper we will describe a universal property of ⊗ 𝔸 , 𝔹 \otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}} , which will induce functoriality of the tensor product and thus allow us to check the monoidal category conditions.



1
Introduction

Many kinds of algebraic structures used in mathematics, such as groups, rings, and vector spaces over a given field, can be described as models of equational theories: they can be specified from a set of operation symbols and a set of equations between pairs of expressions built recursively from operation symbols and variables. The study of such classes of structures is the main theme of classical universal algebra, as developed by A. N. Whitehead, G. Birkhoff and others from the late 19th century to the first half of the 20th century. In 1963, in his doctoral thesis (reprinted as
[
20
]
), W. Lawvere recast the foundations of the subject in terms of category theory by showing how equational theories and their semantics can be encoded via a certain class of categories; these were originally named
algebraic theories
, and later also became known as
Lawvere theories
.

This approach is motivated by the correspondence, for a theory
\mathbb{A}
, between (1) homomorphisms between
\mathbb{A}
-models free on a finite number of generators, and (2) finite sequences of “definable operations” (constructed from variables and the theory’s basic operations) taken up to provable equality (using the theory’s axioms). A consequence of this is that the category of models
\text{Mod}(\mathbb{A})
can be reconstructed from its full subcategory, say
A
, whose objects are coproducts of finitely many copies of the free model on one generator. More precisely,
\text{Mod}(\mathbb{A})
is equivalent to the full subcategory of
\text{Set}^{A^{op}}
spanned by the functors
M:A^{op}\rightarrow\text{Set}
that preserve finite products. At the level of objects, this equivalence is related to the fact that a
\mathbb{A}
-model is specified by two pieces of data: its underlying set, say
X
, and a coherent choice of a map
X^{n}\rightarrow X
for each definable
n
-ary operation, with the requirement that variables be appropriately interpreted as cartesian projection maps. Now, it still makes sense to consider these data if, instead of a set,
X
is an object of an arbitrary category with finite products
C
. This is an
\mathbb{A}
-model in
C
, which can also be described by a finite-product-preserving functor
A^{op}\rightarrow C
. Thus one can, for some purposes, replace the syntactic treatment of the given class of structures by a purely semantic and presentation-independent one; and this allows for a simple characterization, in categorical language, of
\mathbb{A}
-models and homomorphisms valued in categories other than
Set
.

Notions of finite-limit theory

The categorical perspective on model theory was further developed, in particular, through the study of sketches (initiated by Ehresmann in the 1960s; see e.g.
[
6
,
22
,
1
]
), which are devices used for specifying mathematical structures internal to a category
C
as functors
I\rightarrow C
, where
I
is a directed graph, that map certain diagrams (resp. cones, cocones) in
I
to commutative diagrams (resp. limit cones, colimit cocones) in
C
. A particularly relevant example is that of
finite-limit sketches
: those where one only specifies in
I
, in addition to a set of “to-be-commutative” diagrams, a class of finite cones (and no cocones). In
[
17
]
, Gabriel and Ulmer characterized (in different terminology) the class of categories that occur as categories of
Set
-valued models of finite-limit sketches: those are the
locally finitely presentable
categories, which are the (cocomplete) categories that occur by completing some small finitely cocomplete category under filtered colimits. In fact, a locally finitely presentable category
\mathcal{M}
occurs as the category of finite-limit-preserving functors
\mathcal{M}_{lp}^{op}\rightarrow\text{Set}
where
\mathcal{M}_{lp}\subset\mathcal{M}
is the full subcategory spanned by the finitely presentable objects; moreover, this construction extends to an equivalence of 2-categories
\textbf{Lex}^{op}\simeq\textbf{LPr}_{\omega}
, known as Gabriel-Ulmer duality, where
Lex
consists of finitely complete categories, finite-limit-preserving functors, and natural transformations, and
\textbf{LPr}_{\omega}
of locally finitely presentable categories, filtered-colimit-preserving right adjoint functors, and natural transformations. A finite-limit sketch is then one particular way of presenting a locally finitely presentable category, and this passage only retains the semantic aspects of the sketch (more precisely, it retains enough information to define models of the sketch, as well as morphisms between them, in any category with finite limits). Around the same time, Freyd (see
[
16
]
) also considered a notion of “essentially algebraic” theory, which generalize multisorted algebraic theories by allowing partial operations whose domain can be specified via equations involving previously constructed terms. Up to equivalence, the categories of these are also the locally presentable categories (see
[
1
]
, 3.D).

Generalized algebraic theories and contextual categories

Generalized algebraic theories (gats for short), introduced by J. Cartmell in his doctoral thesis (
[
10
]
; see also
[
11
]
), make use of the structural rules of Martin-Löf’s type theory to extend the framework of multisorted algebraic theories by allowing dependent sorts (also known as dependent types), that is, sorts (thought of as sets) that depend on parameters, given by terms (thought of as elements), coming from other dependent sorts. As explained in Cartmell’s work, the category of models of any essentially algebraic theory can be obtained, up to equivalence, as the category of models of a generalized algebraic theory, and vice versa. In this particular sense, finite-limit sketches, essentially algebraic theories and generalized algebraic theories have the same expressive power. However, using the latter to describe a given kind of structure is often natural due to how, in many cases, one specified the structure in terms of (total) operations and equations involving elements of sets parameterized by previously introduced elements. For example, one can construct a category by specifying a set
Ob
of objects, a set
Ar
of arrows endowed with domain and codomain maps
\text{Ar}\rightarrow\text{Ob}
, a composition operation
\circ:\text{Ar}\times_{\text{Ob}}\text{Ar}\rightarrow\text{Ar}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Ar}\times_{\text{Ob}}(\text{Ar}\times_{\text{Ob}}\text{Ar})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\text{Ar}\times_{\text{Ob}}\text{Ar})\times_{\text{Ob}}\text{Ar}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Ar}\times_{\text{Ob}}\text{Ar}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Ar}\times_{\text{Ob}}\text{Ar}}}
Ar
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

commutes, etc. In the framework of generalized algebraic theories, one can specify a set
Ob
objects, a set of arrows
\text{Ar}(x,y)
for each
x
,
y\in\text{Ob}
, an element
g\circ f\in\text{Ar}(x,z)
for each
f\in\text{Ar}(x,y)
,
g\in\text{Ar}(y,z)
, etc.; the associativity axiom becomes the equality
h\circ(g\circ f)=(h\circ g)\circ f
where
f\in\text{Ar}(x,y)
,
g\in\text{Ar}(y,z)
,
h\in\text{Ar}(z,w)
. Formally, this is done by considering
judgments
that tell us which sorts, terms and equalities can be obtained from a list of variables of previously constructed sorts. In the above example, we could take

\displaystyle\vdash\text{Ob}\;\mathsf{sort},

\displaystyle x,y:\text{Ob}

\displaystyle\vdash\text{Ar}(x,y)\;\mathsf{sort},

\displaystyle x,y,z:\text{Ob},f:\text{Ar}(x,y),g:(y,z)

\displaystyle\vdash\circ(x,y,z,f,g):\text{Ar}(x,z),

and, abbreviating
\circ(x,y,z,f,g)
as
g\circ f
,

x,y,z,w:\text{Ob},f:\text{Ar}(x,y),g:\text{Ar}(y,z),h:\text{Ar}(z,w)\vdash h\circ(g\circ f)\equiv(h\circ g)\circ f:\text{Ar}(x,w).

A list of variables which is correctly typed (in a sense to be made precise), such as the one preceding the
\vdash
in each case, is called a
context
. By using sequences of terms to give an appropriate definition of morphism, contexts can be assembled into a category similarly to how one constructs the Lawvere theory associated with an equational theory.

One of the main achievements of Cartmell’s work was the identification of a class of algebraic structures, called
contextual categories
, that encode the essential structure needed to study interpretations between different generalized algebraic theories (which, in turn, is sufficient to encode the usual set-valued models of a theory). In more detail, each theory
\mathbb{A}
has a syntactic category, denoted by
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})
, whose objects are contexts and whose arrows are context morphisms, both taken up to provable equality. This category inherits more structure from
\mathbb{A}
, namely: (i) each object has a
length
, given by the length of a corresponding context; (ii) dependent sorts define a certain class of arrows, the (length-
1
)
display maps
; (iii) substitution of dependent sorts along context morphisms define certain pullback squares in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T})
, which we refer to as (length-
1
)
distinguished squares
. A contextual category is a category with additional structure intended to encapsulate (i)-(iii) above (as well as several related properties) in an axiomatic way. By suitably assembling the classes of generalized algebraic theories and of contextual categories into categories
GAT
and
Cont
, respectively, the assignment
\mathbb{A}\mapsto\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})
extends to an equivalence of categories
\mathcal{C}:\text{GAT}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
.

Tensor products of theories

Shortly after Lawvere’s introduction of algebraic theories, P. Freyd (
[
15
]
) described a way of combining two such theories
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
into a theory
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
such that for any category with finite products
C
,

\text{Mod}(\mathbb{A},\text{Mod}(\mathbb{B},C))\simeq\text{Mod}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B},C)\simeq\text{Mod}(\mathbb{B},\text{Mod}(\mathbb{A},C)).

In summary,
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
contains (1) one (
n
-ary) operation for each (
n
-ary) operation in
\mathbb{A}
or in
\mathbb{B}
, (2) all axioms from
\mathbb{A}
or from
\mathbb{B}
, and (3) for an
m
-ary operation
f
in
\mathbb{A}
and an
n
-ary operation
g
in
\mathbb{B}
, an axiom

f(g(x_{11},...,x_{1n}),...,g(x_{m1},...,x_{mn}))=g(f(x_{11},...,x_{m1}),...,f(x_{1n},...,x_{mn}))

between
mn
-operations expressed in terms of variables
x_{ij}
for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
. This equality states a form of commutativity between
f
and
g
; by making
f
and
g
range over all possible operations, we conclude that a model of
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
in a category with finite limits
C
is an object
X
endowed with a structure of
\mathbb{A}
-model and a structure of
\mathbb{B}
-model such that if
f
(resp.
g
) is as above, then
f:X^{m}\rightarrow X
(resp.
g:X^{n}\rightarrow X
) is a morphism of
\mathbb{B}
-models (resp. of
\mathbb{A}
-models).

A similar construction is given for finitely cocomplete categories (an in fact for much more general classes of enriched categories) by Kelly in
[
19
]
, and its counterpart for locally finitely presentable categories is discussed in Bird’s doctoral thesis
[
9
]
.

A tensor product of sketches, possibly specifying both limits and colimits, has also been defined in the same vein; see
[
2
]
,
[
1
]
(Exercise 1.l),
[
8
]
. For sketches
I
and
J
, one has equivalences
\text{Mod}(I,\text{Mod}(J,C))\simeq\text{Mod}(I\otimes J,C)\simeq\text{Mod}(J,\text{Mod}(I,C))
provided that the co/limits specified by
I
and by
J
commute in an appropriate sense, and that condition always holds when
I
,
J
are limit sketches. If
I
,
J
are finite-limit sketches, then Bird’s tensor product applied to the respective categories of
Set
-valued models satisfies
\text{Mod}(I)\otimes\text{Mod}(J)\simeq\text{Mod}(I\otimes J)
.

Contributions of the present work

In this article, we define the
tensor product

\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
of two generalized algebraic theories
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
. To motivate the construction, it is useful to think of it as an intended joint generalization of

•

the tensor product of Lawvere theories (
[
15
]
);

•

the cartesian product of locally finite direct categories, which are a categorical model of generalized algebraic theories that only have sort axioms (the
dependent type signatures
from
[
21
]
).

Let us first explain the second point. A locally finite direct category (lfdc for short)
C
corresponds to a generalized algebraic theory whose contextual category is (up to equivalence) opposite to the category
\text{PSh}_{fp}(C)
of finitely presentable presheaves on
C
(which, in this case, are the presheaves whose category of elements is finite). Each object
c
encodes a sort axiom whose context is the
boundary
presheaf
\partial c
obtained by removing the element
id_{c}
from
\mathscr{Y}(c)=\text{Hom}_{C}(-,c)
. The corresponding display map is opposite to the inclusion
\partial c\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}(c)
. In concrete settings, we usually view
C
as a category of basic combinatorial shapes to be used as building blocks for more general ones, namely, the objects of
\text{PSh}_{fp}(C)
: a presheaf
X
on
C
is finitely presentable precisely if it fits into a finite sequence
\emptyset=X_{0}\rightarrow X_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow X_{n}=X
where each
X_{i}\rightarrow X_{i+1}
is a pushout of
\partial c\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}(c)
for some
c\in C
.
1
1

1

All small presheaves on
C
can be obtained in this way if we allow
n
to be any ordinal. However, the finiteness assumption is needed to establish the connection with generalized algebraic theories as they only support finitary sorts and operations (at least classically; see
[
21
]
, Remark 1.2.9, and the infinitary theories from
[
23
]
).

For lfdcs
C
and
D
, the category
C\times D
is also locally finite and direct, and the canonical functor
\text{PSh}(C)\times\text{PSh}(D)\rightarrow\text{PSh}(C\times D)
2
2

2

It is obtained by two applications of pointwise left Kan extension: we take the image of the Yoneda embedding
\mathscr{Y}:C\times D\rightarrow\text{PSh}(C\times D)
along the chain
\text{PSh}(C\times D)^{C\times D}\cong(\text{PSh}(C\times D)^{C})^{D}\simeq\textbf{LPr}(\text{PSh}(C),\text{PSh}(C\times D))^{D}\simeq\textbf{LPr}(\text{PSh}(D),\textbf{LPr}(\text{PSh}(C),\text{PSh}(C\times D)))\subset(\text{PSh}(C\times D)^{\text{PSh}(C)})^{\text{PSh}(D)}\cong\text{PSh}(C\times D)^{\text{PSh}(C)\times\text{PSh}(D)}
.
restricts to a functor

\otimes:\text{PSh}_{fp}(C)\times\text{PSh}_{fp}(D)\rightarrow\text{PSh}_{fp}(C\times D).

Notably, for
c\in C
,
d\in D
,
X\in\text{PSh}_{fp}(C)
,
Y\in\text{PSh}_{fp}(D)
we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{Y}(c)\otimes\mathscr{Y}(d)\cong\mathscr{Y}(c,d)

\displaystyle X\otimes 0\cong 0\cong 0\otimes Y

\displaystyle\partial(c,d)\cong\partial c\otimes\mathscr{Y}(d)\sqcup_{\partial c\otimes\partial d}\mathscr{Y}(c)\otimes\partial d

(Leibniz formula)

This implies in particular that, writing
|-|
for the number of elements of a presheaf,
|X\otimes Y|=|X||Y|
for
X\in\text{PSh}_{fp}(C)
,
Y\in\text{PSh}_{fp}(D)
.

When applied to theories that only have sort axioms, we expect the tensor product of gats to recover
\text{PSh}_{fp}(C\times D)
not in terms of the cartesian product
C\times D
, but by using the Leibniz formula as a first principle. This is accomplished in §
3.3
. In fact, these features will be present in the tensor product of two arbitrary gats
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
: for contexts
X
,
Y
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, resp., we will have a context
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
satisfying the following equality in the contextual category
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
:

[\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})]=[\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}]\times_{[\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}]}[\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}].

Together with
\textbf{X}\otimes\emptyset=\emptyset=\emptyset\otimes\textbf{Y}
, where
\emptyset
denotes the empty context, this implies that
\ell(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})=\ell(\textbf{X})\ell(\textbf{Y})
, and that if
\textbf{X}^{\prime}
(resp.
\textbf{Y}^{\prime}
) is an extension of
X
(resp.
Y
), then
\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}
is an extension of
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
up to variable reordering.

This is achieved by introducing for sort symbols
S
,
T
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
a sort symbol
ST
in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
; the context of its axiom is defined via a recursive algorithm described in §
2
.

On the other hand, operations in
\mathbb{A}
and in
\mathbb{B}
induce operations or equalities in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
similarly to how operations and equalities are introduced in the tensor product of two Lawvere theories. Suppose given an operation in
\mathbb{A}
, say encoded by a judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
where
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m})
. Letting
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x:U)
and
\textbf{u}=(x_{1},...,x_{m},u)
, we have a section
[\textbf{u}]:[\textbf{X}]\rightarrow[\textbf{X}^{\prime}]
of the display map
[\textbf{X}^{\prime}]\twoheadrightarrow[\textbf{X}]
. For a context
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
in
\mathbb{B}
, we will have in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
a section-retraction pair

[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}]\overset{[\textbf{u}\otimes\textbf{Y}]}{\longrightarrow}[\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}]\twoheadrightarrow[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}].

The sequence
\textbf{u}\otimes\textbf{Y}
inducing the left arrow contains, in particular, terms
u\otimes y_{1}
, …,
u\otimes y_{n}
(see §
2.1
) that can be thought of as versions of
u
whose sorts are distinct but depend coherently on the
Y_{i}
. This is simplified if
\mathbb{B}
is a Lawvere theory:
Y_{1}=\cdots=Y_{n}
implies that
u\otimes y_{1}
, …,
u\otimes y_{n}
only differ from each other by a change of variables, so they can be viewed as distinct instances of the same term.

An symmetric story can be told regarding how we combine contexts in
\mathbb{A}
and operations in
\mathbb{B}
.

To achieve this, we introduce (i) for each term symbol
s
in
\mathbb{A}
and each sort symbol
T
in
\mathbb{B}
, a term symbol
sT
in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
, and (ii) for each sort symbol
S
in
\mathbb{A}
and each term symbol
t
in
\mathbb{B}
, a term symbol
St
in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
. We explain in §
2
how, by combining these data, we obtain enough term expressions to implement the ideas outlined above.

Again inspired by the tensor product of Lawvere theories, we introduce a term equality axiom for each pair
(s,t)
consisting of term symbols
s
,
t
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, respectively. In fact, in §
2
we describe how to combine a pair of derivable term judgments, one in
\mathbb{A}
and one in
\mathbb{B}
, into a term equality judgment in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
which, by Theorem
5.2
, is derivable. This corresponds (see Lemma
6.1
) to the statement that if
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{X}^{\prime}
,
\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}^{\prime}
are context morphisms in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, then the following diagram in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}].}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{g}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{g}]}

We also introduce in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
equality judgments corresponding to
some
pairs
(J,J^{\prime})
where
J
(resp.
J^{\prime}
) is an axiom in
\mathbb{A}
(resp.
\mathbb{B}
) and either
J
or
J^{\prime}
is an equality judgment.
3
3

3

In certain cases, for example when
J
is a term axiom and
J^{\prime}
is a term equality axiom, we view the result of combining
J
and
J^{\prime}
as being degenerate. This may be related to the fact that, when dealing with gats with no additional structure, sorts in a given context and terms of a given sort form a set (by taking the quotient by the judgmental equality relation) rather than a higher-categorical structure. It could be worth exploring how to improve the construction by working with an intensional notation of equality.
This is summarized in Table
1
.

Categorical aspects

We will verify in a sequel text (
[
4
]
) that this operation defines a closed monoidal structure on
GAT
, which in turn transfers to
Cont
; in fact, writing
Cont
for the strict
2
-category obtained by endowing
Cont
with the natural transformations between contextual functors as
2
-cells, this operation will turn
Cont
into a closed monoidal
Cat
-enriched category.

While the purely syntactic treatment in the present article provides an algorithmic description of a tensor product of gats
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
, it has important limitations. It did not allow us, for example, to describe a universal property of the tensor product, to conclude that the tensor product defines a functor
\otimes:\text{GAT}\times\text{GAT}\rightarrow\text{GAT}
, or to present the exponential theories
\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{A}}
. These gaps will be filled in
[
4
]
, where we work almost entirely within the framework of contextual categories. Nonetheless, in §
6
we construct a comparison functor

\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})

which will play a crucial role in the follow-up text – it will be, in a sense to be defined, a universal
double map
of contextual categories.

Denoting by
Fam
the contextual category of families of sets described in
[
11
]
, the aforementioned closed monoidal structure leads (up to the fact that
Fam
is not small, which requires us to work with contextual categories internal to a larger universe) to isomorphisms of categories

\textbf{Hom}_{\textbf{Cont}}(\mathcal{A},\text{Fam}^{\mathcal{B}})\simeq\textbf{Hom}_{\textbf{Cont}}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\text{Fam})\simeq\textbf{Hom}_{\textbf{Cont}}(\mathcal{B},\text{Fam}^{\mathcal{A}}).

for
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}\in\text{Cont}
. The contextual category
\text{Fam}^{\mathcal{A}}
encodes, in particular, the category of models
\textbf{Hom}_{\textbf{Cont}}(\mathcal{A},\text{Fam})
as its full subcategory spanned by the length-
1
objects. A more detailed study of its features, however, is left for future work.
4
4

4

It is natural to ask whether we have a chain of equivalences
\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A},\text{Mod}(\mathcal{B},\text{Set}))\simeq\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\text{Set})\simeq\text{Mod}(\mathcal{B},\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A},\text{Set}))
where
\text{Mod}(-,-)
denotes the category of morphisms between two display map categories (for a contextual category, we take the underlying display map category, and for a finitely complete category, such as
\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A},\text{Set})
, we let all morphisms be display maps). This question, which will be studied in a future article, is subtle and cannot be answered with our current tools. The key point is that it is not true in general that
\textbf{Hom}_{\textbf{Cont}}(\mathcal{A},\text{Fam})\simeq\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A},\text{Set})
; see
[
23
]
, remarks 2.17 and B.54. For this equivalence to hold, we expect it to be sufficient that
\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})
for some
\mathbb{A}
that does not have sort equality axioms.

We expect that under appropriate assumptions on the factors
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
(see footnote 4 above), our construction will correctly encode the tensor product of locally finitely presentable categories in the sense that
\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\simeq\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A})\otimes\text{Mod}(\mathcal{B})
(where we write
\text{Mod}(-)
for the category of
Set
-models of the underlying display map category). But more information is retained:
\mathcal{A}
(and similarly for
\mathcal{B}
) endows
\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A})
with a distinguished class of arrows, namely, the essential image under the Yoneda embedding
\mathcal{A}^{op}\rightarrow\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A})
of the class of length-
1
display maps. These special maps of models can be regarded as “basic cofibrations” that allow us to view
\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A})
as possessing a sort of cellular structure – for instance, by taking the weak factorization system generated by those maps via the small object argument (in particular, if
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})
, contexts in
\mathbb{A}
correspond to cellular objects with respect to the basic cofibrations). This point of view is adopted, for instance, in
[
18
,
14
,
23
]
.

To relate this extra structure to the tensor product of gats, we borrow a construction from the theory of combinatorial model categories: for two pairs
(M_{1},L_{1})
,
(M_{2},L_{2})
where
M_{i}
is a locally presentable category and
L_{i}
is the left class of a cofibrantly generated weak factorization system on
M_{i}
, we have a pair
(M_{1}\otimes M_{2},L_{1}\otimes L_{2})
such that the canonical functor
M_{1}\times M_{2}\rightarrow M_{1}\otimes M_{2}
is (the “cofibration part” of) a Quillen bifunctor from
((M_{1},L_{1}),(M_{2},L_{2}))
to
(M_{1}\otimes M_{2},L_{1}\otimes L_{2})
, and is initial among Quillen bifunctors out of
((M_{1},L_{1}),(M_{2},L_{2}))
. The class
L_{1}\otimes L_{2}
is generated by the pushout-products
i\hat{\otimes}j
where
i
(resp.
j
) is a generator of
L_{1}
(resp. of
L_{2}
); see
[
24
]
,
[
5
]
. This is analogous to (and motivates) how the sort axioms of
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
are constructed from those of
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, as outlined in the above discussion of the Leibniz formula: semantically, i.e. by viewing derivable sort judgments as cofibrations of models, the sort axioms in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
correspond precisely to the pushout-products of sort axioms in
\mathbb{A}
with ones in
\mathbb{B}
. Hence as long as
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
are such that
\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})\simeq\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A})\otimes\text{Mod}(\mathcal{B})
, we also have
(\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}),L_{\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}})\simeq(\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A}),L_{\mathcal{A}})\otimes(\text{Mod}(\mathcal{B}),L_{\mathcal{B}})
where
L_{-}
is the weak factorization system generated by the sort axioms.

A brief overview of the construction

For gats
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, the tensor product
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is constructed, in summary, as follows:

(1)

Firstly, we use the alphabets
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})
and
\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
to produce an alphabet
\Sigma^{\prime}
. A sort symbol in
\Sigma^{\prime}
will be a pair
(S,T)
consisting of a sort symbol of
\mathbb{A}
and one of
\mathbb{B}
. A term symbol in
\Sigma^{\prime}
will be either
(s,T)
where
s
is a term symbol in
\mathbb{A}
and
T
is a sort symbol in
\mathbb{B}
, or
(S,t)
where
S
is a sort symbol in
\mathbb{A}
and
t
is a term symbol in
\mathbb{B}
.

(2)

We provide an algorithm which, from derivable judgments
J
and
J^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, respectively, constructs a judgment
J\odot J^{\prime}
in the alphabet
\Sigma^{\prime}
in accordance with Table
1
. The key ingredient is a procedure that produces sort/term expressions in
\Sigma^{\prime}
by combining sort/term expressions in
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})
with ones in
\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
.

(3)

We define a pretheory (in the sense of
[
11
]
) structure on
\Sigma^{\prime}
, denoted by
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
, by putting as axioms all tensor products between an axiom in
\mathbb{A}
and one in
\mathbb{B}
.

(4)

We prove that
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is a theory by checking that every axiom is well-formed.

The main source of difficulty is the recursive nature of the definition of a generalized algebraic theory, which reflects both on the construction of
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
as a pretheory (essentially, on checking that our algorithm terminates for any given input expressions or judgments) and on the proof that it is a theory. While dealing with that, we are led to try to perform the required constructions and proofs by induction on the derivations of judgments, or perhaps on some numerical parameter encoding the complexity of judgments. We choose the second option for a technical reason which, I believe, is unessential: it allows us to only specify derivations (or steps thereof) when it is essential to do so.

A natural measure of the complexity of a (derivable) judgment
J
is its
height
: recursively, it is the smallest integer
n
such that
J
can be derived from a list of judgments of height at most
n-1
by employing one of the inference rules. It is not, however, intrinsic to the judgment: there are multiple possible collections of inference rules for a generalized algebraic theory (i.e. that lead to the same derivable judgments as in
[
11
]
), and different such collections induce different “height functions”. On the other hand, our proofs require the height function to have several properties that, in a way, state that the height of a judgment or expression is compatible with its complexity when measured as a mere string or tree of symbols. For example, we would like the height of a context
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}
be to be strictly larger than that of
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n-1}:X_{n-1}
; and the height of a derivable judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash T(t_{1},...,t_{n})\;\mathsf{sort}
to be strictly larger, for
1\leq i\leq n
, than that of some judgment of the form
\textbf{X}\vdash t_{i}:U
. It turns out that not all of the desired conditions hold for the inference rules in
[
11
]
, so we use a suitable modification of the latter. The rules that we use are presented in the appendix along with a proof that they induce the same derivable judgments as in Cartmell’s approach.

Deferred to
[
4
]
, as already mentioned, are descriptions of the functoriality of the tensor product and of a (
1
-categorical) universal property of the functors
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
. Still, in Remark
6.7
we state a condition on the
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
, which will later follow from their universal property, that yields functoriality of the tensor product.

Still, we outline a construction, for theories
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
,
\mathbb{C}
, of an isomorphism
(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})\otimes\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{A}\otimes(\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{C})
. In fact, it can be thought of as an “equality up to change of notation”: we have a canonical bijection between the alphabets
(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})\otimes\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{A}\otimes(\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{C})
, and, after identifying the two alphabets under this bijection, the two theories have the same derivable judgments. We also describe, in a similar way, an isomorphism
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A}
.

Organization of the text

The text is structured as follows:

•

In Section 2, we construct the tensor product
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
of two generalized algebraic theories
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
. We start by presenting an algorithm for combining sort/term expressions from
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
into expressions written in a suitable “tensor product of alphabets”
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
. Then we extend it to an algorithm which, from derivable judgments
J
,
J^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, resp., produces a judgment
J\odot J^{\prime}
in
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
. We let
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
be the pretheory whose signature is
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
and whose axioms are the judgments
J\odot J^{\prime}
where
J
is an axiom in
\mathbb{A}
and
J^{\prime}
is one in
\mathbb{B}
.

•

In Section 3, before proving that
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is a theory, we present several examples of our algorithm. We believe that this section can be quite useful for having a practical understanding of the construction and for recognizing some instances already present in the literature.

•

Sections 4 and 5 are dedicated to proving that
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is a theory. In order to prove that
J\odot J^{\prime}
is well-formed whenever
J
,
J^{\prime}
are axioms in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, resp., we are led to verify the more general statement that
J\odot J^{\prime}
is derivable for any derivable judgments
J
,
J^{\prime}
. We prove by induction on
n\geq 0
that this is the case whenever
\textsf{ht}(J)\textsf{ht}(J^{\prime})=n
, where
ht
is the height function described in the appendix.

As an auxiliary definition, we say that the pair of theories
(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
is
h
-derivable
, where
h\geq 0
, if
J\odot J^{\prime}
is derivable whenever
\textsf{ht}(J)\textsf{ht}(J^{\prime})\leq h
. In Section 4, we establish several consequences of
h
-derivability, which we think of as placing us halfway between
h
-derivability and
(h+1)
-derivability. As we will see in §
6
, tensoring judgments, contexts, or context morphisms (or certain combinations of these) admits a natural interpretation in the contextual category
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
by means of a canonical functor
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
. The role of Section 4 is, informally, to give us tools to reason categorically in a fragment of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
before the latter has been constructed in its totality.

In Section 5, we use the results from the previous section to prove that
(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
is
(h+1)
-derivable whenever it is
h
-derivable. This is done in several cases and requires that we take into account both the kind of judgment and what we call an
initial inference
of
J
,
J^{\prime}
, meaning that the premises have height strictly smaller than that of the conclusion.

•

In Section 6, we verify a certain “two-sided” substitution property of the tensor product
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
(propositions
6.3
and
6.4
), and as a consequence we obtain a comparison functor
\otimes_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
.

•

In Sections 7 and 8 we sketch a description of isomorphisms of the forms
(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})\otimes\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{A}\otimes(\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{C})
and
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A}
, respectively.

•

In the appendix we give an introduction to generalized algebraic theories. Our main goal here is to discuss the particular set of inference rules used to obtain a well-behaved height function on judgments. Some technical work is required to verify the desired properties of the height function and compare our inference rules with Cartmell’s. We also fix some notation and terminology regarding gats that might differ from the usual ones. Readers who are familiar with the topic may consult the appendix as needed.

2
The tensor product of generalized algebraic theories

In this section we describe an algorithm that constructs what we will call the
tensor product
of two generalized algebraic theories
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, denoted by
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
. As explained in the introduction, our approach in the present text will be purely syntactic. While an abstract presentation in terms of contextual categories can be given (as will be discussed in the sequel article), the syntactic approach is suitable for explicit calculations and encodes certain data about tensor products that otherwise would not be readily available. This reflects the fact that one may regard generalized algebraic theories as
presentations
of contextual categories. Thus here, as often happens with monoidal categories of algebraic structures, if
X
,
Y
are two objects with given presentations by generators and relations, we would like to obtain a presentation for
X\otimes Y
by explicitly combining those for
X
and
Y
. For gats
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, the tensor product
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
will be a particular presentation of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})\cong\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
constructed directly from the axioms of
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
.

We will define
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
in several steps. Firstly, for sort-and-term alphabets (see Definition
A.1
)
\Sigma
and
\mathrm{T}
, we define an alphabet
\Sigma\otimes\mathrm{T}
. Then, specializing to the alphabets
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})
,
\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
of
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, we describe how to combine derivable sorts/terms in
\mathbb{A}
with ones in
\mathbb{B}
to obtain sort/term expressions in
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
. This is used, in particular, to define a length-
mn
precontext
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
in
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
where
X
(resp.
Y
) is a length-
m
(resp.
n
) context in
\mathbb{A}
(resp.
\mathbb{B}
).

Next, we define for certain (see Table
1
) derivable judgments
J
,
J^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, resp., a judgment
J\odot J^{\prime}
in
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
. Now, we let
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
be the pretheory (in the sense of
[
11
]
) structure on
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
whose axioms are
J\odot J^{\prime}
(when it is defined) where
J
,
J^{\prime}
are axioms in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, resp. Most of the technical work in the remainder of the article will be devoted to proving:

Theorem
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
be generalized algebraic theories. For any derivable judgments
J
,
J^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, resp., if the judgment
J\odot J^{\prime}
is defined, then it is derivable in the pretheory
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
. In particular,
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is a theory.

While reading this section, the reader is invited to compare each step of the construction with the examples in §
3
, especially the theory of strict double categories (
3.1
) as it exhibits many of the features present in the general definition.

2.1
Expressions from pairs of judgments

We refer the reader to the appendix for the definitions of the syntactic structures that we use below – sort-and-term alphabets, sort/term expressions, the height function, the canonical sort of a term, etc.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\Sigma
and
\mathrm{T}
be sort-and-term alphabets. We define the alphabet
\Sigma\otimes\mathrm{T}
as having the following sets of variables
5
5

5

We admit the possibility of different gats having different (countably infinite) sets of variables; see the Appendix for the definition we will use, and Remark
2.2
for motivation for this convention.
, sort symbols, and term symbols:

\displaystyle(\Sigma\otimes\mathrm{T})^{var}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{var}\times\mathrm{T}^{var}

\displaystyle(\Sigma\otimes\mathrm{T})^{sort}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{sort}\times\mathrm{T}^{sort}

\displaystyle(\Sigma\otimes\mathrm{T})^{term}

\displaystyle=(\Sigma^{sort}\times\mathrm{T}^{term})\sqcup(\Sigma^{term}\times\mathrm{T}^{sort}).

In each of these sets, we will write
ab
for an ordered pair
(a,b)
(or, by abuse of notation, for the image of a pair
(a,b)
under the canonical maps from
\Sigma^{sort}\times\mathrm{T}^{term}
and
\Sigma^{term}\times\mathrm{T}^{sort}
to the above coproduct).

Remark 2.2
.

While the forms of
(\Sigma\otimes\mathrm{T})^{sort}
and
(\Sigma\otimes\mathrm{T})^{term}
are essential, that of
(\Sigma\otimes\mathrm{T})^{var}
can be seen as a notational convenience. Indeed, we could replace
\Sigma^{var}\times\mathrm{T}^{var}
by any other countably infinite set, say
V
, but to perform some of the constructions that will appear in this section it is helpful to choose some (arbitrary) bijection
\Sigma^{var}\times\mathrm{T}^{var}\cong K
.

We now construct operations that assign to certain pairs of judgments, one in
\mathbb{A}
and one in
\mathbb{B}
, a “tensor” expression in
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
.

We let
Der_{s}(\mathbb{A})
and
Der_{t}(\mathbb{A})
, respectively (and similarly for
\mathbb{B}
), be the sets of derivable sort judgments and of derivable term judgments in
\mathbb{A}
; also, we denote by
Der^{+}_{s}(\mathbb{A})
the set of pairs
(J,x)
where
J\in Der_{s}(\mathbb{A})
and
x\in\Sigma(\mathbb{A})^{var}
does not occur in the context of
J
. Note that the elements of
Der^{+}_{s}(\mathbb{A})
, which we think of as “augmented” sort judgments, are in canonical bijection with the nonempty contexts of
\mathbb{A}
.
6
6

6

Although we could directly use the nonempty contexts of
\mathbb{A}
, this alternative form will be useful for the particular use we will make of such structures. In fact, Notation
2.4
suggests us to think of
(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort},x)
as a structure on the variable
x
itself, so that
x
functions informally as a placeholder for a “generic” element of sort
U
in context
X
(rather than, for example, the collection of all elements of that sort).

The sets of sort expressions and of term expressions of
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})
(and similarly for
\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
) will be denoted by
Exp_{s}(\Sigma(\mathbb{A}))
and
Exp_{t}(\Sigma(\mathbb{A}))
, respectively.

Firstly, we will define maps

\otimes_{t}:(Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{A}))\times(Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{B})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{B}))\longrightarrow Exp_{t}(\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})),

\medblackcircle_{t}:(Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{A}))\times(Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{B})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{B}))\longrightarrow Exp_{t}(\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B}))

recursively with respect to the product of the heights of the arguments (see Definition
A.10
).

Remark 2.3
.

This implies (using the notation introduced below) that we will combine derivable terms
u
,
v
of sorts
U
,
V
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, respectively, in two different ways: as
u\otimes_{t}v
and as
u\medblackcircle_{t}v
. They correspond, as reflected in the formulas
\lozenge
,
\blacklozenge
below and Remark
2.7
, that we would like to have two distinct ways of making the operations
u
,
v
act on a matrix of variables: one where we apply
u
to each column and then
v
to the resulting list, and one where we apply
v
to each row and then
u
to the resulting list.

These expressions generalize to the dependently sorted setting the left- and right-hand sides of the equality axiom, discussed in the introduction, stating the commutativity of two operations
f
,
g
in the tensor product of two Lawvere theories. (See also §
3.2
.) Accordingly, the equality
u\otimes_{t}v\equiv u\medblackcircle_{t}v
will be derivable in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
; in fact, it will be an axiom if the term judgments attached to
u
,
v
are axioms.

This is a good point to introduce some conventions and abuses of notation that will be convenient throughout the text:

Notation 2.4
.

(1)

Let
J
be a derivable term judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
in
\mathbb{A}
. Whenever it is used in one of the operations
\otimes_{t}
,
\medblackcircle_{t}
below, we will denote it by
u^{U}
, with the context
X
implicit. Similarly, if
J
is a derivable judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
in
\mathbb{A}
, we will denote a pair
(J,x)\in Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})
by
x^{U}
.

Note that, due to the omission of the context,
x^{U}
– where
x
is a variable – can represent a term judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash x:U
or a sort judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
. This ambiguity only vanishes when the set of variables in the context is taken into account: if
x
occurs in the context, we have a term judgment; if not, we have a sort judgment (or, more precisely, an element of
Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})
).

(2)

When dealing with a judgment of the form
\textbf{X}\vdash u:\mathcal{S}(u)
(see
A.3
), we write
u
instead of
u^{\mathcal{S}(u)}
. This will occur most notably in the following two cases:

•

For a derivable judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash S(s_{1},...,s_{k})\;\mathsf{sort}
, where
S
is a sort symbol and
s_{1}
, …,
s_{k}
are sort expressions, for
1\leq i\leq k
we will – when performing the operations
\otimes_{t}
and
\medblackcircle_{t}
– use
s_{i}
as a notation for the (derivable) judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash s_{i}:\mathcal{S}(s_{i})
.

•

For a derivable judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash s(s_{1},...,s_{k}):U
, where
s
is a term symbol and
s_{1}
, …,
s_{k}
are sort expressions, we denote
\textbf{X}\vdash s_{i}:\mathcal{S}(s_{i})
by
s_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
(again, only when dealing with
\otimes_{t}
and
\medblackcircle_{t}
).

(3)

When using the above notation for sort or term judgments, we will usually write
u^{U}\otimes v^{V}
(resp.
u^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V}
) for
u^{U}\otimes_{t}v^{V}
(resp. for
u^{U}\medblackcircle_{t}v^{V}
).

(4)

Suppose given finite sequences
(u_{1}^{U_{1}},...,u_{m}^{U_{m}})
in
Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{A})
and
(v_{1}^{V_{1}},...,v_{n}^{V_{n}})
in
Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{B})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{B})
. We will denote by

(u_{1}^{U_{1}},...,u_{m}^{U_{m}})\otimes(v_{1}^{V_{1}},...,v_{n}^{V_{n}})

the length-
mn
sequence
(u_{1}^{U_{1}}\otimes v_{1}^{V_{1}},...,u_{1}^{U_{1}}\otimes v_{n}^{V_{n}},...,u_{m}^{U_{m}}\otimes v_{1}^{V_{1}},...,u_{m}^{U_{m}}\otimes v_{n}^{V_{n}})
. Precisely, it is the family
\{1,...,mn\}\rightarrow Exp_{t}(\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B}))
obtained by composing
(u_{i}^{U_{i}}\otimes v_{j}^{V_{j}})_{(i,j)\in\{1,...,m\}\times\{1,...,n\}}
with the bijection
\{1,...,mn\}\cong\{1,...,m\}\times\{1,...,n\}
corresponding to the lexicographic order on the cartesian product. We will also use the matrix notation

\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}^{U_{1}}\otimes v_{1}^{V_{1}}&\cdots&u_{1}^{U_{1}}\otimes v_{n}^{V_{n}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
u_{m}^{U_{m}}\otimes v_{1}^{V_{1}}&\cdots&u_{m}^{U_{m}}\otimes v_{n}^{V_{n}}\end{pmatrix}.

(Although the matrix represents
(u_{i}^{U_{i}}\otimes v_{j}^{V_{j}})_{(i,j)\in\{1,...,m\}\times\{1,...,n\}}
, we use it as a notation for the reindexed family
\{1,...,mn\}\rightarrow Exp_{t}(\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B}))
.)

(5)

For
(J,x)
in
Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})
or
Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{B})
, we let
\textsf{ht}(J,x)=\textsf{ht}(J)
.

2.1.1
Construction of tensor term expressions

Given
h\geq 0
, suppose that
J\otimes_{t}J^{\prime}
has been constructed whenever
J\in Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{A})
and
J^{\prime}\in Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{B})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{B})
satisfy
\textsf{ht}(J)\textsf{ht}(J^{\prime})<h
. Then, if
\textsf{ht}(J)\textsf{ht}(J^{\prime})=h
, we define
J\otimes_{t}J^{\prime}
as follows:

(i)

In the notation described above, suppose that
J
is
x^{U}
and
J^{\prime}
is
y^{U}
where
x
,
y
are variables. Then we let
J\otimes_{t}J^{\prime}
(or
x^{U}\otimes y^{V}
) be
xy
.

More precisely, we assume that either
J
is
(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort},x)
where
x
is a variable not contained in
X
, or it is
\textbf{X}\vdash x:U
. Similarly, either
J^{\prime}
is
(\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort},y)
, where
y
is a variable not in
Y
, or it is
\textbf{Y}\vdash y:V
.

(ii)

Suppose that
J
is either
(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort},x)
or
\textbf{X}\vdash x:U
, where
x
is a variable, and
J^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V)
where
v
is not a variable. Write
U=S(s_{1},...,s_{k})
, where
S
is a sort symbol and
s_{1}
, …,
s_{k}
are term expressions, and
v=t(t_{1},...,t_{\ell})
, where
t
is a term symbol and
t_{1}
, …,
t_{k}
are term expressions. We let
J\otimes_{t}J^{\prime}
(or
x^{U}\otimes v^{V}
) be

St((s_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(s_{1})},...,s_{k}^{\mathcal{S}(s_{k})},x^{U})\otimes(t_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(t_{1})},...,t_{\ell}^{\mathcal{S}(t_{\ell})})).

Following the above conventions, we write it as
St((s_{1},...,s_{k},x^{U})\otimes(t_{1},...,t_{\ell}))
or, in matrix notation,

St\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&s_{1}\otimes t_{\ell}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
s_{k}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&s_{k}\otimes t_{\ell}\\
x^{U}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&x^{U}\otimes t_{\ell}\end{pmatrix}.

Note that this only requires the use of products already constructed in the recursion process. Indeed, by Proposition
A.15
we have

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash s_{i}:\mathcal{S}(s_{i}))<\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}),\qquad\qquad\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash t_{j}:\mathcal{S}(t_{j}))<\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V)

for
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq l
.

(iii)

Suppose that
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)
, where
u
is not a variable, and
J^{\prime}
is either
(\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort},y)
or
\textbf{Y}\vdash y:V
where
y
is a variable. Writing
u=s(s_{1},...,s_{k})
and
V=T(t_{1},...,t_{\ell})
. If
y
, we let
J\otimes_{t}J^{\prime}
(or
u^{U}\otimes y^{V}
) be

sT((s_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(s_{1})},...,s_{k}^{\mathcal{S}(s_{k})})\otimes(t_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(t_{1})},...,t_{\ell}^{\mathcal{S}(t_{\ell})},y^{V})).

As in the previous case, we write it as
sT((s_{1},...,s_{k})\otimes(t_{1},...,t_{\ell},y^{V}))
or, in matrix notation,

sT\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&s_{1}\otimes t_{\ell}&s_{1}\otimes y^{V}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
s_{k}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&s_{k}\otimes t_{\ell}&s_{k}\otimes y^{V}\end{pmatrix}.

Since, by Proposition
A.15
,

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash s_{i}:\mathcal{S}(s_{i}))<\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U),\qquad\qquad\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash t_{j}:\mathcal{S}(t_{j}))<\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}),

this only requires products already constructed in the recursion process.

(iv)

Suppose that
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)
and
J^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V)
where
u
,
v
are not variables. If
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
and
x
is any variable not in
X
, we let
J\otimes_{t}J^{\prime}
(or
u^{U}\otimes v^{V}
) be

(x^{U}\otimes v^{V})[u^{U}\otimes y_{1}^{Y_{1}}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes y_{n}^{Y_{n}}\mid xy_{n}]

where
y_{i}^{Y_{i}}
denotes the pair
(\partial_{i-1}\textbf{Y}\vdash Y_{i}\;\mathsf{sort},y_{i})\in Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{B})
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Recall that (
\lozenge
) is the expression obtained from
x^{U}\otimes v^{V}
by simultaneously replacing every occurrence of
xy_{1}
, …,
xy_{n}
by
u^{U}\otimes y_{1}^{Y_{1}}
, …,
u^{U}\otimes y_{n}^{Y_{n}}
, respectively. It can be checked by induction that (
\lozenge
) does not depend on the choice of
x
.

By Proposition
A.15
we have

\displaystyle\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort},x)=\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})<\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U),

\displaystyle\textsf{ht}(\partial_{i-1}\textbf{Y}\vdash Y_{i}\;\mathsf{sort},y_{i})=\textsf{ht}(\partial_{i}\textbf{Y})\leq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})<\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V),

so only products that have already been constructed are required for (
\lozenge
).

This concludes the definition of
\otimes_{t}
. On the other hand,
\medblackcircle_{t}
is defined as follows:

(i)

Suppose that
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)
and
J^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V)
where
u
,
v
are not variables. If
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m})
and
y
is any variable not in
Y
, we let
J\medblackcircle_{t}J^{\prime}
(or
u^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V}
) be

(u^{U}\otimes y^{V})[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{m}y]

where
x_{i}^{X_{i}}
denotes
(\partial_{i-1}\textbf{X}\vdash X_{i},x_{i})\in Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})
for
1\leq i\leq m
. It can be verified by induction that (
\blacklozenge
) does not depend on the choice of
y
.

(ii)

In all other cases, i.e. if
J
is of the form
x^{U}
for a variable
x
or
J^{\prime}
is of the form
y^{V}
for a variable
y
, we let
J\medblackcircle_{t}J^{\prime}=J\otimes_{t}J^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.5
.

Although (
\lozenge
) is only used to define
\otimes_{t}
on pairs of terms that are not variables, it actually computes
\otimes_{t}
in greater (but not full) generality. This will be convenient later when calculating tensor products of judgments. To study when (
\lozenge
) correctly computes
\otimes_{t}
, consider

J=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U),\text{ where }\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m});\qquad J^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V),\text{ where }\textbf{X}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n}).

Firstly, suppose that
u=x_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,...,m\}
(whereas
v
can be a variable or not). Then (
\lozenge
) becomes

(x^{U}\otimes v^{V})[x_{i}y_{1}\mid xy_{1},...,x_{i}y_{n}\mid xy_{n}]=x_{i}^{U}\otimes v^{V}.

On the other hand, if
v=y_{j}
for some
j\in\{1,...,n\}
, then (
\lozenge
) is

(xy_{j})[u^{U}\otimes y_{1}^{Y_{1}}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes y_{n}\mid xy_{n}]=u^{U}\otimes y_{j}^{Y_{j}},

which is not equal in general to
u^{U}\otimes y_{j}^{V}
; it is when
U=Y_{j}
.

Similarly, (
\blacklozenge
) computes
\medblackcircle_{t}
in some cases other than those where it does by definition. Let
J
and
J^{\prime}
be as above. If
v=y_{j}
for some
j\in\{1,...,n\}
, then (
\blacklozenge
) equals

(u^{U}\otimes y^{V})[x_{1}y_{j}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}y_{j}\mid x_{m}y]=u^{U}\otimes y_{j}^{V}=u^{U}\medblackcircle y_{j}^{V}.

If
u=x_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,...,m\}
, then (
\blacklozenge
) is

x_{i}y[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{m}y]=x_{i}^{X_{i}}\otimes v^{V}=x_{i}^{X_{i}}\medblackcircle v^{V},

which equals
x_{i}^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V}
if
U=X_{i}
.

For future reference, we state as a lemma the case we will be interested in:

Lemma 2.6
.

Formula (
\lozenge
) applied to
u^{U}\in Der_{t}(\mathbb{A})
and
v^{V}\in Der_{t}(\mathbb{B})
calculates
u^{U}\otimes_{t}v^{V}
when
V=\mathcal{S}(v)
, and formula (
\blacklozenge
) applied to the same judgments calculates
u^{U}\medblackcircle_{t}v^{V}
when
U=\mathcal{S}(u)
.

Remark 2.7
.

Using the matrix form can be quite useful for working with formulas (
\lozenge
) and (
\blacklozenge
). Let
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)
and
J^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V)
where
u
,
v
are not variables. Write
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m})
and
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
, and let
x
,
y
be the chosen variables not in
X
,
Y
, respectively.

If
U=S(\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{K})
and
v=t(t_{1},...,t_{\ell})
, then
u^{U}\otimes v^{V}
can be written as

St\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{\ell}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\sigma_{K}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{K}\otimes t_{\ell}\\
x^{U}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&x^{U}\otimes t_{\ell}\end{pmatrix}[u^{U}\otimes y_{1}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes y_{n}\mid xy_{n}].

As
xy_{1}
, …,
xy_{n}
do not occur in the expressions
\sigma_{i}\otimes t_{j}
, by Lemma
2.6
the above expression equals

St\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{\ell}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\sigma_{K}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{K}\otimes t_{\ell}\\
\overline{x^{U}\otimes t_{1}}&\cdots&\overline{x^{U}\otimes t_{\ell}}\end{pmatrix}=St\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{\ell}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\sigma_{K}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{K}\otimes t_{\ell}\\
u^{U}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&u^{U}\otimes t_{\ell}\end{pmatrix}

where we use an upper bar to indicate an application of
[u^{U}\otimes y_{1}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes y_{n}\mid xy_{n}]
.

On the other hand, if
u=s(s_{1},...,s_{k})
and
V=T(\tau_{1},...,\tau_{\ell})
, then
u^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V}
can be written as

sT\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&s_{1}\otimes\tau_{\ell}&s_{1}\otimes y^{V}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
s_{k}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&s_{k}\otimes\tau_{\ell}&s_{k}\otimes y^{V}\end{pmatrix}[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{m}y].

As
x_{1}y
, …,
x_{m}y
do not occur in the expressions
s_{i}\otimes\tau_{j}
, by Lemma
2.6
the above expression equals

sT\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&s_{1}\otimes\tau_{\ell}&\overline{s_{1}\otimes y^{V}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
s_{k}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&s_{k}\otimes\tau_{\ell}&\overline{s_{k}\otimes y^{V}}\end{pmatrix}=sT\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&s_{1}\otimes\tau_{\ell}&s_{1}\medblackcircle v^{V}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
s_{k}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&s_{k}\otimes\tau_{\ell}&s_{k}\medblackcircle v^{V}\end{pmatrix}

where the upper bar now indicates
[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{m}y]
.

2.1.2
Construction of tensor sort expressions

Now, we use
\otimes_{t}
to define an operation

\otimes_{s}:Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})\otimes Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{B})\longrightarrow Exp_{s}(\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B}))

that combines two derivable (augmented) sort judgments to produce a sort expression in the alphabet
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
.

Notation 2.8
.

For a finite sequence
\alpha=(a_{1},...,a_{n})
in a given set, we let
\partial\alpha
be its restriction
(a_{1},...,a_{n-1})
if
n\geq 1
, and the empty sequence if
n=0
(i.e. if
\alpha
is empty).

Given
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort},x)\in Der_{s}(\mathbb{A})
and
J^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort},y)\in Der_{s}(\mathbb{B})
, where

U=S(s_{1},...,s_{k}),\qquad\qquad V=T(t_{1},...,t_{\ell}),

we define
J\otimes_{s}J^{\prime}
, also denoted by
U^{x}\otimes_{s}V^{y}
with
X
,
Y
implicit, as the expression

ST(\partial((s_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(s_{1})},...,s_{k}^{\mathcal{S}(s_{k})},x^{U})\otimes_{t}(t_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(t_{1})},...,t_{\ell}^{\mathcal{S}(t_{\ell})},y^{V}))).

We will usually drop the subscript “
s
” from
\otimes_{s}
and, when this causes no ambiguity, also the superscript variables from
U^{x}
and
V^{y}
.
7
7

7

Note that
U^{x}
denotes the same structure as
x^{U}
(see Notation
2.4
). However, the former is used exclusively when applying the operations
\otimes_{t}
and
\medblackcircle_{t}
, and the latter only when applying
\otimes_{s}
.

Following our conventions for
\otimes_{t}
we write simply
ST(\partial((s_{1},...,s_{k},x^{U})\otimes(t_{1},...,t_{\ell},y^{V})))
. If we were to spell it out in the usual way, we would obtain something like

ST(s_{1}\otimes t_{1},...,s_{1}\otimes t_{\ell},s_{1}\otimes y^{V},...,s_{k}\otimes t_{1},...,s_{k}\otimes t_{\ell},s_{k}\otimes y^{V},x^{U}\otimes t_{1},...,x^{U}\otimes t_{\ell}),

which can be ambiguous. Instead, we use the “incomplete matrix”

ST\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&s_{1}\otimes t_{\ell}&s_{1}\otimes y^{V}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
s_{k}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&s_{k}\otimes t_{\ell}&s_{k}\otimes y^{V}\\
x^{U}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&x^{U}\otimes t_{\ell}&-\end{pmatrix}

(while keeping in mind that the entries are ordered lexicographically).

2.1.3
Construction of tensor precontexts

We use the above construction to define, for contexts
X
and
Y
in
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, respectively, a “tensor” precontext
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
in
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
. If
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m})
and
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
, we let
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
be the sequence

(x_{1}y_{1}:X_{1}^{x_{1}}\otimes Y_{1}^{y_{1}},...,x_{1}y_{n}:X_{1}^{x_{1}}\otimes Y_{n}^{y_{n}},...,x_{m}y_{1}:X_{m}^{x_{m}}\otimes Y_{1}^{y_{1}},...,x_{m}y_{n}:X_{m}^{x_{m}}\otimes Y_{n}^{y_{n}})

obtained by lexicographically ordering the pairs
(x_{i}y_{j}:X_{i}^{x_{i}}\otimes Y_{j}^{y_{j}})
for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
. Again, it will be convenient to use a matrix form:

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

The reason is that we will often consider precontexts obtained by dropping the last entry of (
*
), i.e.

\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})=(x_{1}y_{1}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{1},...,x_{1}y_{n}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{n},...,x_{m}y_{1}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{1},...,x_{m}y_{n}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{n-1}).

We denote the latter by the “incomplete matrix”

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n-1}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{n-1}&x_{1}y_{n}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
x_{m-1}y_{1}:X_{m-1}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{m-1}y_{n-1}:X_{m-1}\otimes Y_{n-1}&x_{m-1}y_{n}:X_{m-1}\otimes Y_{n}\\
x_{m}y_{1}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n-1}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{n-1}&-\end{pmatrix}.

2.2
The tensor product of judgments

We will now define, for theories
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, an operation that assigns to
some
pairs
(J,J^{\prime})
, where
J
(resp.
J^{\prime}
) is a derivable judgment in
\mathbb{A}
(resp.
\mathbb{B}
), a judgment
J\odot J^{\prime}
in the alphabet
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
. This will be done according to the following table:

Sort

Term

Sort eq.

Term eq.

Sort

Sort

Term

Sort eq.

Term eq.

Term

Term

Term eq.

Term eq.

-

Sort eq.

Term eq.

Table 1:
Rules for the tensor product of judgments

For instance,
J\odot J^{\prime}
will be a term equality judgment when
J
(resp.
J^{\prime}
) is a derivable term judgment in
\mathbb{A}
(resp. a derivable sort equality judgment in
\mathbb{B}
), but it will not be defined when both
J
,
J^{\prime}
are term equality judgments.

In what follows, we assume that every context in
\mathbb{A}
or
\mathbb{B}
is endowed with a variable not contained in it.
8
8

8

While this choice is required for the operations below to be defined, it is not essential in the sense that, once we construct the pretheory
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
and prove that it is a theory, different choices will yield sets of axioms that are equal up to variable renaming. Moreover, we can include such a choice function as part of the definition of a generalized algebraic theory without changing the definition of a theory morphism (hence without modifying the category
GAT
in an essential way).

In all cases, we consider contexts
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m})
and
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
in
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, respectively. Also, we let
x
(resp.
y
) be the chosen variable not contained in
X
(resp.
Y
).

sort
\odot
sort

J
is
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
and
J^{\prime}
is
\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
. Let
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x:U)
and
\textbf{Y}^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y},y:U)
. We define
J\odot J^{\prime}
as

\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})\vdash U\otimes V\;\mathsf{sort}.

More explicitly, writing
U=S(s_{1},...,s_{k})
and
V=T(t_{1},...,t_{\ell})
, we have

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{n}&x_{1}y:X_{1}\otimes V\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{n}&x_{m}y:X_{m}\otimes V\\
xy_{1}:U\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&xy_{n}:U\otimes Y_{n}&-\end{pmatrix}\vdash ST\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&s_{1}\otimes t_{\ell}&s_{1}\otimes y^{V}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
s_{k}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&s_{k}\otimes t_{\ell}&s_{k}\otimes y^{V}\\
x^{U}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&x^{U}\otimes t_{\ell}&-\end{pmatrix}\;\mathsf{sort}.

In particular, if
J
and
J^{\prime}
are axioms with
U=S(x_{1},...,x_{m})
and
V=T(y_{1},...,y_{n})
, then the above judgment is

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{n}&x_{1}y:X_{1}\otimes V\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{n}&x_{m}y:X_{m}\otimes V\\
xy_{1}:U\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&xy_{n}:U\otimes Y_{n}&-\end{pmatrix}\vdash ST\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}&x_{1}y\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n}&x_{m}y\\
xy_{1}&\cdots&xy_{n}&-\end{pmatrix}\;\mathsf{sort}.

sort
\odot
term

J
is
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
and
J^{\prime}
is
\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V
. Let
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x:U)
. We define
J\odot J^{\prime}
as

\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash x^{U}\otimes v^{V}:(U\otimes V)[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{m}y].

To describe it explicitly, write
U=S(\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{K})
and
V=T(\tau_{1},...,\tau_{L})
. Then
U\otimes V
equals

ST\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{1}\otimes\tau_{L}&\sigma_{1}\otimes y^{V}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\sigma_{K}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{K}\otimes\tau_{L}&\sigma_{K}\otimes y^{V}\\
x^{U}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&x^{U}\otimes\tau_{L}&-\end{pmatrix}

and, since
x_{1}y
, …,
x_{m}y
do not occur in the terms
\sigma_{i}\otimes\tau_{j}
and
x^{U}\otimes\tau_{j}
, by Lemma
2.6
we have

(U\otimes V)[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{m}y]=ST\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{1}\otimes\tau_{L}&\sigma_{1}\medblackcircle v^{V}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\sigma_{K}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{K}\otimes\tau_{L}&\sigma_{K}\medblackcircle v^{V}\\
x^{U}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&x^{U}\otimes\tau_{L}&-\end{pmatrix}.

It follows that if
v=t(t_{1},...,t_{\ell})
, then
J\odot J^{\prime}
equals

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{n}\\
xy_{1}:U\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&xy_{n}:U\otimes Y_{n}\end{pmatrix}\vdash St\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{\ell}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\sigma_{K}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{K}\otimes t_{\ell}\\
x^{U}\otimes t_{1}&\cdots&x^{U}\otimes t_{\ell}\end{pmatrix}:ST\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{1}\otimes\tau_{L}&\sigma_{1}\medblackcircle v^{V}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\sigma_{K}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{K}\otimes\tau_{L}&\sigma_{K}\medblackcircle v^{V}\\
x^{U}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&x^{U}\otimes\tau_{L}&-\end{pmatrix}.

Otherwise, if
v
is a variable
y_{j}
, we replace the expression
St(\cdots)
by
xy_{j}
in the above judgment.

Note that if
J
and
J^{\prime}
are axioms, say with
U=S(x_{1},...,x_{m})
and
v=t(y_{1},...,y_{n})
, then the expression
St(\cdots)
becomes

St\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n}\\
xy_{1}&\cdots&xy_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

term
\odot
sort

J
is
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
and

is

as

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}\vdash u^{U}\otimes y^{V}:(U\otimes V)[u^{U}\otimes y_{1}^{Y_{1}}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes y_{n}^{Y_{n}}\mid xy_{n}].

To describe it explicitly, write

and

is as in the previous item and, since

\sigma_{K}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{K}\otimes\tau_{L}&\sigma_{K}\otimes y^{V}\\

It follows that if

equals

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}:X_{1}\otimes Y_{n}&x_{1}y:X_{1}\otimes V\\

u^{U}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&u^{U}\otimes\tau_{L}&-\end{pmatrix}.

If, instead,
u
is a variable

by

in the above judgment.

Note that if

and

term
\odot

J
is
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
and

is

where the double bar indicates an application of

While we cannot give a concise general description of

and

–

If

are equal to each other by definition. If both are variables, say

. If

St\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}\otimes y_{1}&\cdots&\sigma_{1}\otimes y_{n}\\

u^{U}\otimes y_{1}&\cdots&u^{U}\otimes y_{n}\end{pmatrix}\equiv sT\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\otimes\tau_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}\otimes\tau_{L}&x_{1}\otimes v^{V}\\

\end{pmatrix}.

2.3
The tensor product of two theories (as a pretheory)

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
be generalized algebraic theories. Their
tensor product
, denoted by
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
, is the following pretheory (in the sense of
[
11
]
):

•

its alphabet is
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
(see Definition
2.1
);

•

its axioms are the judgments
J\odot J^{\prime}
where
J
is an axiom in
\mathbb{A}
and
J^{\prime}
is an axiom in
\mathbb{B}
.

The correctness of this definition, i.e. each sort/term symbol corresponding to exactly one sort/term axiom and that axiom having the required form, follows from Table
1
and the explicit descriptions of
J\odot J^{\prime}
in the “sort axiom
\odot
sort axiom”, “sort axiom
\odot
term axiom” and “term axiom
\odot
sort axiom” cases.

We will later prove (see Theorem
5.2
) that
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is a theory, meaning that every axiom is
well-formed
in the sense of
[
11
]
. Due to the recursive nature of
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, this is a technically involved task: verifying that an axiom
J\odot J^{\prime}
is well-formed corresponds to checking the derivability of other judgments that are, generally, not axioms. Thus our strategy will be to prove not only the desired statement on the axioms, but also, along the way, that
J\odot J^{\prime}
is derivable for any derivable judgments
J
,
J^{\prime}
. In fact, we will prove by induction on
h\geq 0
that
J\odot J^{\prime}
is derivable whenever
\textsf{ht}(J)\textsf{ht}(J^{\prime})=h
. For that reason, we use the following auxiliary definition:

Definition 2.10
.

Given
h\geq 0
, we say that the pair of generalized algebraic theories
(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
is
h
-derivable
if for any derivable judgments
J
,
J^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, respectively, with
\textsf{ht}(J)\textsf{ht}(J^{\prime})\leq h
, we have that
J\odot J^{\prime}
is derivable.

We will prove, in other words, that any pair of gats
(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
is
h
-derivable for all
h\geq 0
.

3
Examples

In this section we describe a few instances of the tensor product of generalized algebraic theories as in Definition
2.9
.

3.1
An illustrative example: strict double categories

One of the classical examples of a gat is the theory
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
of categories, given by the following axioms
9
9

9

We follow the usual practice of omitting multiple consecutive occurrences of a sort in a context. For example, we write
x,y:O
instead of
x:O,y:O
.
(see
[
11
]
):

\displaystyle\vdash O\;\mathsf{sort}

(sort of objects)

\displaystyle x,y:O

\displaystyle\vdash A(x,y)\;\mathsf{sort}

(sort of arrows from
x
to
y
)

\displaystyle x,y,z:O,f:A(x,y),g:A(y,z)

\displaystyle\vdash\circ(x,y,z,f,g):A(x,z)

(composition)

\displaystyle x:O

\displaystyle\vdash id(x):A(x,x)

(identity arrows)

\displaystyle x,y,z,w:O,f:A(x,y),g:A(y,z),h:A(z,w)

\displaystyle\vdash\circ(x,y,w,f,\circ(y,z,w,g,h))

\displaystyle\equiv\circ(x,z,w,\circ(x,y,z,f,g),h):A(x,w)

(associativity)

\displaystyle x,y:O,f:A(x,y)

\displaystyle\vdash\circ(x,x,y,id(x),f)\equiv f:A(x,y)

(left unitality)

\displaystyle x,y:O,f:A(x,y)

\displaystyle\vdash\circ(x,y,y,f,id(y))\equiv f:A(x,y)

(right unitality)

Let us calculate
\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
, which will turn out to be the theory whose
Set
-models are small strict double categories, i.e. (up to equivalence) categories internal to the
1
-category
Cat
.

To organize the construction, in this case and in others, it can be helpful to start by identifying sub-theories of each of the factors and calculating the tensor product of those. In this case, note that the first two axioms above, which introduce
O
and
A
, define the theory
\mathbb{T}_{graph}
of directed graphs. Hence we will first describe
\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}
, then extend it to two theories
\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}
and
\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
, and, finally, extend
(\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph})\cup(\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat})
to
\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
.

3.1.1
Calculating
\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}

Firstly, tensoring
\vdash O\;\mathsf{sort}
with itself yields

\vdash OO\;\mathsf{sort}.

Now, letting
x
and
k
be the variables associated with the empty context and with
x,y:O
, respectively (recall that we choose for each context a variable not contained in it), by tensoring
\vdash O\;\mathsf{sort}
and
x,y:O\vdash A(x,y)\;\mathsf{sort}
we obtain

xx:OO,xy:OO\vdash OA(xx,xy)\;\mathsf{sort}.

Similarly, from
x,y:O\vdash A(x,y)\;\mathsf{sort}
and
\vdash O\;\mathsf{sort}
we get

xx,yx:OO\vdash AO(xx,yx)\;\mathsf{sort}.

The last axiom of
\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}
is obtained by tensoring
x,y:O\vdash A(x,y)\;\mathsf{sort}
with itself:

\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx:OO&xy:OO&xk:OA(xx,xy)\\
yx:OO&yy:OO&yk:OA(yx,yy)\\
kx:AO(xx,yx)&ky:AO(xy,yy)&-\end{pmatrix}\vdash AA\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx&xy&xk\\
yx&yy&yk\\
kx&ky&-\end{pmatrix}.

Here,
OO
can be thought of as the sort of objects;
OA(a,b)
as the sort of “horizontal arrows from
a
to
b
”;
AO(a,b)
as the sort of “vertical arrows from
a
to
b
”; and

AA\begin{pmatrix}a&b&f\\
c&d&g\\
h&i&-\end{pmatrix}

as the sort of “(
2
-dimensional) squares”
s
filling the diagram that consists of the horizontal arrows
f
,
g
and the vertical arrows
h
,
i
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

If we denote
OO
,
OA
,
AO
,
AA
by
O
,
A^{H}
,
A^{V}
,
S
, respectively, rename variables, permute the sorts in the context
10
10

10

Note that these operations do not affect the set of derivable judgments of the resulting theory.
, and use the “informal syntax” described in
[
10
]
(in which one omits variables that are implicit from the context), the axioms above become

\displaystyle\vdash O\;\mathsf{sort}

(Axiom 1’)

\displaystyle a,b:O\vdash A^{H}(a,b)

(Axiom 2’)

\displaystyle a,b:O\vdash A^{V}(a,b)

(Axiom 3’)

\displaystyle a,b,c,d:O,f:A^{H}(a,b),g:A^{H}(c,d),h:A^{V}(a,c),i:A^{V}(b,d)\vdash S(f,g,h,i)

(Axiom 4’)

Let
D
be the category having two objects, say
0
and
1
, and two non-identity arrows
i
,
j:0\rightarrow 1
. Then
\text{Mod}(\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph})
is equivalent to to the category of presheaves
\text{PSh}(D\times D)=\text{Fun}((D\times D)^{op},\text{Set})
. We will call such a presheaf a
double (directed) graph
.

Remark 3.1
.

At this point, it is possible to observe a pattern which, in general, can be verified by induction in a straightforward way: a product of derivable judgments
(\vdash S\;\mathsf{sort})\odot J
, where
\vdash S\;\mathsf{sort}
and
J
come from theories
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{T}
, resp., can be obtained from
J
by replacing (1) each sort symbol
T
by
ST
, (2) each term symbol
t
by
St
, and (3) each variable
y
by
xy
, where
x
is the variable associated with the empty context in
\mathbb{T}
. Similarly, the judgment
J\odot(\vdash S\;\mathsf{sort})
is obtained from
J
by replacing (1) each
T
by
TS
, (2) each
t
by
tS
, and (3) each
y
by
yx
.

3.1.2
Calculating
\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}
and
\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}

To describe
\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}
, we must add to
\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}
, as axioms, all
J\odot J^{\prime}
where
J
is a non-sort axiom in
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
and
J^{\prime}
is an axiom in
\mathbb{T}_{graph}
. Following the above remark on how to express products involving
\vdash O\;\mathsf{sort}
, we directly obtain the following axioms, where we write
\circ^{V}
for
(\circ,O)
and
id^{V}
for
(id,O)
(the superscript
V
stands for “vertical”):

\displaystyle xx,yx,zx:OO,fx:AO(xx,yx),gx:AO(yx,zx)\vdash\circ^{V}(xx,yx,zx,fx,gx):AO(xx,zx)

(Axiom 5)

\displaystyle xx:OO\vdash id^{V}(xx):AO(xx,xx)

(Axiom 6)

\displaystyle xx,yx,zx,wx:OO,fx:AO(xx,yx),gx:AO(yx,zx),hx:AO(zx,wx)\vdash

(Axiom 7)

\displaystyle\circ^{V}(xx,yx,wx,fx,\circ^{V}(yx,zx,wx,gx,hx))\equiv\circ^{V}(xx,zx,wx,\circ^{V}(xx,yx,zx,fx,gx),hx):AO(xx,wx)

\displaystyle xx,yx:OO,fx:AO(xx,yx)\vdash\circ^{V}(xx,xx,yx,id^{V}(xx),fx)\equiv fx:AO(xx,yx)

(Axiom 8)

\displaystyle xx,yx:OO,fx:AO(xx,yx)\vdash\circ^{V}(xx,yx,yx,fx,id^{V}(yx))\equiv fx:AO(xx,yx)

(Axiom 9)

We think of these axioms as prescribing, respectively, the following structures/properties: composition of vertical arrows (“vertical composition”), vertical identities, associativity of vertical composition, vertical left unitality, and vertical right unitality.

In fact, by using the informal syntax, representing
\circ^{V}
in infix notation, and renaming the symbols
OO
,
OA
, etc. as in the description of
\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}
, we obtain the following list of axioms:

\displaystyle a,b,c:O,f:A^{V}(a,b),g:A^{V}(b,c)\vdash\circ^{V}(f,g):A^{V}(a,c)

(Axiom 5’)

\displaystyle a:O\vdash id^{V}(a):A^{V}(a,a)

(Axiom 6’)

\displaystyle a,b,c,d:O,f:A^{V}(a,b),g:A^{V}(b,c),h:A^{V}(c,d)\vdash f\circ^{V}(g\circ^{V}h)\equiv(f\circ^{V}g)\circ^{V}h:A^{V}(a,d)

(Axiom 7’)

\displaystyle a,b:O,f:A^{V}(a,b)\vdash id^{V}(a)\circ^{V}f\equiv f:A^{V}(a,b)

(Axiom 8’)

\displaystyle a,b:O,f:A^{V}(a,b)\vdash f\circ^{V}id^{V}(b)\equiv f:A^{V}(a,b)

(Axiom 9’)

Now, we add the tensor products
J\odot(x,y:O\vdash A(x,y)\;\mathsf{sort})
where
J
is a non-sort axiom in
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
.

When
J
is the axiom for composition, we obtain, denoting
(\circ,A)
by
\square^{V}
,

\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx:OO&xy:OO&xk:A(xx,xy)\\
yx:OO&yy:OO&yk:A(yx,yy)\\
zx:OO&zy:OO&zk:A(zx,zy)\\
fx:AO(xx,yx)&fy:AO(xy,yy)&fk:AA(xx,xy,xk,yx,yy,yk,fx,fy)\\
gx:AO(yx,zx)&gy:AO(yy,zy)&gk:AA(yx,yy,yk,zx,zy,zk,gx,gy)\end{pmatrix}

\top

\square^{V}\begin{pmatrix}xx&xy&xk\\
yx&yy&yk\\
zx&zy&zk\\
fx&fy&fk\\
gx&gy&gk\end{pmatrix}:AA\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx&xy&xk\\
zx&zy&zk\\
\circ(x,y,z,f,g)^{A(x,z)}\otimes x^{O}&\circ(x,y,z,f,g)^{A(x,z)}\otimes y^{O}&-\end{pmatrix}.

The latter sort expression, in turn, equals

AA\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx&xy&xk\\
zx&zy&zk\\
\circ^{V}(xx,yx,zx,fx,gx)&\circ^{V}(xy,yy,zy,fy,gy)&-\end{pmatrix}.

We view
\square^{V}
