as encoding vertical composition of 2-cells: using the informal syntax, permuting the variables and writing
\square^{V}
in infix notation, this axiom becomes

\begin{pmatrix}[l]a:O&b:O&f:A^{H}(a,b)\\
c:O&d:O&g:A^{H}(c,d)\\
e:O&f:O&h:A^{H}(e,f)\\
i:A^{V}(a,c)&j:A^{V}(b,d)&s:S(f,g,i,j)\\
k:A^{V}(c,e)&l:A^{V}(d,f)&t:S(g,h,k,l)\end{pmatrix}\vdash\square^{V}(s,t):S(f,g,i\circ^{V}k,j\circ^{V}l).

Next, let us compute
J\odot(x,y:O\vdash A(x,y)\;\mathsf{sort})
where
J
is the axiom that introduces
id
: writing
id_{\square}^{V}
(“vertical identity of a horizontal arrow”) for
(id,A)
, we obtain

xx:OO,xy:OO,xk:OA(xx,xy)\vdash id_{\square}^{V}(xx,xy,xk):AA\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx&xy&xk\\
xx&xy&xk\\
id(x)^{A(x,x)}\otimes x^{O}&id(x)^{A(x,x)}\otimes y^{O}&-\end{pmatrix},

and the latter sort expression simplifies to

AA\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx&xy&xk\\
xx&xy&xk\\
id^{V}(xx)&id^{V}(xy)&-\end{pmatrix}.

In the informal syntax we have

x,y:O,f:A^{H}(x,y)\vdash id_{\square}^{V}(f):S(f,f,id(a),id(b)).

Now, we calculate
J\odot(x,y:O\vdash A(x,y)\;\mathsf{sort})
where
J
is the associativity axiom:

\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx:OO&xy:OO&xk:OA(xx,xy)\\
yx:OO&yy:OO&yk:OA(yx,yy)\\
zx:OO&zy:OO&zk:OA(zx,zy)\\
wx:OO&wy:OO&wk:OA(wx,wy)\\
fx:AO(xx,yx)&fy:AO(xy,yy)&fk:AA(xx,xy,xk,yx,yy,yk,fx,fy)\\
gx:AO(yx,zx)&gy:AO(yy,zy)&gk:AA(yx,yy,yk,zx,zy,zk,gx,gy)\\
hx:AO(zx,wx)&hy:AO(zy,wy)&hk:AA(zx,zy,zk,wx,wy,wk,hx,hy)\\
\end{pmatrix}

\top

\square^{V}\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx&xy&xk\\
yx&yy&yk\\
wx&wy&wk\\
fx&xy&fk\\
\circ(y,z,w,g,h)^{A(y,w)}\otimes x^{O}&\circ(y,z,w,g,h)^{A(y,w)}\otimes y^{O}&\circ(y,z,w,g,h)^{A(y,w)}\otimes k^{A(x,y)}\end{pmatrix}

\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\equiv$}}

\square^{V}\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx&xy&xk\\
zx&zy&zk\\
wx&wy&wk\\
\circ(x,y,z,f,g)^{A(x,z)}\otimes x^{O}&\circ(x,y,z,f,g)^{A(x,z)}\otimes y^{O}&\circ(x,y,z,f,g)^{A(x,z)}\otimes k^{A(x,y)}\\
hx&hy&hk\end{pmatrix}

\cdot\cdot

AA\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx&xy&xk\\
wx&wy&wk\\
\circ(x,y,w,f,\circ(y,z,w,g,h))^{A(x,w)}\otimes x^{O}&\circ(x,y,w,f,\circ(y,z,w,g,h))^{A(x,w)}\otimes y^{O}&-\end{pmatrix}.

To complete the description, we note that the terms involving “
\otimes
” are as follows:

\displaystyle\circ(y,z,w,g,h)^{A(y,w)}\otimes x^{O}

\displaystyle=\circ^{V}(yx,zx,wx,gx,hx)

\displaystyle\circ(y,z,w,g,h)^{A(y,w)}\otimes y^{O}

\displaystyle=\circ^{V}(yy,zy,wy,gy,hy)

\displaystyle\circ(y,z,w,g,h)^{A(y,w)}\otimes k^{A(x,y)}

\displaystyle=\square^{V}(yx,yy,yk,zx,zy,zk,wx,wy,wk,gx,gy,gk,hx,ky,hk)

\displaystyle\circ(x,y,z,f,g)^{A(x,z)}\otimes x^{O}

\displaystyle=\circ^{V}(xx,yx,zx,fx,gx)

\displaystyle\circ(x,y,z,f,g)^{A(x,z)}\otimes y^{O}

\displaystyle=\circ^{V}(xy,yy,zy,fy,gy)

\displaystyle\circ(x,y,z,f,g)^{A(x,z)}\otimes k^{A(x,y)}

\displaystyle=\square^{V}(xx,xy,xk,yx,yy,yk,zx,zy,zk,fx,fy,fk,gx,gy,gk)

\displaystyle\circ(x,y,w,f,\circ(y,z,w,g,h))^{A(x,w)}\otimes x^{O}

\displaystyle=\circ^{V}(xx,yx,wx,fx,\circ^{V}(yx,zx,wx,gx,hx))

\displaystyle\circ(x,y,w,f,\circ(y,z,w,g,h))^{A(x,w)}\otimes y^{O}

\displaystyle=\circ^{V}(xy,yy,wy,fy,\circ^{V}(yy,zy,wy,gy,hy)).

The counterpart of Axiom 12 in the informal syntax is

\begin{pmatrix}[l]a_{1}:O&b_{1}:O&f_{1}:A^{H}(a_{1},b_{1})\\
a_{2}:O&b_{2}:O&f_{2}:A^{H}(a_{2},b_{2})\\
a_{3}:O&b_{3}:O&f_{3}:A^{H}(a_{3},b_{3})\\
a_{4}:O&b_{4}:O&f_{4}:A^{H}(a_{4},b_{4})\\
g_{1}:A^{V}(a_{1},a_{2})&h_{1}:A^{V}(b_{1},b_{2})&s_{1}:S(f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},h_{1})\\
g_{2}:A^{V}(a_{2},a_{3})&h_{2}:A^{V}(b_{2},b_{3})&s_{2}:S(f_{2},f_{3},g_{2},h_{2})\\
g_{3}:A^{V}(a_{3},a_{4})&h_{3}:A^{V}(b_{3},b_{4})&s_{3}:S(f_{3},f_{4},g_{3},h_{3})\\
\end{pmatrix}

\top

s_{1}\square^{V}(s_{2}\square^{V}s_{3})\equiv(s_{1}\square^{V}s_{2})\square^{V}s_{3}:S(f_{1},f_{4},g_{1}\circ^{V}(g_{2}\circ^{V}g_{3}),h_{1}\circ^{V}(h_{2}\circ^{V}h_{3})).

Now, when
J
is the left unitality axiom,
J\odot(x,y:O\vdash A(x,y))
equals

\begin{pmatrix}xx:OO&xy:OO&xk:OA(xx,xy)\\
yx:OO&yy:OO&yk:OA(yx,yy)\\
fx:AO(xx,yx)&fy:AO(xy,yy)&fk:S(xx,xy,xk,yx,yy,yk,fx,fy)\end{pmatrix}

\top

\circ(x,x,y,id(x),f)\otimes k^{A(x,y)}\equiv fk:S(xx,xy,xk,yx,yy,yk,fx,fy),

and the remaining expression is given by

\circ(x,x,y,id(x),f)\otimes k^{A(x,y)}=\square^{V}\begin{pmatrix}xx&xy&xk\\
xx&xy&xk\\
yx&yy&yk\\
id^{V}(xx)&id^{V}(xy)&id_{\square}^{V}(xx,xy,xk)\\
fx&fy&fk\end{pmatrix}.

In the informal syntax we have

\begin{pmatrix}a:O&b:OO&f:A^{V}(a,b)\\
c:O&d:O&g:A^{V}(c,d)\\
h:A^{V}(a,c)&i:A^{V}(b,d)&s:S(a,b,f,c,d,g,h,i)\end{pmatrix}\vdash id_{\square}^{V}(f)\square^{V}s\equiv s:S(f,g,h,i).

When
J
is the right unitality axiom,
J\odot(x,y:O\vdash A(x,y))
is calculated similarly: we have

\begin{pmatrix}xx:OO&xy:OO&xk:OA(xx,xy)\\
yx:OO&yy:OO&yk:OA(yx,yy)\\
fx:AO(xx,yx)&fy:AO(xy,yy)&fk:S(xx,xy,xk,yx,yy,yk,fx,fy)\end{pmatrix}

\top

\circ(x,y,y,f,id(y))\otimes k^{A(x,y)}\equiv fk:S(xx,xy,xk,yx,yy,yk,fx,fy),

and the remaining expression is given by

\circ(x,y,y,f,id(y))\otimes k^{A(x,y)}=\square^{V}\begin{pmatrix}xx&xy&xk\\
yx&yy&yk\\
yx&yy&yk\\
fx&fy&fk\\
id^{V}(yx)&id^{V}(yy)&id_{\square}^{V}(yx,yy,yk)\end{pmatrix}.

The corresponding judgment in the informal syntax is

\begin{pmatrix}a:O&b:O&f:A^{V}(a,b)\\
c:O&d:O&g:A^{V}(c,d)\\
h:A^{V}(a,c)&i:A^{V}(b,d)&s:S(a,b,f,c,d,g,h,i)\end{pmatrix}\vdash s\square^{V}id_{\square}^{V}(g)\equiv g:S(f,g,h,i).

This concludes the description of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}
. We can view axioms 5-14 as extending
\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}
with operations that endow a given double graph with a category structure whose objects are the horizontal arrows and whose morphisms are the squares.

The description of
\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
is symmetric to that of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}
: the axioms will be the judgments
J\odot J^{\prime}
where
J
is an axiom in
\mathbb{T}_{graph}
and
\mathbb{T}
is a non-sort axiom in
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
. We leave the full description of these axioms (15 to 24) to the interested reader. Models of
\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
will be double graphs with a category structure having the vertical arrows as its objects and the squares as its morphisms; thus in this case we depict squares as composing horizontally. In what follows, we will denote the symbols
(O,\circ)
,
(O,id)
,
(A,\circ)
,
(A,id)
by
\circ^{H}
,
id^{H}
,
\square^{H}
,
id_{\square}^{H}
, respectively.

3.1.3
Calculating
\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}

The theory
\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}\cup\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
has as models double graphs endowed with two category structures, each having the squares as its morphisms: one in which squares compose vertically (having horizontal arrows are their sources/targets), and one where they compose vertically (having vertical arrows as their sources/targets). However, there is no other required relation between these two categories. That role will be played by the axioms added via the extension
\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{graph}\cup\mathbb{T}_{graph}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
, which establishes a kind of “parameterized commutativity” between the composition and identity-assigning operations on both sides.

We must add as axioms the judgments
J\odot J^{\prime}
where
J
,
J^{\prime}
are non-sort axioms in
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
. In fact, it suffices to consider a pair of term axioms.

Firstly, suppose that both
J
,
J^{\prime}
are the axiom that introduces
\circ
. Then
J\odot J^{\prime}
is

\begin{pmatrix}[l]xx:OO&xy:OO&xz:OO&xf:OA(xx,xy)&xg:OA(xy,xz)\\
yx:OO&yy:OO&yz:OO&yf:OA(yx,yy)&yg:OA(yy,yz)\\
zx:OO&zy:OO&zz:OO&zf:OA(zx,zy)&zg:OA(zy,zz)\\
fx:AO(xx,yx)&fy:AO(xy,yy)&fz:AO(xz,yz)&ff:AA(xx,xy,xk,yx,yy,yk,kx,ky)&fg:AA(xy,xz,xk,yy,yz,yk,ky,kz)\\
gx:AO(yx,zx)&gy:AO(yy,zy)&gz:AO(yz,zz)&gf:AA(yx,yy,yk,zx,zy,zk,kx,ky)&gg:AA(yy,yz,yk,zy,zz,zk,ky,kz)\end{pmatrix}

\top

\square^{H}\begin{pmatrix}xx&xy&xz&xf&xg\\
zx&zy&zz&zf&zg\\
\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes x^{O}&\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes y^{O}&\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes z^{O}&\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes f^{A(x,y)}&\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes g^{A(y,z)}\end{pmatrix}

\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\equiv$}}

\square^{V}\begin{pmatrix}xx&xz&x^{O}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\\
yx&yz&y^{O}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\\
zx&zz&z^{O}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\\
fx&fz&f^{A(x,y)}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\\
gx&gz&g^{A(y,z)}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\end{pmatrix}

\cdot\cdot

AA\begin{pmatrix}xx&xz&x^{O}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\\
zx&zz&z^{O}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\\
\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes x^{O}&\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes z^{O}&-\end{pmatrix}.

The terms involving “
\otimes
” can be calculated in a straightforward way; for example, we have

\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes f^{A(x,y)}=\square^{V}\begin{pmatrix}xx&xy&xf\\
yx&yy&yf\\
zx&zy&zf\\
fx&fy&ff\\
gx&gy&gf\end{pmatrix},\qquad\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes g^{A(y,z)}=\square^{V}\begin{pmatrix}xy&xz&xg\\
yy&yz&yg\\
zy&zz&zg\\
fy&fz&fg\\
gy&gz&gg\end{pmatrix},

f^{A(x,y)}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)=\square^{H}\begin{pmatrix}xx&xy&xz&xf&xg\\
yx&yy&yz&yf&yg\\
fx&fy&fz&ff&fg\end{pmatrix},\qquad g^{A(y,z)}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)=\square^{H}\begin{pmatrix}yx&yy&yz&yf&yg\\
zx&zy&zz&zf&zg\\
gx&gy&gz&gf&gg\end{pmatrix}.

In the informal syntax, we have

\begin{pmatrix}[l]a_{11}:O&a_{12}:O&a_{13}:O&h_{11}:A^{H}(a_{11},a_{12})&h_{12}:A^{H}(a_{12},a_{13})\\
a_{21}:O&a_{22}:O&a_{23}:O&h_{21}:A^{H}(a_{21},a_{22})&h_{22}:A^{H}(a_{22},a_{23})\\
a_{31}:O&a_{31}:O&a_{33}:O&h_{31}:A^{H}(a_{31},a_{32})&h_{32}:A^{H}(a_{32},a_{33})\\
v_{11}:A^{V}(a_{11},a_{21})&v_{12}:A^{V}(a_{12},a_{22})&v_{13}:A^{V}(a_{13},a_{23})&s_{11}:S(h_{11},h_{21},v_{11},v_{12})&s_{12}:S(h_{12},h_{22},v_{12},v_{13})\\
v_{21}:A^{V}(a_{21},a_{31})&v_{22}:A^{V}(a_{22},a_{32})&v_{23}:A^{V}(a_{23},a_{33})&s_{21}:S(h_{21},h_{31},v_{21},v_{22})&s_{22}:S(h_{22},h_{32},v_{22},v_{23})\end{pmatrix}

\top

(s_{11}\square^{V}s_{21})\square^{H}(s_{12}\square^{V}s_{22})\equiv(s_{11}\square^{H}s_{12})\square^{V}(s_{21}\square^{H}s_{22}):S(h_{11}\circ h_{12},h_{31}\circ h_{32},v_{11}\circ v_{21},v_{13}\circ v_{23}).

We view this judgment as the assertion that the
2
-dimensional diagram below on the left has an unambiguous pasting as a square whose boundary is as on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{11}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{12}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{13}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{11}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{13}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{21}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{22}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{23}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{31}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{32}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{33}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{31}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{33}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{11}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{11}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{11}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{13}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{11}\circ^{H}h_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{11}\circ^{V}v_{21}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{13}\circ^{V}v_{23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{21}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{21}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{21}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{22}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{22}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{22}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{31}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{32}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{31}\circ^{H}h_{32}}

Now, suppose that
J
is the axiom that introduces
\circ
and
J^{\prime}
is the one that introduces
id
. Then
J\odot J^{\prime}
equals

\begin{pmatrix}xx:OO\\
yx:OO\\
zx:OO\\
fx:AO(xx,yx)\\
gx:AO(yx,zx)\end{pmatrix}

\top

id_{\square}^{H}\begin{pmatrix}xx\\
\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes x^{O}\end{pmatrix}\equiv\square^{V}\begin{pmatrix}xx&xx&x^{O}\otimes id(x)\\
yx&yx&y^{O}\otimes id(x)\\
zx&zx&z^{O}\otimes id(x)\\
fx&fx&f^{A(x,y)}\otimes id(x)\\
gx&gx&g^{A(y,z)}\otimes id(x)\end{pmatrix}:AA\begin{pmatrix}xx&xx&x^{O}\otimes id(x)\\
zx&zx&z^{O}\otimes id(x)\\
\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes x^{O}&\circ(x,y,z,f,g)\otimes x^{O}&-\end{pmatrix}.

In the informal syntax, renaming the variables and sort/term symbols, we have the judgment

a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}:O,v_{1}:A^{V}(a_{1},a_{2}),v_{2}:A^{V}(a_{2},a_{3})

\top

id_{\square}^{H}(v_{1}\circ^{V}v_{2})\equiv id_{\square}^{H}(v_{1})\square^{V}id_{\square}^{H}(v_{2}):S(id^{H}(a_{1}),id^{H}(a_{3}),v_{1}\circ^{V}v_{2},v_{1}\circ^{V}v_{2})

Similarly, if
J
is the axiom that introduces
id
and
J^{\prime}
is the one that introduces
\circ
, then
J\odot J^{\prime}
equals

\begin{pmatrix}xx:OO&xy:OO&xz:OO&xf:OA(xx,xy)&xg:OA(xy,xz)\end{pmatrix}

\top

\square^{H}\begin{pmatrix}xx&xy&xz&xf&xg\\
xx&xy&xz&xf&xg\\
id(x)\otimes x^{O}&id(x)\otimes y^{O}&id(x)\otimes x^{O}&id(x)\otimes f^{A(x,y)}&id(x)\otimes g^{A(y,z)}\end{pmatrix}\equiv id^{V}\begin{pmatrix}xx&xy&x^{O}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)^{A(x,z)}\end{pmatrix}

\cdot\cdot

AA\begin{pmatrix}xx&xz&x^{O}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)^{A(x,z)}\\
xx&xz&x^{O}\otimes\circ(x,y,z,f,g)^{A(x,z)}\\
id(x)\otimes x^{O}&id(x)\otimes z^{O}&-\end{pmatrix}.

In the informal syntax we obtain

a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}:O,h_{1}:A^{H}(a_{1},a_{2}),h_{2}:A^{H}(a_{2},a_{3})

\top

id_{\square}^{V}(h_{1})\square^{H}id_{\square}^{V}(h_{2})\equiv id^{V}(h_{1}\circ^{H}h_{2}):S(h_{1}\circ^{H}h_{2},h_{1}\circ^{H}h_{2},id^{V}(a_{1}),id^{V}(a_{3})).

Finally, if
J
and
J^{\prime}
are both the axiom that introduces
id
, then
J\odot J^{\prime}
is

xx:OO\vdash id_{\square}^{H}(xx,xx,id(x)\otimes x^{O})\equiv id_{\square}^{V}(xx,xx,x^{O}\otimes id(x)):AA\begin{pmatrix}xx&xx&x^{O}\otimes id(x)\\
xx&xx&x^{O}\otimes id(x)\\
id(x)\otimes x^{O}&id(x)\otimes x^{O}&-\end{pmatrix}.

In the informal syntax we have

x:O\vdash id_{\square}^{H}(id^{V}(x))\equiv id_{\square}^{V}(id^{H}(x)):S(id^{H}(x),id^{H}(x),id^{V}(x),id^{V}(x)).

This concludes the description of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
.

3.2
The tensor product of (multisorted) Lawvere theories

A Lawvere theory can be presented by a generalized algebraic theory having a single sort symbol, possibly term and term equality axioms, and no sort equality axioms. To see this, note first that, by restricting Cartmell’s equivalence
\text{GAT}\simeq\text{Cont}
, such theories correspond up to isomorphism to the contextual categories that have a single length
1
object. On the other hand, as verified in
[
13
]
, the full subcategory of
Cont
spanned by the latter is isomorphic to the category of Lawvere theories.

In particular, for a gat of that form, say whose sort symbol is
O
, all contexts are of the form
x_{1}:O,...,x_{n}:O
and all term axioms are of the form
x_{1}:O,...,x_{n}:O\vdash t(x_{1},...,x_{n}):O
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
are (presentations of) Lawvere theories in the above sense. Writing
O
for the sort symbol in both cases,
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is as follows:

•

It has a single sort axiom
OO
, which we will also denote by
O
.

•

Its term symbols are
sO:=(s,O)
where
s\in\Sigma(\mathbb{A})^{term}
and
Ot:=(O,t)
where
t\in\Sigma(\mathbb{B})^{term}
. By abuse of notation, we will also write
s
for
sO
and
t
for
Ot
.

The axiom of
sO
in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is, after performing the above symbol identifications, the same as the axiom of
s
in
\mathbb{A}
. Indeed, tensoring
x_{1}:O,...,x_{n}:O\vdash s(x_{1},...,x_{n}):O
with the axiom
\vdash O\;\mathsf{sort}
from
\mathbb{B}
gives

x_{1}y:OO,...,x_{n}y:OO\vdash sO(x_{1}y,...,x_{m}y):OO

(where
y
is the variable associated with the empty context of
\mathbb{B}
), which we rewrite as

x_{1}y:O,...,x_{n}y:O\vdash s(x_{1}y,...,x_{m}y):O.

Similarly, the axiom of
Ot
in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is the same, up to symbol renaming, as the axiom of
t
in
\mathbb{B}
.

•

For each term equality axiom
J
in
\mathbb{A}
, we have a term equality axiom
J\odot(\vdash O\;\mathsf{sort})
in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
. Following the above conventions, the latter judgment can be viewed as
J
itself. Similarly, every term equality axiom in
\mathbb{B}
can be viewed as an axiom in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
.

•

We also have a term equality axiom for each
s\in\Sigma(\mathbb{A})^{term}
and
t\in\Sigma(\mathbb{B})^{term}
obtained by tensoring the respective axioms, say
x_{1},...,x_{m}:O\vdash s(x_{1},...,x_{m}):O
and
y_{1},...,y_{n}:O\vdash t(y_{1},...,y_{n}):O
:

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}:OO&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}:OO\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}:OO&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n}:OO\end{pmatrix}

\top

Ot\begin{pmatrix}s(x_{1},...,x_{m})^{O}\otimes y_{1}^{O}&\cdots&s(x_{1},..,x_{m})^{O}\otimes y_{n}^{O}\end{pmatrix}\equiv sO\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{O}\otimes t(y_{1},...,y_{n})^{O}\\
\vdots\\
x_{m}^{O}\otimes t(y_{1},...,y_{n})^{O}\end{pmatrix}:OO.

But note that for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
we have

x_{i}^{O}\otimes t(y_{1},...,y_{n})^{O}=Ot(x_{i}y_{1},...,x_{i}y_{n}),

s(x_{1},...,x_{m})^{O}\otimes y_{j}^{O}=sO(x_{1}y_{j},...,x_{m}y_{j}),

so, after writing
O
for
OO
, etc., we conclude that (
*
) equals

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}:O&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}:O\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}:O&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n}:O\end{pmatrix}\vdash t\begin{pmatrix}s\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}\end{pmatrix}&\cdots&s\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{n}\\
\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{n}\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}\equiv s\begin{pmatrix}t(x_{1}y_{1}\;\cdots\;x_{1}y_{n})\\
\vdots\\
t(x_{m}y_{1}\;\cdots\;x_{m}y_{n})\end{pmatrix}:O.

In fact, since every term is of sort
O
, it causes no ambiguity to simply write

t(s(x_{1}y_{1},...,x_{m}y_{1}),...,s(x_{1}y_{n},...,x_{m}y_{n}))\equiv s(t(x_{1}y_{1},...,x_{1}y_{n}),...,t(x_{m}y_{1},...,x_{m}y_{n})).

Hence
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
coincides with the tensor product between
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
as in
[
15
]
. The above axioms can be viewed in the following way: a model of
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is a set
X
(given by the sort axiom) endowed with an
\mathbb{A}
-model structure (given by the terms and axioms from
\mathbb{A}
) and a
\mathbb{B}
-model structure (given by the terms and axioms of
\mathbb{B}
) such that the action on
X
of every
n
-ary operation from
\mathbb{B}
is an
\mathbb{A}
-model morphism
X^{n}\rightarrow X
. The latter condition can be replaced by an analogous one: the action on
X
of every
n
-ary operation from
\mathbb{A}
is a
\mathbb{B}
-model morphism
X^{n}\rightarrow X
.

We can also consider the tensor product of two
multisorted Lawvere theories
: generalized algebraic theories in which every sort axiom is of the form
\vdash S\;\mathsf{sort}
, and which have no sort equality axioms. If
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
are two such theories, it can be checked
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is given by the following data up to variable renaming:

•

For sort symbols
S
,
T
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, respectively, we have a sort symbol
ST
introduced by

\vdash ST\;\mathsf{sort}.

•

Suppose that
T
is a sort symbol in
\mathbb{B}
.

For a term symbol
s
in
\mathbb{A}
, say introduced by
x_{1}:S_{1},...,x_{m}:S_{m}\vdash s(x_{1},...,x_{m}):S
, we have a term symbol
sT
introduced by

x_{1}y:S_{1}T,...,x_{m}y:S_{m}T\vdash sT(x_{1}y,...,x_{m}y):ST.

Similarly, a term equality axiom
J
in
\mathbb{A}
yields an axiom
J\otimes(\vdash T\;\mathsf{sort})
in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
. The latter judgment is obtained from
J
by replacing each sort symbol
S
by
ST
, each term symbol
s
by
sT
, and each variable
x
by
xy
.

•

For a sort symbol
S
in
\mathbb{A}
, we have an axiom
(\vdash S\;\mathsf{sort})\otimes J
in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
whenever
J
is a term or term equality axiom in
\mathbb{B}
. An explicit description can be given as in the previous item.

•

Suppose given term axioms

\displaystyle x_{1}:S_{1},...,x_{m}:S_{m}

\displaystyle\vdash s(x_{1},...,x_{m}):S

\displaystyle y_{1}:T_{1},...,y_{n}:T_{n}

\displaystyle\vdash t(y_{1},...,y_{n}):T

in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, respectively. Then we have the following axiom in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
:

(x_{i}y_{j}:S_{i}T_{j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}\vdash St\begin{pmatrix}sT_{1}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}\end{pmatrix}&\cdots&sT_{n}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{n}\\
\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{n}\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}\equiv sT\begin{pmatrix}S_{1}t(x_{1}y_{1}\;\cdots\;x_{1}y_{n})\\
\vdots\\
S_{m}t(x_{m}y_{1}\;\cdots\;x_{m}y_{n})\end{pmatrix}:ST.

Remark 3.2
.

Note that multisorted Lawvere theories where every term symbol is unary, i.e. is introduced by an axiom of the form
x:X\vdash f(x):Y
, can be used as presentations of categories. In fact, denoting the full subcategory of
GAT
spanned by such theories by
\text{GAT}_{cat}
, we have an equivalence of categories
F:\text{GAT}_{cat}\rightarrow\text{Cat}
that sends
\mathbb{B}
to the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{B})
spanned by the length-
1
objects. Moreover,
\text{GAT}_{cat}
is closed in
GAT
under
\otimes
and, for
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}\in\text{GAT}_{cat}
, we have a canonical isomorphism
F(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})\cong F(\mathbb{A})\times F(\mathbb{B})
.

3.3
Locally finite direct categories

Generalized algebraic theories that only have sort introduction axioms admit a convenient combinatorial model, namely,
locally finite direct categories
(which we will abbreviate as lfdc). We recall (see
[
21
]
, Chapter 1) that a category
D
is said to be

•

Locally finite
if for every object
a
the slice category
D/a
is finite, i.e. it has finitely many morphisms.

•

Direct
if it has no infinite sequence of non-identity morphisms of the form

\cdots\longrightarrow a_{n+1}\longrightarrow a_{n}\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow a_{1}\longrightarrow a_{0}.

As explained in
[
21
]
, 1.6.1, if
\mathbb{T}
is a theory that only has sort axioms – there called a
type signature
–, the contextual category
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T})
is equivalent (as a category) to
\text{PSh}_{fp}(D)^{op}
where
\text{PSh}_{fp}(D)
is the category of finitely presentable presheaves on a lfdc
D
. Precisely,
D
can be taken as the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T})^{op}
, say
D(\mathbb{T})
, whose objects are the equivalence classes of contexts
\textbf{X},x:U
where
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
is an axiom. On the other hand, any category of the form
\text{PSh}_{fp}(D)^{op}
, where
D
is a ldfc, is equivalent to
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T})
for some type signature
\mathbb{T}
whose sort symbols correspond bijectively to objects of
D
.
11
11

11

Note, however, that we have not stated an equivalence between a category of lfdcs and one of type signatures.

To better understand the lfdc
D(\mathbb{T})
, we will consider the following characterization of lfdcs, which can be obtained from
[
21
]
, Lemma 1.1.17 by well-ordering the set of dimension
n
objects for each
n\geq 0
(we have omitted the details):

Lemma 3.3
.

A small category
D
is locally finite and direct if and only if there exist a well-ordered set
(L,\leq)
with a maximum element
\top
and a functor
D_{*}:(L,\leq)\rightarrow\text{Cat}
such that

(i)

D\cong D_{\top}
.

(ii)

For every limit element
n\in L
, the canonical functor
\underrightarrow{\text{lim }}_{m<n}D_{m}\rightarrow D_{n}
is an isomorphism. (In particular,
D_{\bot}=\emptyset
where
\bot
is the smallest element of
L
.)

(iii)

If
n\in L
has a successor
n^{+}
(hence if
n\neq\top
), there exists a presheaf
F:D_{n}^{op}\rightarrow\text{Set}
such that
\bigsqcup_{a\in D_{n}}F(a)
is finite, and the functor
\iota:D_{n}\rightarrow D_{n^{+}}
from the diagram fits into a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\int F)^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{n^{+}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\pi:\int F\rightarrow D_{n}
is the canonical projection from the category of elements of
F
, and
(\int F)^{+}
is obtained from
\int F
by freely adjoining a terminal object.

The presheaf
F
can be recovered from
\iota
up to isomorphism: writing
1
for the terminal object of
(\int F)^{+}
, so that
\pi^{\prime}(1)\in D_{n^{+}}
is the only object not in the image of
\iota
, we have a natural isomorphism from
F
to
\text{Hom}(\iota-,\pi^{\prime}(1)):D_{n}^{op}\rightarrow\text{Set}
that maps
x\in F(a)
to the image of of the unique arrow
(a,x)\rightarrow 1
under
\pi^{\prime}
. Conversely, this remark allows us to characterize when
D_{n}\rightarrow D_{n^{+}}
satisfies condition (iii):
\iota
is full, faithful and injective on objects,
D_{n^{+}}
has a single object not in the image of
\iota
, and, denoting that object by
b
, its only endomorphism is the identity and there exists no morphism
b\rightarrow a
for
a
in the image of
\iota
.

For an object
a
of a lfdc
D
, the category
D/a
being finite implies that the presheaf
Y(a)=\text{Hom}_{D}(-,a):D^{op}\rightarrow\text{Set}
has finitely many elements. The
boundary
of
a
, denoted by
\partial a
, is defined as the sub-presheaf of
Y(a)
given by
(\partial a)(a^{\prime})=\text{Hom}(a^{\prime},a)
if
a^{\prime}\neq a
, and
(\partial a)(a)=\emptyset
(in other words, it is obtained from
Y(a)
by removing the element
id_{a}\in Y(a)(a)
).

In item (iii), the object
b
adjoined via
\iota:D_{n}\rightarrow D_{n^{+}}
is such that
\partial b=F
(more precisely, we refer to the restriction of
\partial b
along
\iota
). Hence Lemma
3.3
states, informally, that
D
can be obtained by successively adding objects with a specified finite presheaf as its boundary.

Now, given a type signature
\mathbb{T}
, we can choose a well-ordered set
(L,\leq)
with a maximum element
\top
, and a family of theories
(\mathbb{T}_{n})_{n\in L}
such that
12
12

12

We assume, without loss of generality, that there exists an enumeration
(x_{i})_{i\geq 0}
of the set of variables of
\mathbb{T}
such that the contexts of all axioms are of the form
x_{1}:X_{1},x_{2}:X_{2},...,x_{k}:X_{k}
.

•

\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}_{\top}
.

•

For a limit element
n\in L
we have
\mathbb{T}_{n}=\bigcup_{m<n}\mathbb{T}_{m}
.

•

If
n\in L
has a successor
n^{+}
, then
\mathbb{T}_{n^{+}}
is obtained from
\mathbb{T}_{n}
by adjoining a sort symbol, say
S_{n}
, introduced by

x_{1}:X^{n}_{1},...,x_{k(n)}:X^{n}_{k(n)}\vdash S_{n}(x_{1},...,x_{k(n)})\;\mathsf{sort}.

The induced diagram of categories
(D(\mathbb{T}_{n}))_{n\in L}
, where
D(\mathbb{T}_{n})
is as described previously, satisfies condition (ii). This follows from the fact that the underlying-category functor
\text{Cont}\rightarrow\text{Cat}
preserves filtered colimits – in particular, colimits indexed by linearly ordered sets.

To check that (iii) is also satisfied, note that
D(\mathbb{T}_{n^{+}})
is the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T}_{n^{+}})^{op}
obtained from the image of the functor
D(\mathbb{T}_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T}_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T}_{n^{+}})
(which is full and faithful) by adjoining a single object: the equivalence class
[\textbf{X}^{n}]
of the context

\textbf{X}^{n}=(x_{1}:X^{n}_{1},...,x_{k(n)}:X^{n}_{k(n)},x_{k(n)+1}:S_{n}(x_{1},...,x_{k(n)})).

But a morphism added via
D(\mathbb{T}_{n})\rightarrow D(\mathbb{T}_{n^{+}})
is either
id_{[\textbf{X}^{n}]}
or opposite to one of the form
[\textbf{X}_{n}]\rightarrow[\textbf{X}_{m}]
with
m<n
. Indeed, by working syntactically it can be verified that the only endomorphism of
\textbf{X}_{n}
is the identity, and that there exists no context morphism from
\textbf{X}_{m}
to
\textbf{X}_{n}
for
m<n
. Moreover, the set of context morphisms

\{\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}_{n}\rightarrow\textbf{X}_{m}\mid m<n\}

is finite since every term symbol that occurs in
\textbf{X}_{m}
must also occur in
\textbf{X}_{n}
.

This shows that
D(\mathbb{T})
is locally finite and direct. In
[
21
]
, it is shown that
D(\mathbb{T})
determines
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T})
in the following sense: letting
\iota:D(\mathbb{T})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T})^{op}
be inclusion, the functor

N_{\mathbb{T}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T})^{op}\longrightarrow\text{PSh}_{fp}(D(\mathbb{T}))

given by
N_{\mathbb{T}}([\textbf{X}])=\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T})}([\textbf{X}],\iota-)
(which is the restricted Yoneda embedding associated with
\iota
) is an equivalence of categories. Moreover, it is natural with respect to extensions of type signatures: if
\varepsilon:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
adds a set of sort axioms to
\mathbb{T}
, the following diagram commutes up to isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T})^{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{PSh}_{fp}(D(\mathbb{T}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T}^{\prime})^{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{PSh}_{fp}(D(\mathbb{T}^{\prime})),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\varepsilon)^{op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\varepsilon)_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathbb{T}}}

where
D(\varepsilon)_{!}
is given by left Kan extension along
D(\varepsilon)^{op}:D(\mathbb{T})^{op}\rightarrow D(\mathbb{T}^{\prime})^{op}
(more explicitly,
D(\varepsilon)^{op}(F)
maps
[\textbf{X}]
to
F([\textbf{X}])
if
[\textbf{X}]
is in the image of
\varepsilon
, and to
\emptyset
otherwise).

Let
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{T}
be type signatures. Then, by definition,
\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T}
is a type signature whose sort symbols are pairs
ST:=(S,T)
where
S
and
T
are sort symbols in
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{T}
, respectively. This implies that
\text{Ob}(D(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T}))\cong\text{Ob}(D(\mathbb{S}))\times\text{Ob}(D(\mathbb{T}))
. We will now verify that, in fact,
D(\mathbb{S}\times\mathbb{T})\cong D(\mathbb{S})\times D(\mathbb{T})
.

This relies on a structure that will be introduced later: in Construction
6.5
, we obtain a functor

\otimes_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T})

given on objects by
[\textbf{X}]\otimes_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}[\textbf{Y}]=[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}]
, and on arrows by
[\textbf{f}]\otimes_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}[\textbf{g}]=[\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{g}]
where, if
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{m})
and
\textbf{g}=(g_{1},...,g_{n})
are context morphisms, then
\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{g}
is obtained by lexicographically reindexing the family of term expressions
(f_{i}^{\mathcal{S}(f_{i})}\otimes g_{j}^{\mathcal{S}(g_{j})})_{i\leq m,\;j\leq n}
.

Given axioms
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
and
\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
in
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{T}
, respectively, and choosing contexts
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x:S(x_{1},...,x_{m}))
and
\textbf{Y}^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y},y:T(y_{1},...,y_{n}))
, we have that
[\textbf{X}^{\prime}]\otimes_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}[\textbf{Y}^{\prime}]
is the equivalence class of the context
(\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}),z:ST(\cdots))
obtained from the axiom that introduces
ST
. This implies that
\otimes_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}
restricts to a bijective-on-objects functor

\otimes_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}^{D}:D(\mathbb{S})\times D(\mathbb{T})\longrightarrow D(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T}).

Our goal is to prove that it is an isomorphism. As a consequence, using the equivalence
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})^{op}\simeq\text{PSh}_{fp}(D(\mathbb{A}))
, we will then have
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T})^{op}\simeq\text{PSh}_{fp}(D(\mathbb{S})\times D(\mathbb{T}))
.

Firstly, let us consider extensions of type signatures
\mathbb{S}\subset\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{T}\subset\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
, each of which adds a single sort axiom. Denoting
D(\mathbb{S})
,
D(\mathbb{S}^{\prime})
,
D(\mathbb{T})
,
D(\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
by
D
,
D^{\prime}
,
E
,
E^{\prime}
, respectively, consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T})^{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{PSh}_{fp}(D\times E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T})^{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{PSh}_{fp}(D^{\prime}\times E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})^{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{PSh}_{fp}(D\times E^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\iota^{\prime})^{op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\iota)^{op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{\prime}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}}

where

•

\iota:\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
and
\iota^{\prime}:\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}
are the trivial interpretations.

•

N_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}
maps a context class
[\textbf{A}]
to the presheaf
\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T})}(-\otimes_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}^{D}-,[\textbf{A}]):(D\times E)^{op}\rightarrow\text{Set}
. In other words,
N_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}
is the restricted Yoneda embedding associated with the composite

D\times E\overset{\otimes_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}^{D}}{\longrightarrow}D(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T})^{op}.

Similarly for
N_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
and
N_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}}
.

•

I:D\times E\rightarrow D\times E^{\prime}
and
I^{\prime}:D\times E\rightarrow D^{\prime}\times E
are the inclusion functors, and the subscript
!
indicates the left Kan extension functor restricted to finitely presentable presheaves.

Now, assume that
\otimes^{D}_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}
,
\otimes^{D}_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
and
\otimes^{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}}
are isomorphisms. By pseudo-naturality of
N_{\mathbb{A}}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A})^{op}\rightarrow\text{PSh}_{fp}(D(\mathbb{A}))
with respect to extensions of type signatures, the above diagram commutes up to isomorphism.

Write
\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
for the theory
(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T})\cup(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
. Since the extensions in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))}}

are full and faithful, and the canonical functor

(D^{\prime}\times E)\times_{D\times E}(D\times E^{\prime})\cong D(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T})\bigsqcup_{D(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T})}D(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\longrightarrow D(\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))

is bijective on objects, the latter is an isomorphism. This implies that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T})^{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{PSh}_{fp}(D\times E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T})^{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{PSh}_{fp}(D^{\prime}\times E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})^{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{PSh}_{fp}(D\times E^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime}))^{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{PSh}_{fp}((D^{\prime}\times E)\sqcup_{D\times E}(D\times E^{\prime}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\iota^{\prime})^{op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\iota)^{op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{\prime}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}

extending (
*
) in the evident way commutes up to isomorphism, and
N_{\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}
is an equivalence of categories.

Next, let
\textbf{X}=(\partial\textbf{X},x:U)
and
\textbf{Y}=(\partial\textbf{Y},y:V)
be contexts in
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
, respectively, such that
\partial\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
and
\partial\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
are the axioms added via
\mathbb{S}\subset\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{T}\subset\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
. Then
N_{\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}
induces isomorphisms

\displaystyle N_{\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}([\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}]\times_{[\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}]}[\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}])

\displaystyle\cong N_{\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}[\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}]\bigsqcup_{N_{\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}[\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}]}N_{\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}[\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}]

\displaystyle\cong Y([\textbf{X}],[\partial\textbf{Y}])\bigsqcup_{Y([\partial\textbf{X}],[\partial\textbf{Y}])}Y([\partial\textbf{X}],[\textbf{Y}])

\displaystyle\cong\partial([\textbf{X}],[\textbf{Y}])

between presheaves on
(D^{\prime}\times E)\sqcup_{D\times E}(D\times E^{\prime})
. Here,
\partial([\textbf{X}],[\textbf{Y}])
denotes the boundary presheaf of
([\textbf{X}],[\textbf{Y}])\in D^{\prime}\times E^{\prime}
viewed, by restriction, as a presheaf on
(D^{\prime}\times E)\sqcup_{D\times E}(D\times E^{\prime})
. As the extension
\partial(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\subset\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
is given by adjoining a sort axiom in context
[\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}]\times_{[\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}]}[\partial\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}]
and
D^{\prime}\times E^{\prime}
fits into a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int\partial([\textbf{X}],[\textbf{Y}])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(D^{\prime}\times E)\sqcup_{D\times E}(D\times E^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int(\partial([\textbf{X}],[\textbf{Y}]))^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}\times E^{\prime},}}

we obtain an isomorphism
D(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\simeq D^{\prime}\times E^{\prime}
.

This result can be used as the induction step in a proof that
D(\mathbb{S})\times D(\mathbb{T})\rightarrow D(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{T})
is an isomorphism for all type signatures
\mathbb{S}
,
\mathbb{T}
. In more detail, choose a family of theories
(\mathbb{T}_{n})_{n\in L}
presenting
\mathbb{T}
in the way considered previously (with
(L,\leq)
a well-ordered set, etc.), and, analogously, choose a family
(\mathbb{S}_{m})_{m\in K}
presenting
\mathbb{S}
.

To conclude that
D(\mathbb{S}_{m})\times D(\mathbb{T}_{n})\rightarrow D(\mathbb{S}_{m}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{n})
is an isomorphism – call this statement
P(m,n)
– for all
m\in K
,
n\in L
, as the poset
K\times L
is well-founded it suffices to verify the following: for all
(m,n)\in K\times L
, if
P(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
holds for all
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})<(m,n)
, then
P(m,n)
holds. We consider two cases:

•

m
and
n
are successor elements, say of
m^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime}
, respectively. Since, by assumption,
P(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
,
P(m,n^{\prime})
and
P(m^{\prime},n)
hold, it follows from the above discussion that so does
P(m,n)
.

•

m
or
n
is a limit element. As
\mathbb{S}_{m}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{n}=\bigcup_{(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})<(m,n)}\mathbb{S}_{m^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{n^{\prime}}
, the desired functor
D(\mathbb{S}_{m})\times D(\mathbb{T}_{n})\rightarrow D(\mathbb{S}_{m}\otimes\mathbb{S}_{n})
is an isomorphism, being the colimit

\underrightarrow{\text{lim }}_{(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})<(m,n)}D(\mathbb{S}_{m^{\prime}})\times D(\mathbb{T}_{n^{\prime}})\longrightarrow\underrightarrow{\text{lim }}_{(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})<(m,n)}D(\mathbb{S}_{m^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{n^{\prime}})

of the isomorphisms given by the induction hypothesis. (Details have been omitted.)

3.4
The theory of morphisms vs the theory of displayed structures

Let
\mathbb{I}
be the gat given by the axioms

\displaystyle\vdash S

(‘source” sort)

\displaystyle\vdash T\;\mathsf{sort}

(‘target” sort)

\displaystyle x:S

\displaystyle\vdash\varphi(x):T

(“arrow” term)

A
Fam
-model of
\mathbb{I}
is a triple consisting of small sets
X
,
Y
, and a function
f:X\rightarrow Y
. On the other hand, let
\mathbb{E}
be the gat given by a single dependent sort over a “base” constant sort:

\displaystyle\vdash B

\displaystyle x:B

\displaystyle\vdash E(x)

A
Fam
-model of
\mathbb{E}
consists of a small set
X
and, for each
x\in X
, a small set
Y(x)
.

Given a theory
\mathbb{A}
, we define its
theory of morphisms
as
\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{A}
, and its
theory of displayed structures
as
\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{A}
. They will not be studied in the present paper, in particular since the machinery from
[
4
]
will be more appropriate for discussing their semantics. For the moment, we will calculate them in the particular case where
\mathbb{A}
is the theory
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
from §
3.1
.

3.4.1
Calculating
\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}

For
\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
, we start by observing that tensoring
\vdash S\;\mathsf{sort}
and
\vdash T\;\mathsf{sort}
with the axioms of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
has the effect of creating two disjoint copies of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
. We will represent them by adding a subscript
S
or
T
to each sort/term symbol: up to variable renaming, we will have axioms

\displaystyle\vdash O_{S}\;\mathsf{sort}

(sort of objects – source)

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle x,y:O_{S},f:A_{S}(x,y)

\displaystyle\vdash\circ_{S}(x,y,y,f,id_{S}(y))\equiv f:A_{S}(x,y)

(right unitality – source)

\displaystyle\vdash O_{T}\;\mathsf{sort}

(sort of objects – target)

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle x,y:O_{T},f:A_{T}(x,y)

\displaystyle\vdash\circ_{T}(x,y,y,f,id_{T}(y))\equiv f:A_{T}(x,y)

(right unitality – target)

We now tensor, if applicable,
x:S\vdash\varphi(x):T
with each axiom of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
. Writing
\varphi_{O}
for
\varphi O
and
\varphi_{A}
for
\varphi A
, we have

\displaystyle x:O_{S}\vdash\varphi_{O}(x):O_{T}

\displaystyle x,y:O_{S},f:A_{S}(x,y)\vdash\varphi_{A}(x,y,f):A_{T}(\varphi_{O}(x),\varphi_{O}(x))

\displaystyle x,y,z:O_{S},f:A_{S}(x,y),g:A_{S}(y,z)\vdash

\displaystyle\circ_{T}(\varphi_{O}(x),\varphi_{O}(y),\varphi_{O}(z),\varphi_{A}(x,y,f),\varphi_{A}(y,z,g))\equiv\varphi_{A}(x,z,\circ_{S}(x,y,z,f,g)):A_{T}(\varphi_{O}(x),\varphi_{O}(z))

\displaystyle x:O_{S}\vdash id_{T}(\varphi_{O}(x))\equiv\varphi_{A}(x,x,id_{S}(x)):A_{T}(\varphi_{O}(x),\varphi_{O}(x))

These can be expressed in the informal syntax as

\displaystyle x:O_{S}

\displaystyle\vdash\varphi_{O}(x):O_{T}

\displaystyle x,y:O_{S},f:A_{S}(x,y)

\displaystyle\vdash\varphi_{A}(f):A_{T}(\varphi_{O}(x),\varphi_{O}(y))

\displaystyle x,y,z:O_{S},f:A_{S}(x,y),g:A_{S}(y,z)

\displaystyle\vdash\varphi_{A}(f)\circ_{T}\varphi_{A}(g)\equiv\varphi_{A}(f\circ_{S}g):A_{T}(\varphi_{O}(x),\varphi_{O}(z))

\displaystyle x:O_{S}

\displaystyle\vdash id_{T}(\varphi_{O}(x))\equiv\varphi_{A}(id_{S}(x)):A_{T}(\varphi_{O}(x),\varphi_{O}(x)).

We thus recognize
\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
as a gat whose
Fam
-models are triples consisting of small categories (
Fam
-models of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
)
X
,
Y
, and a functor
f:X\rightarrow Y
.

Remark 3.4
.

\mathbb{I}
plays the role of the poset category
\{0\rightarrow 1\}
. More generally, any category
K
induces, as in Remark
3.2
, a gat
\mathbb{K}
, from which we obtain for each
\mathbb{A}
a theory
\mathbb{K}\otimes\mathbb{A}
encoding diagrams of shape
K
in
\text{Mod}(\mathbb{A})
.

3.4.2
Calculating
\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}

Tensoring
\vdash B\;\mathsf{sort}
with the axioms of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
yields a copy of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
. We represent it by adding the subscript
B
to each sort/term symbol:

\displaystyle\vdash O_{B}\;\mathsf{sort}

(sort of objects – base)

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle x,y:O_{B},f:A_{B}(x,y)

\displaystyle\vdash\circ_{B}(x,y,y,f,id_{B}(y))\equiv f:A_{B}(x,y)

(right unitality – base)

Now, by tensoring
x:B\vdash E(x)\;\mathsf{sort}
with the axioms of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
we obtain, in the informal syntax,

\displaystyle x:O_{B}\vdash O_{E}(x)\;\mathsf{sort}

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}[l]x:O_{B}&y:O_{B}&f:A_{B}(x,y)\\
x^{\prime}:O_{E}(x)&y^{\prime}:O_{E}(y)&-\end{pmatrix}\vdash A_{E}(f,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\;\mathsf{sort}

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}[l]x:O_{B}&y:O_{B}&z:O_{B}&f:A_{B}(x,y)&g:A_{B}(y,z)\\
x^{\prime}:O_{E}(x)&y^{\prime}:O_{E}(y)&z^{\prime}:O_{E}(z)&f^{\prime}:A_{E}(f,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})&g^{\prime}:A_{E}(g^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}\vdash f^{\prime}\circ_{E}g^{\prime}:A_{E}(f\circ_{B}g,x^{\prime},z^{\prime})

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}[l]x:O_{B}\\
x^{\prime}:O_{E}(x)\end{pmatrix}\vdash id_{E}(x^{\prime}):A_{E}(id_{B}(x),x^{\prime},x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}[l]x:O_{B}&y:O_{B}&z:O_{B}&w:O_{B}&f:A_{B}(x,y)&g:A_{B}(y,z)&h:A_{B}(z,w)\\
x^{\prime}:O_{E}(x)&y^{\prime}:O_{E}(y)&z^{\prime}:O_{E}(z)&w^{\prime}:O_{E}(w)&f^{\prime}:A_{E}(f,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})&g^{\prime}:A_{E}(g,y^{\prime},z^{\prime})&h^{\prime}:A_{E}(h,z^{\prime},w^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\vdash f^{\prime}\circ_{E}(g^{\prime}\circ_{E}h^{\prime})\equiv(f^{\prime}\circ_{E}g^{\prime})\circ_{E}h^{\prime}:A_{E}(f\circ_{B}(g\circ_{B}h),x^{\prime},w^{\prime})

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}[l]x:O_{B}&y:O_{B}&f:A_{B}(x,y)\\
x^{\prime}:O_{E}(x)&y^{\prime}:O_{E}(y)&f^{\prime}:A_{E}(f,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}\vdash id_{E}(x^{\prime})\circ_{E}f^{\prime}\equiv f^{\prime}:A_{E}(id_{B}(x)\circ_{B}f,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}[l]x:O_{B}&y:O_{B}&f:A_{B}(x,y)\\
x^{\prime}:O_{E}(x)&y^{\prime}:O_{E}(y)&f^{\prime}:A_{E}(f,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}\vdash f^{\prime}\circ_{E}id_{E}(y^{\prime})\equiv f^{\prime}:A_{E}(f\circ_{B}id_{B}(y),x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

These correspond precisely to the axioms for a
displayed category
in the sense of Ahrens–Lumsdaine (
[
3
]
): a model of
\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
is a pair consisting of a “base” small category
X
(a model of the copy of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
corresponding to the sort
B
) and a category
Y
whose data is split into small sets suitably indexed by configurations of objects and arrows in
X
. Up to isomorphism, this amounts to an arbitrary functor
Y\rightarrow X
. Compare with the correspondence, due to Bénabou (see
[
7
]
), between functors
Y\rightarrow X
and normal lax functors from
X
to the bicategory of distributors.

However,
\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
and
\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat}
induce distinct structures on their categories of models. As mentioned in the introduction, the display maps of a contextual category
\mathcal{A}
generate, via the Yoneda embedding and the small object argument, a weak factorization on
\text{Mod}(\mathcal{A})
.

In
\text{Mod}(\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat})
, the cofibrations, i.e. the arrows in the left class of the wfs, are generated by the commutative squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

viewed as a morphism
(i,j):f\rightarrow f^{\prime}
, where (1)
i=id_{X}
and
j
freely adds an object to
Y
, or (2)
i=id_{X}
and
j
freely adds an arrow to
Y
, or (3)
i
freely adds an object to
X
and the square is a pushout, or (4)
i
freely adds an arrow to
X
and the square is a pushout.

On the other hand, in
\text{Mod}(\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{T}_{cat})
, the cofibrations are generated by the squares as above where (1)
i=id_{X}
and
j
freely adds an object to
Y
, or (2)
i=id_{X}
and
j
freely adds an arrow to
Y
, or (3)
j=id_{Y}
and
i
freely adds an object that is sent by
f^{\prime}
to a specified object of
Y^{\prime}
, or (4)
j=id_{Y}
and
i
freely adds an arrow that is sent by
f^{\prime}
to a specified arrow in
Y^{\prime}
.

We refer the reader to
[
23
]
, particularly §3.9 (which uses a variant of
\mathbb{T}_{cat}
), for a discussion of homotopy-theoretic implications of this distinction.

4
Some consequences of
h
-derivability

Recall from Definition
2.10
that a pair of gats
(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
is
h
-derivable
, where
h\geq 0
, if
J\odot J^{\prime}
is derivable in the pretheory
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
whenever
J
,
J^{\prime}
are derivable judgments in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, resp., such that
\textsf{ht}(J)\textsf{ht}(J^{\prime})\leq h
.

In this section, we will prove several consequences of
(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
being
h
-derivable. Roughly, some of them mean that, under certain numerical constraints on the heights of the structures involved, tensoring expressions/contexts is compatible with equality or substitution in each of the two arguments separately. Others allow us to construct contexts or morphisms in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
by tensoring contexts or morphisms in
\mathbb{A}
with ones in
\mathbb{B}
. They are instances of the general results from
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec: comparison functor}
.

We will use Proposition
A.15
, which lists some of the basic properties of the height function for judgments, contexts, etc., several times without explicit reference. We believe doing that will not create confusion as that proposition contains (by design) all of the properties of the height function that will be referred to throughout the text.

We start by introducing some notation:

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
be gats. Suppose given context morphisms

\displaystyle\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{M}):\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\textbf{A}=(a_{1}:A_{1},...,a_{M}:A_{M}),

\displaystyle\textbf{g}=(g_{1},...,g_{N}):\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\textbf{B}=(b_{1}:B_{1},...,b_{N}:B_{N})

in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, respectively. Recall that
\textbf{A}\otimes\textbf{B}
is the length-
mn
sequence obtained by lexicographically reindexing the family
(x_{i}y_{j}:X_{i}\otimes Y_{j})_{(i,j)\in\{1,...,M\}\times\{1,...,N\}}
. Similarly, using the notation from
2.4
, we let

\displaystyle\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{B}

\displaystyle=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{M})\otimes(b_{1},\ldots,b_{N})

\displaystyle=(f_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(f_{1})}\otimes b_{1}^{B_{1}},\ldots,f_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(f_{1})}\otimes b_{N}^{B_{N}},\ldots,f_{M}^{\mathcal{S}(f_{M})}\otimes b_{1}^{B_{1}},\ldots,f_{M}^{\mathcal{S}(f_{M})}\otimes b_{N}^{B_{N}}),

\displaystyle\textbf{A}\otimes\textbf{g}

\displaystyle=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{M})\otimes(g_{1},\ldots,g_{N})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}^{A_{1}}\otimes g_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(g_{1})},\ldots,a_{1}^{A_{1}}\otimes g_{N}^{\mathcal{S}(g_{N})},\ldots,a_{M}^{A_{M}}\otimes g_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(g_{1})},\ldots,a_{M}^{A_{M}}\otimes g_{N}^{\mathcal{S}(g_{N})}),

\displaystyle\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{g}

\displaystyle=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{M})\otimes(g_{1},\ldots,g_{N})

\displaystyle=(f_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(f_{1})}\otimes g_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(g_{1})},\ldots,f_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(f_{1})}\otimes g_{N}^{\mathcal{S}(g_{N})},\ldots,f_{M}^{\mathcal{S}(f_{M})}\otimes g_{1}^{\mathcal{S}(g_{1})},\ldots,f_{M}^{\mathcal{S}(f_{M})}\otimes g_{N}^{\mathcal{S}(g_{N})}).

4.1
First set of statements

Consider a pair of gats
(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
and a natural number
k
. Below, when we talk without further qualification about a derivable judgment, a context, or a context morphism, we mean so in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, or the pretheory
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
.

We will consider the following statements:

Cont(k)

For all contexts
X
in
\mathbb{A}
and
Y
in
\mathbb{B}
, if
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})=k
, then
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
is a context.

Sub^{1}_{t,t}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a context morphism
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
and a derivable judgment
\textbf{A}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}
in
\mathbb{A}
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a derivable judgment
\textbf{Y}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term}
.

If
{\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})}
,
{\textsf{ht}(\textbf{A}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})\leq k}
, then the following is derivable:

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash(u\otimes v)[\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}]\equiv u[\textbf{f}]\otimes v\;\mathsf{term}.

Sub^{1}_{t,s}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a context morphism
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
and a derivable judgment
\textbf{A}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a derivable judgment
\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
. Let
\textbf{Y}^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y},y:V)
.

If
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{A}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort})\leq k
, then the following is derivable:

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}\vdash(u\otimes y)[\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}]\equiv u[\textbf{f}]\otimes y\;\mathsf{term}.

Sub^{1}_{s,t}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a context morphism
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
and a derivable judgment
\textbf{A}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a derivable judgment
\textbf{Y}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term}
. Let
\textbf{A}^{\prime}=(\textbf{A},a:U)
,
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x:U[\textbf{f}])
, and
\textbf{f}^{\prime}=(\textbf{f},x):\textbf{X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\textbf{A}^{\prime}
.

If
{\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})}
,
{\textsf{ht}(\textbf{A}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})}
,
{\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U[\textbf{f}])\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})\leq k}
, then the following is derivable:

\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash(a\otimes v)[\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}]\equiv x\otimes v\;\mathsf{term}.

Sub^{1}_{s,s}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a context morphism
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
and a derivable judgment
\textbf{A}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a derivable judgment
\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
. Let
{\textbf{A}^{\prime}=(\textbf{A},a:U)}
,
{\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x:U[\textbf{f}])}
,
{\textbf{f}^{\prime}=(\textbf{f},x):\textbf{X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\textbf{A}^{\prime}}
, and
{\textbf{Y}^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y},y:V)}
.

If
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{A}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U[\textbf{f}]\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})\leq k
, then the following is derivable:

\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})\vdash(U\otimes V)[\partial(\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})]\equiv U[\textbf{f}]\otimes V\;\mathsf{sort}.

Sub^{2}_{t,t}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a derivable judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a context morphism
\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B}
and a derivable judgment
\textbf{B}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term}
.

If
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{B}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})\leq k
, then the following is derivable:

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash(u\otimes v)[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{g}]\equiv u\otimes v[\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{term}.

Sub^{2}_{t,s}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a derivable judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a context morphism
\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B}
and a derivable judgment
\textbf{B}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
. Let
\textbf{B}^{\prime}=(\textbf{B},b:V)
,
\textbf{Y}^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y},y:V[\textbf{g}])
, and
\textbf{g}^{\prime}=(\textbf{g},y):\textbf{Y}^{\prime}\rightarrow\textbf{B}^{\prime}
.

If
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{B}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash V[\textbf{g}])\leq k
, then the following is derivable:

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}\vdash(u\otimes b)[\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{g}^{\prime}]\equiv u\otimes y\;\mathsf{term}.

Sub^{2}_{s,t}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a derivable judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a context morphism
\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B}
and a derivable judgment
\textbf{B}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term}
. Let
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x:U)
.

If
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{B}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})\leq k
, then the following is derivable:

\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash(x\otimes v)[\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{g}]\equiv x\otimes v[\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{term}.

Sub^{2}_{s,s}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a derivable judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a context morphism
\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B}
and a derivable judgment
\textbf{B}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
. Let
\textbf{B}^{\prime}=(\textbf{B},b:V)
,
\textbf{Y}^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y},y:V[\textbf{g}])
,
\textbf{g}^{\prime}=(\textbf{g},y):\textbf{Y}^{\prime}\rightarrow\textbf{B}^{\prime}
, and
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x:U)
.

If
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{B}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash V[\textbf{g}])\leq k
, then the following is derivable:

\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}^{\prime})\vdash(U\otimes V)[\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{g}^{\prime})]\equiv U\otimes V[\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{sort}.

Mor^{1}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a context morphism
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a context
Y
.

If
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})\leq k
, then the following is derivable:

\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}:\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\longrightarrow\textbf{A}\otimes\textbf{Y}.

Mor^{1}_{+}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a context morphism
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
and a derivable judgment
\textbf{A}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a context
Y
. Let
\textbf{A}^{\prime}=(\textbf{A},a:U)
,
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x:U[\textbf{f}])
, and
\textbf{f}^{\prime}=(\textbf{f},x):\textbf{X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\textbf{A}^{\prime}
.

If
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{A}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})\leq k-1
and
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U[\textbf{f}]\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})\leq k
, then the following is derivable:

\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}:\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}\longrightarrow\textbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}.

Mor^{2}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a context
X
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a context morphism
\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B}
.

If
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B})\leq k
, then the following is derivable:

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\longrightarrow\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{B}.

Mor^{2}_{+}(k)

Suppose given in
\mathbb{A}
a context
X
, and in
\mathbb{B}
a context morphism
\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B}
and a derivable judgment
\textbf{B}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
. Let
\textbf{B}^{\prime}=(\textbf{B},b:V)
,
\textbf{Y}^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y},y:V[\textbf{g}])
, and
\textbf{g}^{\prime}=(\textbf{g},y):\textbf{Y}^{\prime}\rightarrow\textbf{B}^{\prime}
.

If
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{B}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort})\leq k-1
and
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash V[\textbf{g}])\leq k
, then the following is derivable:

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{g}^{\prime}:\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{B}^{\prime}.

In what follows, we fix theories
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, a natural number
h
, and work under the following assumptions on
(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
:

•

it is
h
-derivable;

•

for every
k<h
, it satisfies all of the above statements with parameter
k
, i.e.
Cont(k)
, …,
Mor_{+}^{2}(k)
.

Using these, we will prove

Proposition 4.2
.

(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
satisfies
\text{Cont}(h)
, …,
Mor^{2}+(h)
.

Proof of
Cont(h)

Suppose given contexts
X
in
\mathbb{A}
and
Y
in
\mathbb{B}
such that
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})=h
. Write
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},\ldots,x_{m}:X_{m})
and
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:Y_{n})
. We have
\textsf{ht}(\partial\textbf{X}\vdash X_{m}\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\partial\textbf{Y}\vdash Y_{n}\;\mathsf{sort})=h
, so by
h
-derivability we can derive
\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})\vdash X_{m}\otimes Y_{n}\;\mathsf{sort}
. It follows that
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}=(\partial(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}),x_{m}y_{n}:X_{m}\otimes Y_{n})
is a context.

Proof of
Sub^{1}_{t,t}(h)

Suppose given a context morphism

\textbf{f}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{M}):\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},\ldots,x_{m}:X_{m})\longrightarrow\textbf{A}=(a_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,a_{M}:A_{M})

in
\mathbb{A}
, a context
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}:Y_{n})
in
\mathbb{B}
, and derivable judgments
\textbf{A}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}
,
\textbf{Y}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term}
. Assume that
\textbf{H}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})
and
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{A}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})
are at most
h
.

Note that
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})<h
, so by
Mor^{1}(h-1)
we have a morphism
\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}:\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{A}\otimes\textbf{Y}
.

In what follows, if
e
is an expression in the alphabet of
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
, we will write
\overline{e}
for
e[\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}]=e[f_{i}\otimes y_{j}\mid a_{i}y_{j}]_{i\leq M,j\leq n}
. Similarly, if
e
is an expression in the alphabet of
\mathbb{A}
, we write
\underline{e}
for
e[\textbf{f}]=e[f_{i}\mid a_{i}]_{i\leq M}
. Thus our goal is to derive the equality

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash\overline{u\otimes v}\equiv\underline{u}\otimes v\;\mathsf{term}.

We will prove by induction on
P\in\{0,\ldots,h\}
that this holds whenever
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{A}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})=P
. Let
0\leq P\leq h
be such that the claim holds for all
P^{\prime}<P
, and suppose that
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{A}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})=P
.
13
13

13

Note that we do not verify a base case separately. In fact, for each
P
the proof is done unconditionally for certain
u
and
v

-
namely, when both are variables.

Consider contexts
\textbf{A}^{\prime}=(\textbf{A},a^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}(u))
and
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}(\underline{u}))=(\textbf{X},x^{\prime}:\underline{\mathcal{S}(u)})
. We have the following cases:

•

u
is a variable
a_{I}
and
v
is a variable
y_{J}
. Then
\overline{u\otimes v}
and
\underline{u}\otimes v
are both
f_{I}\otimes y_{J}
.

•

u
is a variable
a_{I}
and
v=t(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l})
. Express
A_{I}
as
S(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p})
. Then
\underline{A_{I}}=S(\underline{\sigma_{1}},\ldots,\underline{\sigma_{p}})
and

\textbf{X}\vdash\mathcal{S}(f_{I})\equiv\underline{A_{I}}\;\mathsf{sort}

is derivable. Denoting this judgment by
J
, we have

\textsf{ht}(J)\leq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{I}:\underline{A_{I}})\leq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}).

Then
\textsf{ht}(J)\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:\mathcal{S}(v))\leq h
, so by tensoring
J
with
\textbf{Y}\vdash v:\mathcal{S}(v)
we derive an equality

\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash x^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}(f_{I})}\otimes v\equiv x^{{}^{\prime}\underline{A_{I}}}\otimes v\;\mathsf{term}.

Note that
f_{I}\otimes v
is, by definition,

(x^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}(f_{I})}\otimes v)[f_{I}\otimes y_{1}\mid x^{\prime}y_{1},\ldots,f_{I}\otimes y_{n}\mid x^{\prime}y_{n}],

which is then provably equal in context
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
to

(x^{{}^{\prime}\underline{A_{I}}}\otimes v)[f_{I}\otimes y_{1}\mid x^{\prime}y_{1},\ldots,f_{I}\otimes y_{n}\mid x^{\prime}y_{n}].

Here we have used substitution along the context morphism
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},f_{I})\otimes\textbf{Y}:\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}
.

Expanding (
*
) we obtain

St((\underline{\sigma_{1}},\ldots,\underline{\sigma_{p}},x^{{}^{\prime}\underline{A_{I}}})\otimes(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l}))[f_{I}\otimes y_{1}\mid x^{\prime}y_{1},\ldots,f_{I}\otimes y_{n}\mid x^{\prime}y_{n}].

On the other hand,
\overline{a_{I}\otimes v}
is

\overline{St((\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p},a_{I})\otimes(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l}))},

which, by the induction hypothesis, is provably equal in context
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
to

St((\underline{\sigma_{1}},\ldots,\underline{\sigma_{p}},f_{I})\otimes(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l})).

To derive an equality between (1) and (2) in context
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
, it suffices
-
noting that
x^{\prime}
does not occur in
\underline{\sigma_{1}}
, …,
\underline{\sigma_{p}}

-
to derive for
j=1
, …,
l
an equality between
f_{I}\otimes t_{j}
and

(x^{{}^{\prime}\underline{A_{I}}}\otimes t_{j})[f_{I}\otimes y_{1}\mid x^{\prime}y_{1},\ldots,f_{I}\otimes y_{n}\mid x^{\prime}y_{n}].

Since, by definition,
f_{I}\otimes t_{j}
is
(x^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}(f_{I})}\otimes t_{j})[f_{I}\otimes y_{1}\mid x^{\prime}y_{1},\ldots,f_{I}\otimes y_{n}\mid x^{\prime}y_{n}]
, the desired equality follows from

\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash x^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}(f_{I})}\otimes t_{j}\equiv x^{{}^{\prime}\underline{A_{I}}}\otimes t_{j}\;\mathsf{term}

being derivable.

•

u=s(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})
and
v
is a variable
y_{J}
. Express
Y_{J}
as
T(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{q})
. Then
u\otimes v
is
sT((s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\otimes(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{q},y_{J}))
, so
\overline{u\otimes v}
is provably equal in context
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
, by the induction hypothesis, to

sT((\underline{s_{1}},\ldots,\underline{s_{k}})\otimes(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{q},y_{J})).

But the latter is precisely
\underline{u}\otimes v
.

•

u=s(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})
and
v=t(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l})
. Then
u\otimes v
is
(a^{\prime}\otimes v)[u\otimes y_{1}\mid a^{\prime}y_{1},\ldots,u\otimes y_{n}\mid a^{\prime}y_{n}]
, so

\overline{u\otimes v}=(a^{\prime}\otimes v)[u\otimes y_{1}\mid a^{\prime}y_{1},\ldots,u\otimes y_{n}\mid a^{\prime}y_{n}][f_{1}\otimes y_{1}\mid a_{1}y_{1},\ldots,f_{M}\otimes y_{n}\mid a_{m}y_{n}].

Since the variables
a^{\prime}y_{1}
, …,
a^{\prime}y_{n}
do not occur in
f_{1}\otimes y_{1}
, …,
f_{M}\otimes y_{n}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\overline{u\otimes v}

\displaystyle=(a^{\prime}\otimes v)[f_{i}\otimes y_{j}\mid a_{i}y_{j}]_{i\leq M,j\leq n}[\overline{u\otimes y_{j}}\mid a^{\prime}y_{j}]_{j\leq n}

\displaystyle=\overline{a^{\prime}\otimes v}[\overline{u\otimes y_{j}}\mid a^{\prime}y_{j}]_{j\leq n}.

We will verify that this expression is provably equal in context
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
to
\underline{u}\otimes v
.

Expressing
\mathcal{S}(u)
as
S(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p})
, we have that
a^{\prime}\otimes v
is
St((\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p},a^{\prime})\otimes(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l}))
. Hence

\overline{a^{\prime}\otimes v}=St(\overline{\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{1}},\ldots,\overline{\sigma_{p}\otimes t_{l}},\overline{a^{\prime}\otimes t_{1}},\ldots,\overline{a^{\prime}\otimes t_{l}})

and, as
a^{\prime}
does not occur in
\sigma_{1}
, …,
\sigma_{p}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{u\otimes v}

\displaystyle=\overline{a^{\prime}\otimes v}[\overline{u\otimes y_{j}}\mid a^{\prime}y_{j}]_{j\leq n}

\displaystyle=St(\overline{\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{1}},\ldots,\overline{\sigma_{p}\otimes t_{l}},\overline{a^{\prime}\otimes t_{1}}[\overline{u\otimes y_{j}}\mid a^{\prime}y_{j}]_{j\leq n},\ldots,\overline{a^{\prime}\otimes t_{l}}[\overline{u\otimes y_{j}}\mid a^{\prime}y_{j}]_{j\leq n})

\displaystyle=St(\overline{\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{1}},\ldots,\overline{\sigma_{p}\otimes t_{l}},\overline{u\otimes t_{1}},\ldots,\overline{u\otimes t_{l}}).

By the induction hypothesis, the latter is provably equal in context
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
to

St(\underline{\sigma_{1}}\otimes t_{1},\ldots,\underline{\sigma_{p}}\otimes t_{l},\underline{u}\otimes t_{1},\ldots,\underline{u}\otimes t_{l})=\underline{u}\otimes v.

This concludes the proof.

Proof of
Sub^{2}_{t,t}(h)

Suppose given a context
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},\ldots,x_{m}:X_{m})
in
\mathbb{A}
, a context morphism

\textbf{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{N}):\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}:Y_{n})\rightarrow\textbf{B}=(b_{1}:B_{1},\ldots,b_{N}:B_{N})

in
\mathbb{B}
, and derivable judgments
\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}
,
\textbf{B}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term}
. Assume that
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B})
and
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{B}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})
are at most
h
.

Note that
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B})<h
, so by
Mor^{2}(h-1)
we have a context morphism
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{B}
.

Our goal, in notation analogous to the one used above, is to derive the equality

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash\overline{u\otimes v}\equiv u\otimes\underline{v}\;\mathsf{term}.

We will prove by induction on
P\in\{0,\ldots,h\}
that this holds whenever
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{B}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})=P
. Let
0\leq P\leq h
be such that the claim holds for all
P^{\prime}<P
, and suppose that
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{B}\vdash v\;\mathsf{term})=P
.

Consider contexts
\textbf{B}^{\prime}=(\textbf{B},b^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}(v))
and
\textbf{Y}^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y},y^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}(\underline{v}))=(\textbf{Y},y^{\prime}:\underline{\mathcal{S}(v)})
. We have the following cases:

•

u
is a variable
x_{I}
and
v
is a variable
b_{J}
. Then
\overline{u\otimes v}
and
u\otimes\underline{v}
are both
x_{I}\otimes g_{J}
.

•

u
is a variable
x_{I}
and
v=t(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l})
. Express
X_{I}
as
S(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p})
. Then
u\otimes v
is
St((\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p},x_{I})\otimes(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l}))
, so
\overline{u\otimes v}
is provably equal in context
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
, by the induction hypothesis, to

St((\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p},x_{I})\otimes(\underline{t_{1}},\ldots,\underline{t_{l}})).

But the latter is precisely
u\otimes\underline{v}
.

•

u=s(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})
and
v
is a variable
b_{J}
. Express
B_{J}
as
T(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{q})
. Then
\underline{B_{J}}=T(\underline{\tau_{1}},\ldots,\underline{\tau_{q}})
and

\textbf{Y}\vdash\mathcal{S}(g_{J})\equiv\underline{B_{J}}\;\mathsf{sort}

is derivable. Denoting this judgment by
J
, we have

\textsf{ht}(J)\leq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash g_{J}:\underline{B_{J}})\leq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B}).

Then
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:\mathcal{S}(u))\textsf{ht}(J)\leq h
, so by tensoring
\textbf{X}\vdash u:\mathcal{S}(u)
with
J
we derive an equality

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y'}\vdash u\otimes y^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}(g_{J})}\equiv u\otimes y^{{}^{\prime}\underline{B_{J}}}\;\mathsf{term}.

Also, note that since

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash g_{J}\;\mathsf{term})\leq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{B})\leq h,

by
h
-derivability we can tensor
\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}
and
\textbf{Y}\vdash g_{J}\;\mathsf{term}
to derive

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash u\otimes g_{J}\equiv(u\otimes y^{{}^{\prime}\mathcal{S}(g_{J})})[x_{1}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{1}y^{\prime},\ldots,x_{m}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{m}y^{\prime}]\;\mathsf{term}.

Now, by using (
*
) it follows that

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash u\otimes g_{J}\equiv(u\otimes y^{{}^{\prime}\underline{B_{J}}})[x_{1}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{1}y^{\prime},\ldots,x_{m}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{m}y^{\prime}]\;\mathsf{term}

is derivable. In this step, we have used substitution along the context morphism
\textbf{X}\otimes(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},g_{J}):\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime}
.

We will verify that the expression on the right-hand side of (
*
*
) is provably equal in context
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
to
\overline{u\otimes b_{J}}
.

On the one hand, we have

u\otimes y^{{}^{\prime}\underline{B_{J}}}=sT((s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\otimes(\underline{\tau_{1}},\ldots,\underline{\tau_{q}},y^{\prime})),

hence
(u\otimes y^{{}^{\prime}\underline{B_{J}}})[x_{1}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{1}y^{\prime},\ldots,x_{m}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{m}y^{\prime}]
equals

sT((s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\otimes(\underline{\tau_{1}},\ldots,\underline{\tau_{q}},y^{{}^{\prime}\underline{B_{J}}}))[x_{1}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{1}y^{\prime},\ldots,x_{m}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{m}y^{\prime}]

As
y
does not occur in
\tau_{1}
, …,
\tau_{1}
, this expression equals

sT\bigg(s_{1}\otimes\underline{\tau_{1}},\ldots,s_{1}\otimes\underline{\tau_{q}},(s_{1}\otimes y^{{}^{\prime}\underline{B_{J}}})[x_{1}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{1}y^{\prime},\ldots,x_{m}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{m}y^{\prime}],

\ldots,

s_{k}\otimes\underline{\tau_{1}},\ldots,s_{k}\otimes\underline{\tau_{q}},(s_{k}\otimes y^{{}^{\prime}\underline{B_{J}}})[x_{1}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{1}y^{\prime},\ldots,x_{m}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{m}y^{\prime}]\bigg).

On the other hand,

u\otimes b_{j}=sT((s_{1},...,s_{k})\otimes(\tau_{1},...,\tau_{q},b_{j})),

thus, by the induction hypothesis, the judgment

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash\overline{u\otimes b_{j}}\equiv sT((s_{1},...,s_{k})\otimes(\underline{\tau_{1}},...,\underline{\tau_{q}},g_{J}))\;\mathsf{term}

is derivable. It remains to prove that (
\blacklozenge
) is provably equal, in context
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
, to the expression
sT((s_{1},...,s_{k})\otimes(\underline{\tau_{1}},...,\underline{\tau_{q}},g_{J}))
as in (
\blacklozenge\blacklozenge
).

We conclude by noting that, by the induction hypothesis, the judgment

\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash(s_{i}\otimes y^{{}^{\prime}\underline{B_{J}}})[x_{1}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{1}y^{\prime},\ldots,x_{m}\otimes g_{J}\mid x_{m}y^{\prime}]\equiv s_{i}\otimes g_{J}

is derivable for
1\leq i\leq k
.

•

u=s(s_{1},...,s_{k})
and
v=t(t_{1},...,t_{l})
. Then

u\otimes v=(x^{\prime}\otimes v)[u\otimes b_{1}\mid x^{\prime}b_{1},...,u\otimes b_{N}\mid x^{\prime}b_{N}]

where we consider a context
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}(u))
. It follows that

\displaystyle\overline{u\otimes v}

\displaystyle=\overline{(x^{\prime}\otimes v)[u\otimes b_{j}\mid x^{\prime}b_{j}]_{j\leq N}}

\displaystyle=\overline{x^{\prime}\otimes v}[\overline{u\otimes b_{j}}\mid x^{\prime}b_{j}]_{j\leq N}.

Expressing
\mathcal{S}(u)
as
S(\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{p})
, we have

x^{\prime}\otimes v=St((\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{p},x^{\prime})\otimes(t_{1},...,t_{l})),

hence

\overline{x^{\prime}\otimes v}=St(\underline{\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{1}},...,\overline{\sigma_{p}\otimes t_{l}},\overline{x^{\prime}\otimes t_{1}},...,\overline{x^{\prime}\otimes t_{l}}).

Noting that
x^{\prime}
does not occur in
\sigma_{1}
, …,
\sigma_{p}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\overline{u\otimes v}

\displaystyle=\overline{x^{\prime}\otimes v}[\overline{u\otimes b_{j}}\mid x^{\prime}b_{j}]_{j\leq N}

\displaystyle=St(\overline{\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{1}},...,\overline{\sigma_{p}\otimes t_{l}},\overline{x^{\prime}\otimes t_{1}}[\overline{u\otimes b_{j}}\mid x^{\prime}b_{j}]_{j\leq N},...,\overline{x^{\prime}\otimes t_{l}}[\overline{u\otimes b_{j}}\mid x^{\prime}b_{j}]_{j\leq N})

\displaystyle=St(\overline{\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{1}},...,\overline{\sigma_{p}\otimes t_{l}},\overline{(x^{\prime}\otimes t_{1})[u\otimes b_{j}\mid x^{\prime}b_{j}]_{j\leq N}},...,\overline{(x^{\prime}\otimes t_{1})[u\otimes b_{j}\mid x^{\prime}b_{j}]_{j\leq N}})

\displaystyle=St(\overline{\sigma_{1}\otimes t_{1}},...,\overline{\sigma_{p}\otimes t_{l}},\overline{u\otimes t_{1}},...,\overline{u\otimes t_{l}}).

But by the induction hypothesis, the latter term expression is provably equal in context
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
to

\displaystyle St(\sigma_{1}\otimes\underline{t_{1}},...,\sigma_{p}\otimes\underline{t_{l}},u\otimes\underline{t_{1}},...,u\otimes\underline{t_{l}})

\displaystyle=St((\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{p},u)\otimes(\underline{t_{1}},...,\underline{t_{l}}))

\displaystyle=u\otimes\underline{v}.

Proof of
Mor^{1}_{+}(h)

Suppose given a morphism

\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{M}):\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m})\longrightarrow\textbf{A}=(a_{1}:A_{1},...,a_{M}:A_{M})

and a derivable judgment
\textbf{A}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
in
\mathbb{A}
, and a context
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
in
\mathbb{B}
. Assume that
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{A}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})\leq h-1
and
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U[\textbf{f}]\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})\leq h
. Consider contexts
\textbf{A}^{\prime}=(\textbf{A},a^{\prime}:U)
and
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x^{\prime}:U[\textbf{f}])
, and write
\textbf{f}^{\prime}
for the tuple
(f_{1},...,f_{M},x^{\prime})
. We must verify that

\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}:\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}\longrightarrow\textbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}

is a context morphism.

Firstly, note that as
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}^{\prime})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{A}^{\prime})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})\leq h
, we obtain from
Cont(h)
that
\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}
and
\textbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}
are contexts.

Moreover, by
Mor^{1}(h-1)
we have a context morphism
\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}:\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{A}\otimes\textbf{Y}
, thus also a morphism

\textbf{f}\otimes\textbf{Y}:\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{A}\otimes\textbf{Y}.

If
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})=0
, then the tuple
\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}
, which is empty, is the identity of the empty context
\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}
. Otherwise, we have
\textsf{ht}(\partial\textbf{Y})<\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})
, so we can use
Mor^{1}_{+}(h-1)
to derive
\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}:\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}
, hence

\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}:\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}\longrightarrow\textbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes\partial\textbf{Y}.

From (
*
) and (
**
) we obtain a morphism

\partial(\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}):\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}\longrightarrow\partial(\textbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}).

To conclude, using a double bar to indicate substitution along
\partial(\textbf{f}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y})
, let us verify that the judgment

\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash x^{\prime}y_{n}:\overline{\overline{U\otimes Y_{n}}}

is derivable. It suffices to prove that
