J\otimes_{t}(J^{\prime}\otimes_{t}J^{\prime\prime})=J\medblackcircle_{t}(J^{\prime}\otimes_{t}J^{\prime\prime}),\qquad J\otimes_{t}(J^{\prime}\medblackcircle_{t}J^{\prime\prime})=J\medblackcircle_{t}(J^{\prime}\medblackcircle_{t}J^{\prime\prime}),

so it suffices to derive
\big(
(E1) or (E3)
\big)
and
\big(
(E2) or (E4)
\big)
. Similarly, if
J^{\prime}
or
J^{\prime\prime}
is of that form, it suffices to derive
\big(
(E1) or (E2)
\big)
and
\big(
(E3) or (E4)
\big)
; if two among
J
,
J^{\prime}
,
J^{\prime\prime}
are of that form, then (E1)-(E4) are the same; and if the three judgments are of that form, then (E1)-(E4) are trivially derivable as they equal
xyz\equiv xyz
for variables
x
,
y
and
z
.

We will now outline the verification of the claim in some of these cases.

1st case
: suppose that
J
,
J^{\prime}
,
J^{\prime\prime}
are term judgments whose term expressions are not variables, say

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)=(\textbf{X}\vdash r(a_{1},...,a_{m}):R(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{M})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V)=(\textbf{Y}\vdash s(b_{1},...,b_{n}):S(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{N})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Z}\vdash w:W)=(\textbf{Z}\vdash t(c_{1},...,c_{p}):T(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{P})).

Then we consider the following equalities in context
(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})\otimes\textbf{Z}
:

(E1)

(u^{U}\otimes v^{V})\otimes w^{W}\equiv u^{U}\otimes(v^{V}\otimes w^{W})
.

Note that we can express it as

RS\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1}&\cdots&\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{N}&\alpha_{1}\medblackcircle v^{V}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\alpha_{M}\otimes\beta_{1}&\cdots&\alpha_{M}\otimes\beta_{N}&\alpha_{M}\medblackcircle v^{V}\\
u^{U}\otimes\beta_{1}&\cdots&u^{U}\otimes\beta_{N}&u^{U}\otimes v^{V}\end{pmatrix}\square t(c_{1},...,c_{p})\equiv R(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{M},u^{U})\square St\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{1}\otimes c_{1}&\cdots&\beta_{1}\otimes c_{p}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\beta_{N}\otimes c_{1}&\cdots&\beta_{N}\otimes c_{p}\\
v^{V}\otimes c_{1}&\cdots&v^{V}\otimes c_{p}\end{pmatrix}.

By the induction hypothesis, the left- and right-hand sides are entrywise provably equal, i.e. the following equalities are derivable in
(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})\otimes\textbf{Z}
:

\displaystyle(\alpha_{i}\otimes\beta_{j})\otimes c_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv\alpha_{i}\otimes(\beta_{j}\otimes c_{k})

(for all
i
,
j
,
k
)

\displaystyle(\alpha_{i}\medblackcircle v^{V})\otimes c_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv\alpha_{i}\otimes(v^{V}\otimes c_{k})

(for all
i
,
k
)

\displaystyle(u^{U}\otimes\beta_{j})\otimes c_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv u^{U}\otimes(\beta_{j}\otimes c_{k})

(for all
j
,
k
)

\displaystyle(u^{U}\otimes v^{V})\otimes c_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv u^{U}\otimes(v^{V}\otimes c_{k})

(for all
k
)

(E2)

(u^{U}\otimes v^{V})\medblackcircle w^{W}\equiv u^{U}\otimes(v^{V}\medblackcircle w^{W})
.

This can be expressed as

Rs\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}\otimes b_{1}&\cdots&\alpha_{1}\otimes b_{n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\alpha_{M}\otimes b_{1}&\cdots&\alpha_{M}\otimes b_{n}\\
u^{U}\otimes b_{1}&\cdots&u^{U}\otimes b_{n}\end{pmatrix}\medblacksquare T(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{P},w^{W})\equiv R(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{M},u^{U})\square sT\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}\otimes\gamma_{1}&\cdots&b_{1}\otimes\gamma_{P}&b_{1}\medblackcircle w^{W}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
b_{n}\otimes\gamma_{1}&\cdots&b_{n}\otimes\gamma_{P}&b_{n}\medblackcircle w^{W}\end{pmatrix}.

Similarly to the previous case, we conclude by using the induction hypothesis to derive the term equality corresponding to each of the entries:

\displaystyle(\alpha_{i}\otimes b_{j})\otimes\gamma_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv\alpha_{i}\otimes(b_{j}\otimes\gamma_{k})

(for all
i
,
j
,
k
)

\displaystyle(\alpha_{i}\otimes b_{j})\medblackcircle w^{W}

\displaystyle\equiv\alpha_{i}\otimes(b_{j}\medblackcircle w^{W})

(for all
i
,
j
)

\displaystyle(u^{U}\otimes b_{j})\otimes\gamma_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv u^{U}\otimes(b_{j}\otimes\gamma_{k})

(for all
j
,
k
)

\displaystyle(u^{U}\otimes b_{j})\medblackcircle w^{W}

\displaystyle\equiv u^{U}\otimes(b_{j}\medblackcircle w^{W})

(for all
j
)

(E4)

(u^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V})\medblackcircle w^{W}\equiv u^{U}\medblackcircle(v^{V}\medblackcircle w^{W})
.

We can express it as

rS\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}\otimes\beta_{1}&\cdots&a_{1}\otimes\beta_{N}&a_{1}\medblackcircle v^{V}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a_{m}\otimes\beta_{1}&\cdots&a_{m}\otimes\beta_{N}&a_{m}\medblackcircle v^{V}\end{pmatrix}\medblacksquare T(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{P},w^{W})\equiv r(a_{1},...,a_{m})\medblacksquare ST\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{1}\otimes\gamma_{1}&\cdots&\beta_{1}\otimes\gamma_{P}&\beta_{1}\medblackcircle w^{W}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\beta_{N}\otimes\gamma_{1}&\cdots&\beta_{N}\otimes\gamma_{P}&\beta_{N}\medblackcircle w^{W}\\
v^{V}\otimes\gamma_{1}&\cdots&v^{V}\otimes\gamma_{P}&v^{V}\medblackcircle w^{W}\end{pmatrix}.

Its derivability follows from that of:

\displaystyle(a_{i}\otimes\beta_{j})\otimes\gamma_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv a_{i}\otimes(\beta_{j}\otimes\gamma_{k})

(for all
i
,
j
,
k
)

\displaystyle(a_{i}\otimes\beta_{j})\medblackcircle w^{W}

\displaystyle\equiv a_{i}\otimes(\beta_{j}\medblackcircle w^{W})

(for all
i
,
j
)

\displaystyle(a_{i}\medblackcircle v^{V})\otimes\gamma_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv a_{i}\otimes(v^{V}\otimes\gamma_{k})

(for all
i
,
k
)

\displaystyle(a_{i}\medblackcircle v^{V})\medblackcircle w^{W}

\displaystyle\equiv a_{i}\medblackcircle(v^{V}\medblackcircle w^{W})

(for all
i
)

(E3)

(u^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V})\otimes w^{W}\equiv u^{U}\medblackcircle(v^{V}\otimes w^{W})
.

This case differs from the others: while the outer term symbol in the left expression is
RSt
, the one in the right expression is
rST
, so it is not sufficient to derive an entrywise equality. Instead, we start by observing that
u^{U}\medblackcircle(v^{V}\otimes w^{W})
equals

r(a_{1},...,a_{m})\medblacksquare ST\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{1}\otimes\gamma_{1}&\cdots&\beta_{1}\otimes\gamma_{P}&\beta_{1}\medblackcircle w^{W}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\beta_{N}\otimes\gamma_{1}&\cdots&\beta_{N}\otimes\gamma_{P}&\beta_{N}\medblackcircle w^{W}\\
v^{V}\otimes\gamma_{1}&\cdots&v^{V}\otimes\gamma_{P}&v^{V}\otimes w^{W}\end{pmatrix},

which is – by the induction hypothesis – entrywise provably equal to
u^{U}\medblackcircle(v^{V}\medblackcircle w^{W})
in
(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})\otimes\textbf{Z}
. The desired equality then follows from

u^{U}\medblackcircle(v^{V}\medblackcircle w^{W})\equiv(u^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V})\medblackcircle w^{W}\equiv(u^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V})\otimes w^{W}.

2nd case
: suppose that

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=(\textbf{X}\vdash x:U)=(\textbf{X}\vdash x:R(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{M})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V)=(\textbf{Y}\vdash s(b_{1},...,b_{n}):S(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{N})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Z}\vdash w:W)=(\textbf{Z}\vdash t(c_{1},...,c_{p}):T(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{P}))

where
x
is a variable occurring in
X
. By Remark
7.4
, it suffices to derive the following equalities in context
(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})\otimes\textbf{Z}
:

(E1)

(x^{U}\otimes v^{V})\otimes w^{W}\equiv x^{U}\otimes(v^{V}\otimes w^{W})
. The proof of its derivability is analogous to that of (E1) in the 1st case.

(E2)

(x^{U}\otimes v^{V})\medblackcircle w^{W}\equiv x^{U}\otimes(v^{V}\medblackcircle w^{W})
. Here, we argue as for (E2) in the 1st case.

A similar argument allows us to derive (E1) and (E2) when
J
is replaced by
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
.

3rd case
: suppose that

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)=(\textbf{X}\vdash r(a_{1},...,a_{m}):R(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{M})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Y}\vdash y:V)=(\textbf{Y}\vdash y:S(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{N})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Z}\vdash w:W)=(\textbf{Z}\vdash t(c_{1},...,c_{p}):T(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{P}))

where
y
is a variable occurring in
Y
. By Remark
7.4
, it suffices to derive the following equalities in
(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})\otimes\textbf{Z}
:

(E1)

(u^{U}\otimes y^{V})\otimes w^{W}\equiv u^{U}\otimes(y^{V}\otimes w^{W})
. Derived as (E1) in the 1st case.

(E4)

(u^{U}\medblackcircle y^{V})\medblackcircle w^{W}\equiv u^{U}\medblackcircle(y^{V}\medblackcircle w^{W})
. Derived as (E4) in the 1st case.

With a similar argument we can derive (E1) and (E4) when
J^{\prime}
is replaced by
\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
.

4th case
: suppose that

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)=(\textbf{X}\vdash r(a_{1},...,a_{m}):R(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{M})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V)=(\textbf{Y}\vdash s(b_{1},...,b_{n}):S(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{N})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Z}\vdash z:W)=(\textbf{Z}\vdash z:T(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{P}))

where
z
is a variable occurring in
Z
. By Remark
7.4
, it suffices to derive the following equalities in
(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})\otimes\textbf{Z}
:

(E2)

(u^{U}\otimes v^{V})\medblackcircle z^{W}\equiv u^{U}\otimes(v^{V}\medblackcircle z^{W})
. Derived as (E2) in the 1st case.

(E4)

(u^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V})\medblackcircle z^{W}\equiv u^{U}\medblackcircle(v^{V}\medblackcircle z^{W})
. Derived as (E4) in the 1st case.

With a similar argument we can derive (E2) and (E4) when
J^{\prime\prime}
is replaced by
\textbf{Z}\vdash W\;\mathsf{sort}
.

5th case
: suppose that

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=(\textbf{X}\vdash x:U)=(\textbf{X}\vdash x:R(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{M})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Y}\vdash y:V)=(\textbf{Y}\vdash y:S(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{N})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Z}\vdash w:W)=(\textbf{Z}\vdash t(c_{1},...,c_{p}):T(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{P}))

where
x
,
y
are variables in
X
,
Y
, respectively. By Remark
7.4
, it suffices to derive (E1) –
(x^{U}\otimes y^{V})\otimes w^{W}\equiv x^{U}\otimes(y^{V}\otimes w^{W})
– in context
(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})\otimes\textbf{Z}
. Here, we can argue as for (E1) in the 1st case.

We can proceed similarly when
J
or
J^{\prime}
is replaced by
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
or
\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
, respectively.

6th case
: suppose that

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=(\textbf{X}\vdash x:U)=(\textbf{X}\vdash x:R(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{M})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V)=(\textbf{Y}\vdash s(b_{1},...,b_{n}):S(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{N})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Z}\vdash z:W)=(\textbf{Z}\vdash z:T(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{P}))

where
x
,
z
are variables in
X
,
Z
, respectively. By Remark
7.4
, it suffices to derive (E2) –
(x^{U}\otimes y^{V})\medblackcircle w^{W}\equiv x^{U}\otimes(y^{V}\medblackcircle w^{W})
– in context
(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})\otimes\textbf{Z}
. For that, we can proceed as for (E2) in the 1st case.

We can use a similar argument when
J
or
J^{\prime\prime}
is replaced by
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
or
\textbf{Z}\vdash W\;\mathsf{sort}
, respectively.

7th case
: suppose that

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)=(\textbf{X}\vdash r(a_{1},...,a_{m}):R(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{M})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Y}\vdash y:V)=(\textbf{Y}\vdash y:S(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{N})),

\displaystyle J^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(\textbf{Z}\vdash z:W)=(\textbf{Z}\vdash z:T(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{P}))

where
y
,
z
are variables in
Y
,
Z
, respectively. By Remark
7.4
, it suffices to derive (E4) –
(x^{U}\medblackcircle y^{V})\medblackcircle w^{W}\equiv x^{U}\medblackcircle(y^{V}\medblackcircle w^{W})
– in context
(\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y})\otimes\textbf{Z}
. For that, we can proceed as for (E4) in the 1st case.

A similar argument can be used when
J^{\prime}
or
J^{\prime\prime}
is replaced by
\textbf{Y}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
or
\textbf{Z}\vdash W\;\mathsf{sort}
, respectively.

This concludes the sketch of proof that for any
J
,
J^{\prime}
,
J^{\prime\prime}
, the eight term expressions under consideration are derivable and provably equal to each other in the appropriate context.

Remark 7.5
.

It is natural to try use the isomorphisms
(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})\otimes\mathbb{C}\equiv\mathbb{A}\otimes(\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{C})
described in this section as components of an associator for a monoidal structure on
GAT
, the unit of which would be the theory
\mathbb{O}_{1}
having a single axiom
\vdash O\;\mathsf{sort}
. We defer this discussion to
[
4
]
since we have, at this point, little to no access to the functoriality of the tensor product.

8
Towards symmetry

We will now describe for generalized algebraic theories
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
an isomorphism
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A}
.

Firstly, observe that we have a canonical isomorphism between the alphabets
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
and
\Sigma(\mathbb{B})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{A})
given by swapping the entries of each element: a variable
xy
, a sort symbol
ST
, and term symbols
sT
and
St
in
\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})
correspond, respectively, to
yx
,
ST
,
St
and
sT
in
\Sigma(\mathbb{B})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{A})
. Note also that, because of the ad-hoc (and asymmetric) choice of lexicographic order in the definition of tensor expressions (§
2
), the “arity matrix” of (for example)
sT
in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
is transpose to the one of
Ts
in
\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A}
.

Precisely, we define a preinterpretation, in the sense of
[
11
]
, §12
15
15

15

However, since we allow different theories to have different sets of variables, we must modify the definition of a preinterpretation by requiring a choice of function between the respective sets of variables.
, of
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
in
\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A}
as follows:

•

A variable
xy
is sent to
yx
.

•

A sort symbol
ST
, say introduced as

ST\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}&x_{1}y^{\prime}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n}&x_{m}y^{\prime}\\
x^{\prime}y_{1}&\cdots&x^{\prime}y_{n}&-\end{pmatrix},

is sent to the expression

TS\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}x_{1}&\cdots&y_{1}x_{m}&y_{1}x^{\prime}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{n}x_{1}&\cdots&y_{n}x_{m}&y_{n}x^{\prime}\\
y^{\prime}x_{1}&\cdots&y^{\prime}x_{n}&-\end{pmatrix}.

•

A term symbol
sT
, say introduced as

sT\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}&x_{1}y^{\prime}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n}&x_{m}y^{\prime}\end{pmatrix},

is sent to the expression

Ts\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}x_{1}&\cdots&y_{1}x_{m}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
y_{n}x_{1}&\cdots&y_{n}x_{m}\\
y^{\prime}x_{1}&\cdots&y^{\prime}x_{m}\end{pmatrix}.

•

A term symbol
St
, say introduced as

St\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}y_{1}&\cdots&x_{1}y_{n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{m}y_{1}&\cdots&x_{m}y_{n}\\
x^{\prime}y_{1}&\cdots&x^{\prime}y_{n}\end{pmatrix},

is sent to the expression

tS\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}x_{1}&\cdots&y_{1}x_{m}&y_{1}x^{\prime}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{n}x_{1}&\cdots&y_{n}x_{m}&y_{n}x^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

Denote this preinterpretation by
I_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
. As explained in
[
11
]
, it defines by recursion a map
\hat{I}_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}:\Sigma(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\Sigma(\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A})
between the respective sets of derivable judgments. Similarly, by swapping the roles of
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
we obtain a preinterpretation
I_{\mathbb{B},\mathbb{A}}
of
\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A}
in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
, and it is immediate that
\hat{I}_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
and
\hat{I}_{\mathbb{B},\mathbb{A}}
are inverse to each other.

Hence as soon as we prove that
I_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
(and, analogously,
I_{\mathbb{B},\mathbb{A}}
) is an interpretation, it will be an isomorphism of gats.

Proposition 8.1
.

I_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
is an interpretation of
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
in
\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A}
.

We must verify that if
J
,
J^{\prime}
are axioms in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, respectively, then
\hat{I}_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}(J\odot J^{\prime})
is derivable in
\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A}
.

The reason why this is not clear is that, even after taking into account the modifications of symbols and their arities described above, the definition of tensor expressions from §
2.1
does not become symmetric in the two factors. Indeed, the operations
\otimes_{t}
and
\medblackcircle_{t}
do not play a symmetric role: whenever the recursion requires using a previously defined tensor expression, it will use, say,
u^{U}\otimes_{t}v^{V}
rather than
u^{U}\medblackcircle_{t}v^{V}
. But that choice was completely arbitrary, and we expect that swapping the roles of
\otimes_{t}
and
\medblackcircle_{t}
in the definition of
J\odot J^{\prime}
will also yield a derivable judgment with respect to the previously constructed theory.

We will now explain more precisely how this modification is made and how it leads to a proof that
I_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
is an interpretation.

Construction 8.2
.

Consider gats
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
. In the notation f §
2
, we sketch a definition of operations

\displaystyle\otimes^{*}_{t}:

\displaystyle(Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{A}))\times(Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{B})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{B}))\longrightarrow Exp_{t}(\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})),

\displaystyle\medblackcircle^{*}_{t}:

\displaystyle(Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{A}))\times(Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{B})\cup Der_{t}(\mathbb{B}))\longrightarrow Exp_{t}(\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})),

\displaystyle\otimes^{*}_{s}:

\displaystyle Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{A})\otimes Der_{s}^{+}(\mathbb{B})\longrightarrow Exp_{s}(\Sigma(\mathbb{A})\otimes\Sigma(\mathbb{B})),

as well as
\otimes^{*}
for precontexts and
\odot^{*}
for derivable judgments, by using the same recursive procedure as for
\otimes_{t}
,
\medblackcircle_{t}
,
\otimes_{s}
,
\otimes
,
\odot
, respectively, except for two cases: if
J=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m}\vdash u:U)
and
J^{\prime}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n}\vdash v:V)
where
u
,
v
are not variables, we let

\displaystyle u^{U}\medblackcircle^{*}v^{V}

\displaystyle=J\medblackcircle^{*}_{t}J^{\prime}=(x^{U}\otimes^{*}v^{V})[u^{U}\otimes^{*}y_{1}^{Y_{1}}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes^{*}y_{n}^{Y_{n}}\mid xy_{n}],

\displaystyle u^{U}\otimes^{*}v^{V}

\displaystyle=J\otimes_{t}^{*}J^{\prime}=(u^{U}\otimes^{*}y^{V})[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes^{*}v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes^{*}v^{V}\mid x_{m}y].

Note that these are the only cases where the use of
\otimes_{t}
and
\medblackcircle_{t}
is asymmetric.

Arguing by induction yields the following result on the preinterpretation
I_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
defined above:

Proposition 8.3
.

For derivable judgments
J
,
J^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, respectively, we have

\hat{I}_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}(J\odot J^{\prime})=J^{\prime}\odot^{*}J.

Thus Proposition
8.1
will follow once we verify that
J^{\prime}\odot^{*}J
is derivable.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
J
,
J^{\prime}
be derivable judgments in gats
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, respectively. Then
J\odot^{*}J^{\prime}
is derivable in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
.

Proof sketch.

We will outline a proof by induction that the following holds for all
h\geq 0
: if
\textsf{ht}(J)\textsf{ht}(J^{\prime})
, then the judgment
J\odot^{*}J^{\prime}
is derivable and has the same interpretation in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B})
as
J\odot J^{\prime}
.

Given
h\geq 0
, suppose that the claim holds for all
h^{\prime}<h
, and let
J
,
J^{\prime}
be such that
\textsf{ht}(J)\textsf{ht}(J^{\prime})=h
. Several cases need to be verified, corresponding to Table
1
. Firstly, note that in all of them, the precontext of
J\odot^{*}J^{\prime}
is either

•

of the form
\textbf{X}\otimes^{*}\textbf{Y}
where
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})<h
, or

•

of the form
\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes^{*}\textbf{Y}^{\prime})
where
\textbf{X}=\partial\textbf{X}^{\prime}
and
\textbf{Y}=\partial\textbf{Y}^{\prime}
satisfy
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}^{\prime})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}^{\prime})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y})<h
.

In either case, every sort expression occurring in the context is, by construction,
U\otimes^{*}V
for some derivable judgments
\textbf{A}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
and
\textbf{B}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
such that
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{A}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})\textsf{ht}(\textbf{B}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort})<h
. But by the induction hypothesis,
\partial(\textbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{B}^{\prime})\vdash U\otimes^{*}V\equiv U\otimes V\;\mathsf{sort}
is derivable where
\textbf{A}^{\prime}=(\textbf{A},a:U)
,
\textbf{B}^{\prime}=(\textbf{B},b:V)
. Hence in the first (resp. second) case,
\textbf{X}\otimes^{*}\textbf{Y}
(resp.
\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes^{*}\textbf{Y}^{\prime})
) is a context and is provably equal to
\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}
(resp. to
\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})
).

It remains analyze the second part of
J\odot^{*}J^{\prime}
:

•

Suppose that
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)
and
J^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash v:V)
where
u
,
v
are not variables. Then
J\odot^{*}J^{\prime}
is

\displaystyle\textbf{X}\otimes^{*}\textbf{Y}

\displaystyle\vdash u^{U}\otimes^{*}v^{V}\equiv u^{U}\medblackcircle^{*}v^{V}

\displaystyle:(U\otimes^{*}V)[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes^{*}v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes^{*}v^{V}\mid x_{m}y,...,u^{U}\otimes^{*}y_{1}^{Y_{1}}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes^{*}y_{n}^{Y_{n}}\mid xy_{n}].

Letting
\textbf{X}^{\prime}=(\textbf{X},x:U)
and
\textbf{Y}^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y},y:V)
, the induction hypothesis implies that

\displaystyle\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes^{*}\textbf{Y}^{\prime})\equiv\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})\;\textsf{ctx}

\displaystyle\partial(\textbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{Y}^{\prime})\vdash U\otimes^{*}V\equiv U\otimes V\;\mathsf{sort}

\displaystyle\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash x_{i}^{X_{i}}\otimes^{*}v^{V}\equiv x_{i}^{X_{i}}\otimes v^{V}\;\mathsf{term}

(
1\leq i\leq m
)

\displaystyle\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash u^{U}\otimes^{*}y_{j}^{Y_{j}}\equiv u^{U}\otimes y_{j}^{Y_{j}}\;\mathsf{term}

(
1\leq j\leq n
)

are derivable. Now, by substitution we obtain

\displaystyle\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}

\displaystyle\vdash(U\otimes^{*}V)[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes^{*}v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes^{*}v^{V}\mid x_{m}y,...,u^{U}\otimes^{*}y_{1}^{Y_{1}}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes^{*}y_{n}^{Y_{n}}\mid xy_{n}]

\displaystyle\equiv(U\otimes V)[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{m}y,...,u^{U}\otimes y_{1}^{Y_{1}}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes y_{n}^{Y_{n}}\mid xy_{n}]\;\mathsf{sort}.

Since
J\odot J^{\prime}
, which is

\displaystyle\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}

\displaystyle\vdash u^{U}\otimes v^{V}\equiv u^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V}

\displaystyle:(U\otimes V)[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{m}y,...,u^{U}\otimes y_{1}^{Y_{1}}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes y_{n}^{Y_{n}}\mid xy_{n}],

is derivable, to obtain
J\odot^{*}J^{\prime}
it suffices to derive

\displaystyle\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash u^{U}\otimes^{*}v^{V}\equiv u^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V}\;\mathsf{term},

\displaystyle\textbf{X}\otimes\textbf{Y}\vdash u^{U}\medblackcircle^{*}v^{V}\equiv u^{U}\otimes v^{V}\;\mathsf{term}.

But the induction hypothesis yields

\displaystyle u^{U}\otimes^{*}v^{V}

\displaystyle=(u^{U}\otimes^{*}y^{V})[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes^{*}v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes^{*}v^{V}\mid x_{m}y]

\displaystyle\equiv(u^{U}\otimes y^{V})[x_{1}^{X_{1}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{1}y,...,x_{m}^{X_{m}}\otimes v^{V}\mid x_{m}y]

\displaystyle=u^{U}\medblackcircle v^{V},

\displaystyle u^{U}\medblackcircle^{*}v^{V}

\displaystyle=(x^{U}\otimes^{*}v^{V})[u^{U}\otimes^{*}y_{1}^{Y_{1}}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes^{*}y_{n}^{Y_{n}}\mid xy_{n}]

\displaystyle\equiv(x^{U}\otimes v^{V})[u^{U}\otimes y_{1}^{Y_{1}}\mid xy_{1},...,u^{U}\otimes y_{n}^{Y_{n}}\mid xy_{n}]

\displaystyle=u^{U}\otimes v^{V},

which concludes the proof in this case.

•

In all other cases, the definition of
J\odot^{\prime}J^{\prime}
does not involve any term expressions
u\otimes^{*}v
corresponding to judgments
K=(\textbf{A}\vdash u:U)
,
K^{\prime}=(\textbf{B}\vdash v:V)
where
u
,
v
are not variables and
\textsf{ht}(K)\textsf{ht}(K^{\prime})\geq h
. This implies (details have been omitted) that:

-

Any sort expression
U\otimes^{*}V
occurring in
J\odot^{\prime}J^{\prime}
can be expanded and proved equal to
U\otimes V
using the induction hypothesis.

-

For a term expression
u\otimes^{*}v
occurring in
J\odot^{\prime}J^{\prime}
, say with
u
,
v
corresponding to judgments
K
,
K^{\prime}
, if
u
and
v
are not variables, then the induction hypothesis
u\otimes^{*}v\equiv u\otimes v
.

-

Otherwise, if
u
or
v
is a variable, then
u\otimes^{*}v
can be expanded and proved equal to
u\otimes v
via the induction hypothesis.

This concludes our sketch of proof that
J\odot^{*}J^{\prime}
is derivable in
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
for any
J
,
J^{\prime}
derivable in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
.
∎

As a consequence we obtain Proposition
8.1
, which in turn implies that
\mathbb{A}\otimes\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{B}\otimes\mathbb{A}
are isomorphic via
I_{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}}
and
I_{\mathbb{B},\mathbb{A}}
.

Like the situation with associativity, a categorical study of this symmetry isomorphism is out of reach in the current article. We will return to this problem in
[
4
]
.

Appendix A
An overview of generalized algebraic theories

We now give an overview of the concept of a generalized algebraic theory (gat for short), introduced by J. Cartmell in his doctoral thesis
[
10
]
(see also
[
11
]
).

Our approach to defining a generalized algebraic theory differs from Cartmell’s in the choice of derivation rules. However, as must be the case for our presentation to be valid, our list of derivation rules is “as expressive” as Cartmell’s in the following sense (see Proposition
A.21
): for any pretheory, the sets of derivable judgments obtained by the two procedures are the same. In other words, the classes of “theories” obtained in the two cases are identical as long as one only considers the
set
of derivable judgments (rather than the structure of derivations).

The reason for this modification is that, with the new derivation rules, we have a finer control over the recursive process by which a judgment is derived. Ultimately, this will allow us to show that a certain
height
function – which, in a sense, measures the complexity of a derivable judgment in terms of a “shortest” derivation – has several good properties allowing us to prove statements on judgments by induction.

A.1
Alphabets and pretheories

The starting point is what we will call a
sort-and-term alphabet
, which provides the symbols required for constructing a generalized algebraic theory:

Definition A.1
.

A
sort-and-term alphabet

-
or, in our setting, simply
alphabet

-
is a triple
\Sigma=(\Sigma^{var},\Sigma^{sort},\Sigma^{term})
consisting of a countably infinite set
\Sigma^{var}
of
variables
, a set
\Sigma^{sort}
of
sort symbols
(or
type symbols
), and a set
\Sigma^{term}
of
term symbols
(or
operation symbols
).

Variables will usually denoted by lowercase letters such as
a
,
b
,
x
,
y
, often with indices as in
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
when we work with lists of variables. Sort symbols will be denoted by uppercase letters such as
S
and
T
, and term symbols by lowercase letters such as
s
and
t
.

Construction A.2
.

Consider the (single-sorted) algebraic theory
\mathbb{A}
having
\mathbb{N}\times(\Sigma^{sort}\sqcup\Sigma^{term})
as its set of function symbols, where
(n,w)
has arity
n
for each
n
and
w
, and having no axioms.

We define
\Sigma^{*}
as the free model of
\mathbb{A}
on the set
\Sigma^{var}
. An element of
\Sigma^{*}
is called an
expression
on the alphabet
\Sigma
.

To provide an explicit description of
(\Sigma^{var},\Sigma^{sort},\Sigma^{term})^{*}
, let
X
be the set of all finite sequences
w_{1}
, …,
w_{n}
(possibly with
n=0
, i.e. we include the empty sequence) of elements of a set obtained from
\mathbb{N}\times(\Sigma^{sort}\sqcup\Sigma^{term})
by adjoining two elements, which we denote by
(
and
)
, to be treated as left and right parentheses. Precisely, we have

X=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}\bigg(\mathbb{N}\times(\Sigma^{sort}\sqcup\Sigma^{term})\;\sqcup\;\{\;(,\;)\;\}\bigg)^{n}.

Then
(\Sigma^{var},\Sigma^{sort},\Sigma^{term})^{*}
is the smallest subset
Y\subset X
that satisfies the following conditions:

•

If
x
is a variable, then the length-
1
sequence
x
belongs to
Y
.

•

Suppose given
\sigma\in\Sigma^{sort}\sqcup\Sigma^{term}
and a sequence
w_{1}
, …,
w_{n}
of elements of
Y
where
n\geq 0
. Then the length-
n+3
sequence

\sigma,\;(,\;w_{1},...,w_{n},\;)

belongs to
Y
.

We will denote it by
\sigma(w_{1},...,w_{n})
when
n\geq 0
, or simply by
\sigma
when
n=0
.

The structure of model of
\mathbb{A}
on this set is given as follows: for
(\sigma,n)\in\mathbb{N}\times(\Sigma^{sort}\sqcup\Sigma^{term})
, i.e. an element of
\Sigma^{sort}\sqcup\Sigma^{term}
viewed as an
n
-ary operation, its action on
X
is given by sending a sequence
w_{1}
, …,
w_{n}
in
X
to the sequence
\sigma(w_{1},...,w_{n})
described above.

Definition A.3
.

In the above notation, if
w\in(\Sigma^{var},\Sigma^{sort},\Sigma^{term})^{*}
and
\sigma
is a variable, sort symbol or term symbol, we define an
occurrence
of
\sigma
as an index
i
such that the
i
-th entry of
w
equals
\sigma
. If there exists an occurrence of
\sigma
in
w
, we say that
\sigma

occurs
in
w
.

Given
w
,
u\in(\Sigma^{var},\Sigma^{sort},\Sigma^{term})^{*}
and a variable
x
, we write
w[u\mid x]
for the element of
(\Sigma^{var},\Sigma^{sort},\Sigma^{term})^{*}
obtained by replacing every occurrence of
x
by
u

More generally, for
k\geq 1
, a sequence of variables
x_{1}
, …,
x_{k}
, and a sequence
u_{1}
, …,
u_{k}
in
(\Sigma^{var},\Sigma^{sort},\Sigma^{term})^{*}
, we write
w[u_{1}\mid x_{1},...,u_{k}\mid x_{k}]
for the expression obtained by
simultaneously
replacing every occurrence of
x_{i}
by
u_{i}
for each
i
.

An expression
w\in(\Sigma^{var},\Sigma^{sort},\Sigma^{term})^{*}
is said to be a
term expression
if no sort symbol occurs in
w
. We say that
w
is a
sort expression
if it is of the form
T(u_{1},...,u_{n})
where
T
is a sort symbol and
u_{1}
, …,
u_{n}
are term expressions.

Following our convention for sort symbols, we will usually denote term expressions by using lowercase letters, and sort expressions by using uppercase ones.

Definition A.4
.

Given an alphabet
\Sigma=(\Sigma^{var},\Sigma^{sort},\Sigma^{term})
, a
(length-
n
) precontext
is a sequence of ordered pairs
((x_{1},X_{1}),...,(x_{n},X_{n}))
, with
n\geq 0
, where, for each
i
,
x_{i}
is a variable and
X_{i}
is a sort expression that only contains variables among
x_{1}
, …,
x_{i-1}
. We denote it by

x_{1}:X_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:X_{n}.

We will usually denote contexts by boldface letters such as
X
and
Y
.

Given contexts

\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m}),\qquad\qquad\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n}),

we write
\textbf{X},\textbf{Y}
or
\textbf{X},y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n}
for the concatenation

x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m},y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n}

of
X
and
Y
.

The unique precontext of length
0
will be said to be
empty
.

Notation A.5
.

For a precontext
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
, we denote its length (i.e.
n
) by
\boldsymbol{\ell}(\textbf{X})
. Also, for
i\in\{0,...,n\}
we will write
\partial_{i}\textbf{X}
for the truncation
(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{i}:X_{i})
.

Definition A.6
.

We define a

•

sort judgment
as a pair
(\textbf{X},U)
consisting of a precontext
X
and a sort expression
U
. We denote it by

\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}.

•

term judgment
as a triple
(\textbf{X},U,u)
consisting of a precontext
X
, a sort expression
U
, and a term expression
u
. We denote it by

\textbf{X}\vdash u:U.

•

sort equality judgment
as a triple
(\textbf{X},U,V)
consisting of a precontext
X
and sort expressions
U
,
V
. We denote it by

\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv V\;\mathsf{sort}.

•

term equality judgment
as a quadruple
(\textbf{X},U,u,v)
consisting of a precontext
X
, a sort expression
U
, and term expressions
t
,
t^{\prime}
. We denote it by

\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv v:U.

We will use the word “judgment” to refer collectively to the structures introduced in these four items, but, more generally, also to other kinds of structure, introduced later.

In contrast, a judgment of one of the four kinds above will be called a
standard judgment
.

We will usually denote judgments by the letter
J
and variants such as
J^{\prime}
and
J_{1}
, …,
J_{n}
.

If
J
is a standard judgment, the precontext
X
preceding the turnstile symbol will be referred to as the
context of

J
.

When the context of a standard judgment is empty, we omit it from the notation:
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
becomes
\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
.

A precontext
X
corresponds to a
context judgment
, which we denote by
16
16

16

Technically, a context judgment is the same as a precontext. However, the shift in terminology and notation corresponds to the fact that, in what follows, the same data will be treated from two different perspectives.

\textbf{X}\;\textsf{ctx}.

We will write
\mathscr{J}(\Sigma)
, or just
\mathscr{J}
when the alphabet is implicit, for the union of the set of standard judgments and that of context judgments of
\Sigma
.

Definition A.7

(
[
10
]
)
.

A
pretheory
is a pair
\mathbb{T}=(\Sigma,\mathscr{A})
where
\Sigma
is a sort-and-term alphabet and
\mathscr{A}
is a subset of the set of standard judgments of
\Sigma

-
whose elements we call the
axioms
of
\mathbb{T}

-
with the following properties:

(i)

If
T
is a sort symbol, then
\mathscr{A}
contains a unique judgment of the form

x_{1}:X_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash T(x_{1},...,x_{n})\;\mathsf{sort},

which we refer to as axiom that
introduces

T
.

Moreover,
\mathscr{A}
does not contain any other sort judgments.

(ii)

If
t
is a term symbol, then
\mathscr{A}
contains a unique judgment of the form

x_{1}:X_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash t(x_{1},...,x_{n}):U,

which we refer to as axiom that
introduces

t
.

Moreover,
\mathscr{A}
does not contain any other term judgments.

Generally, an axiom
J\in\mathscr{A}
is called a
sort
(resp.
term
,
sort equality
,
term equality
)
axiom
if it is, respectively, a sort (resp. term, sort equality, term equality) judgment.

In the above notation, we refer to
x_{1}:X_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash T(x_{1},...,x_{n})\;\mathsf{sort}
, resp.
x_{1}:X_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash t(x_{1},...,x_{n}):U
, as the
introduction axiom
of
T
, resp.
t
.

For a pretheory
\mathbb{T}
, we will write
\Sigma^{var}(\mathbb{T})
,
\Sigma^{sort}(\mathbb{T})
,
\Sigma^{term}(\mathbb{T})
, and
\mathscr{A}(\mathbb{T})
, respectively, for its set of variables, of sort symbols, of term symbols, and of axioms.

Definition A.8
.

An
inference step
with respect to a given sort-and-term alphabet is a sequence
(J_{1},...,J_{n},J_{n+1})
in
\mathscr{J}
with
n\geq 0
(hence each entry is either a standard judgment or a context judgment). We denote it by

\frac{J_{1}\quad J_{2}\quad\ldots\ \quad J_{n}}{J_{n+1}}.

The judgments
J_{1}
, …,
J_{n}
are called the
premises
, and
J_{n+1}
is called the
conclusion
.

By an
inference rule
we will mean a set whose elements are inference steps. If
R
is an inference rule, we refer to an inference step in
R
as an
instance
of
R
. If
(J_{1},...,J_{n},J_{n+1})
is an instance of
R
, we write

\frac{J_{1}\quad J_{2}\quad\ldots\ \quad J_{n}}{J_{n+1}}(R).

For a fixed pretheory
\mathbb{T}
, we write
\mathscr{R}_{gat}
for the set of all inference steps, with respect to the alphabet of
\mathbb{T}
, of one of the sixteen forms below. Note that for each item we obtain an inference rule by taking the set of all inference steps of the given form.

(ctx)

\frac{\;}{\emptyset\;\textsf{ctx}}

where
\emptyset
denotes the empty precontext. And, for
n\geq 0
,

\frac{x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}}{x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n},x_{n+1}:U\quad\;\textsf{ctx}}

whenever
x_{n+1}
is a variable distinct from
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
.

(s1)

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}}{\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort}}.

(s2)

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}}{\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort}}.

(s3)

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\equiv U^{\prime\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}}{\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}}.

(t1)

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash u:U}{\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u:U}.

(t2)

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U}{\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}\equiv u:U}.

(t3)

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}\equiv u^{\prime\prime}:U}{\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime\prime}:U}.

(seq/t)

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u:U}{\textbf{X}\vdash u:U^{\prime}}.

(seq/teq)

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u:U^{\prime}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}:U^{\prime}}{\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U^{\prime}}.

(var)

\frac{x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash X_{i}\;\mathsf{sort}}{x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash x_{i}:X_{i}}

where
n\geq 1
and
1\leq i\leq n
.

(s-a)

For each sort axiom
\textbf{X}\vdash T(x_{1},...,x_{n})\;\mathsf{sort}
we consider

\frac{\textbf{X}\;\textsf{ctx}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash x_{1}:X_{1}\quad\cdots\quad\textbf{X}\vdash x_{n}:X_{n}}{\textbf{X}\vdash T(x_{1},...,x_{n})\;\mathsf{sort}}

where
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
.

(t-a)

For each term axiom
\textbf{X}\vdash t(x_{1},...,x_{n}):U
we consider

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash x_{1}:X_{1}\quad\cdots\quad\textbf{X}\vdash x_{n}:X_{n}}{\textbf{X}\vdash t(x_{1},...,x_{n}):U}

where
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
.

(seq-a)

For each sort equality axiom
\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}
we consider

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}}{\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}}.

(teq-a)

For each term equality axiom
\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U
, we consider

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash u:U\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}:U}{\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U}.

(s-sub)

Suppose given a sort axiom
J
, say
\textbf{X}\vdash T(x_{1},...,x_{n})\;\mathsf{sort}
, where
X
is
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}
(possibly with
n=0
), a precontext
Y
, and term expressions
f_{1}
, …,
f_{n}
. For
i=1
, …,
n
, let
J_{i}
be the judgment

\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}:X_{i}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid x_{i-1}].

We consider

\frac{J\quad\textbf{Y}\;\textsf{ctx}\quad J_{1}\quad J_{2}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash T(f_{1},...,f_{n})\;\mathsf{sort}}.

(t-sub)

Suppose given a term axiom
J
, say
\textbf{X}\vdash t(x_{1},...,x_{n}):U
where
X
is
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}
(possibly with
n=0
), a precontext
Y
, and term expressions
f_{1}
, …,
f_{n}
. For
i=1
, …,
n
, let
J_{i}
be the judgment

\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}:X_{i}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid x_{i-1}].

Writing
J^{\prime}
for
\textbf{Y}\vdash U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]\;\mathsf{sort}
, we consider

\frac{J\quad J^{\prime}\quad J_{1}\quad J_{2}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{n}):U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]}.

(seq-sub-1)

Suppose given a sort equality axiom
J
, say
\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}
where
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
. Suppose given a precontext
Y
, term expressions
f_{1}
, …,
f_{n}
, and, for
i=1
, …,
n
, let
J_{i}
be the judgment

\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}:X_{i}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid x_{i-1}].

Then, writing
J^{\prime}
for
\textbf{Y}\vdash U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]\;\mathsf{sort}
and
J^{\prime\prime}
for
\textbf{Y}\vdash U^{\prime}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]\;\mathsf{sort}
, we consider

\frac{J\quad J^{\prime}\quad J^{\prime\prime}\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]\equiv U^{\prime}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]\;\mathsf{sort}}.

(seq-sub-2)

Suppose given a sort axiom
J
, say
\textbf{X}\vdash T(x_{1},...,x_{n})\;\mathsf{sort}
where
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
. Suppose given a precontext
Y
, term expressions
f_{1}
, …,
f_{n}
,
g_{1}
, …,
g_{n}
, and, for
i=1
, …,
n
, let
J_{i}
be the judgment

\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}\equiv g_{i}:X_{i}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid x_{i-1}].

Then, writing
J^{\prime}
for
\textbf{Y}\vdash T(f_{1},...,f_{n})\;\mathsf{sort}
and
J^{\prime\prime}
for
\textbf{Y}\vdash T(g_{1},...,g_{n})\;\mathsf{sort}
, we consider

\frac{J\quad J^{\prime}\quad J^{\prime\prime}\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash T(f_{1},...,f_{n})\equiv T(g_{1},...,g_{n})\;\mathsf{sort}}.

(teq-sub-1)

Suppose given a term equality axiom
J
, say
\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U
where
X
is
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}
. Suppose given a precontext
Y
, term expressions
f_{1}
, …,
f_{n}
, and, for
i=1
, …,
n
, let
J_{i}
be the judgment

\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}:X_{i}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid x_{i-1}].

Then, writing
J^{\prime}
for
\textbf{Y}\vdash u[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]:U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]
and
J^{\prime\prime}
for
\textbf{Y}\vdash u^{\prime}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]:U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]
, we consider

\frac{J\quad J^{\prime}\quad J^{\prime\prime}\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash u[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]\equiv u^{\prime}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]:U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]}.

(teq-sub-2)

Suppose given a term axiom
J
, say
\textbf{X}\vdash t(x_{1},...,x_{n}):U
where
X
is
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}
. Suppose given a precontext
Y
, term expressions
f_{1}
, …,
f_{n}
,
g_{1}
, …,
g_{n}
, and, for
i=1
, …,
n
, let
J_{i}
be the judgment

\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}\equiv g_{i}:X_{i}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid x_{i-1}].

Then, writing
J^{\prime}
for
\textbf{Y}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{n}):U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]
and
J^{\prime\prime}
for
\textbf{Y}\vdash t(g_{1},...,g_{n}):U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]
, we consider

\frac{J\quad J^{\prime}\quad J^{\prime\prime}\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{n})\equiv t(g_{1},...,g_{n}):U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]}.

A.2
Derivable judgments

We will now define
derivable judgments
with respect to a pretheory
\mathbb{T}
.

Definition A.9
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a pretheory, and recall the definition of the set of inference steps
\mathscr{R}_{gat}
given above.

We have an increasing sequence
(\mathscr{D}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
of subsets of
\mathscr{R}_{gat}
constructed recursively as follows:

(i)

\mathscr{D}_{0}
is the empty set.

(ii)

For each
n\geq 0
,
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}
is the union of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
with the set of all inference steps

\frac{J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{k}}{J}

(where
k\geq 0
) in
\mathscr{R}_{gat}
such that
J_{1}
, …,
J_{k}
occur as the conclusion of some inference step in
\mathscr{D}_{n}
.

Definition A.10
.

A judgment
J
is
derivable
if it is the conclusion of a derivation step in
\mathscr{D}_{n}
for some
n\geq 0
. The
height
of
J
is defined as the smallest such
n
. We will denote it by
\textsf{ht}(J)
.

An
initial inference
of a derivable judgment
J
is an inference step in
\mathscr{D}_{\textsf{ht}(J)}
whose conclusion is
J
. (Hence whose premises have height
<\textsf{ht}(J)
.)

A
context
is a precontext
X
such that
X

ctx
is derivable. The height of
X
is defined as follows: expressing
X
as
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}
, we let
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})
be
0
if
n=0
, and
\textsf{ht}(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n-1}:X_{n-1}\vdash X_{n}\;\mathsf{sort})
otherwise. (Note, in particular, that
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\;\textsf{ctx})=\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})+1
.)

Definition A.11
.

A
generalized algebraic theory
(which we will often refer to simply as a
theory
, or abbreviate as
gat
) is a pretheory in which every axiom is derivable.

This means, for instance, that if
J
is a sort axiom, say
\textbf{X}\vdash T(x_{1},...,x_{n})\;\mathsf{sort}
, then the premises of the corresponding instance of (s-a), i.e.

\textbf{X}\;\textsf{ctx},\quad\textbf{X}\vdash x_{1}:X_{1},\quad\ldots,\quad\textbf{X}\vdash x_{n}:X_{n},

are derivable. Similarly for the other three kinds of axioms.

A.3
Other kinds of judgment, the canonical sort of a term, and context morphisms

We will also consider the following structures:

•

An
partial term judgment
is a pair
(\textbf{X},u)
consisting of a precontext
X
and a term expression
u
. We denote it by

\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}.

We say that it is
derivable
if there exists a sort expression
U
such that
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
is derivable.

•

An
partial term equality judgment
is a triple
(\textbf{X},u,u^{\prime})
of a precontext
X
and term expressions
u
,
u^{\prime}
. We denote it by

\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}\;\mathsf{term}.

We say that it is
derivable
if there exists a sort expression
U
such that
\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U
is derivable.

Remark A.12
.

The absence of a sort symbol from these judgments is not intended to suggest that it is meaningful to specify terms that do not have a sort. The judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}
can be viewed, by the definition of when it is derivable, as an abbreviation for “
u
is a term of
some
sort
U
in context
X
”. In fact, we make sense of
u
being a “term in context
X
” at the same time as we assign a sort to it.

But for
\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}
to be semantically unambiguous, it is crucial that the syntax of gats be such that if
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
and
\textbf{X}\vdash u:V
are derivable, then
\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv V\;\mathsf{sort}
is derivable. This is indeed the case, as noted by Cartmell in
[
10
]
, page 1.35, as a corollary of the “derivation lemma”.

Considering such judgments turned out to be quite useful in the present text for the following reason: if we have derivable judgments
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
,
\textbf{X}\vdash v:V
, and
\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv V\;\mathsf{sort}
, then both
\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv v:U
and
\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv v:V
are well-formed (in the sense of
[
10
]
), and one is derivable precisely when the other is. The judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv v\;\mathsf{term}
encodes the meaning of either without the need of artificially choosing a sort expression such as
U
,
V
, etc.

•

A
context equality judgment
is a pair
(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})
consisting of two precontexts of the same length, say
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
and
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
. We denote it by

\textbf{X}\equiv\textbf{Y}\;\textsf{ctx}.

We say that it is
derivable
if
X

ctx
and
Y

ctx
are derivable and, for
1\leq i\leq n
, the judgment

\partial_{i-1}\textbf{X}\vdash X_{i}\equiv Y_{i}[x_{1}\mid y_{1},...,x_{i-1}\mid y_{i-1}]\;\mathsf{sort}

is derivable.

•

Consider a derivable judgment
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}
. The
canonical sort
of
u
(with the context implicit), denoted by
\mathcal{S}(u)
, is defined as follows: if
u
is a variable
x_{i}
, then
\mathcal{S}(u)=X_{i}
; if
u=t(f_{1},...,f_{k})
with
t
introduced by the axiom
\textbf{A}\vdash t(a_{1},...,a_{k}):V
, then
\mathcal{S}(u)=V[f_{1}\mid a_{1},...,f_{k}\mid a_{k}]
.

(Later we will check that
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash u:\mathcal{S}(u)
is derivable.)

•

A
premorphism
is a triple
(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y},\textbf{f})
consisting of precontexts
X
,
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
and a sequence of term expressions
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n})
. We denote it by

\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\longrightarrow\textbf{Y}.

We say that it is
derivable
or that it is a
(context) morphism
if
X

ctx
and
Y

ctx
are derivable and, for
1\leq i\leq n
, the judgment

\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}:Y_{i}[f_{1}\mid y_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid y_{i-1}]

is derivable.

•

A
premorphism equality
is a quadruple
(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y},\textbf{f},\textbf{g})
consisting of precontexts
X
,
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
and sequences of term expressions
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n})
,
\textbf{g}=(g_{1},...,g_{n})
. We denote it by

\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\longrightarrow\textbf{Y}.

We say that it is
derivable
or that it is a
(context) morphism equality
if
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
and
\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
are derivable and, for
1\leq i\leq n
, the judgment

\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}\equiv g_{i}:Y_{i}[f_{1}\mid y_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid y_{i-1}]

is derivable.

We extend the height function
ht
, previously defined for standard judgments and context judgments, to the newly introduced ones in the following way:

•

For derivable partial term judgments,

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})=\min_{U}\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)

where the minimum is taken over all sort expressions
U
such that
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
is derivable.

•

For derivable partial term equality judgments,

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}\;\mathsf{term})=\min_{U}\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U)

where the minimum is taken over all sort expressions
U
such that
\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U
is derivable.

•

For derivable context equality judgments,

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\equiv\textbf{Y}\;\textsf{ctx})=\max\{\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}),\;\;\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}),\;\;\textsf{ht}(\partial_{0}\textbf{X}\vdash X_{1}\equiv Y^{\prime}_{1}\;\mathsf{sort}),\;\;\;...,\;\;\;\textsf{ht}(\partial_{n-1}\textbf{X}\vdash X_{n}\equiv Y^{\prime}_{n}\;\mathsf{sort})\}

where
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
,
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
, and
Y^{\prime}_{i}=Y_{i}[x_{1}\mid y_{1},...,x_{i-1}\mid y_{i-1}]
.

•

For morphisms,

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y})=\max\{\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}),\;\;\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}),\;\;\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{1}:Y^{\prime}_{1}),\;\;...,\;\;\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{n}:Y^{\prime}_{n})\}

where
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
and
Y^{\prime}_{i}=Y_{i}[f_{1}\mid y_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid y_{i-1}]
.

•

For morphism equalities,

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y})=\max\{\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}),\;\;\textsf{ht}(\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}),\;\;\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{1}\equiv g_{1}:Y^{\prime}_{1}),\;\;...,\;\;\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{n}\equiv g_{n}:Y^{\prime}_{n})\}

where
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
and
Y^{\prime}_{i}=Y_{i}[f_{1}\mid y_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid y_{i-1}]
.

Notation A.13
.

Given a sequence of term expressions
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n})
, a context
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
, and an expression
w
that only contains variables among
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}
, we write
w[\textbf{f}]
for
w[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]
, with
X
implicit. We will use this, for example, when
f
is a morphism (or premorphism) from some context
Y
to
X
.

For
i=0
, …,
n
, we let
\partial_{i}\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{i})
. Note that for a premorphism (resp. morphism)
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
, we have a premorphism (resp. morphism)
\partial_{i}\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\partial_{i}\textbf{Y}
.

Definition A.14
.

Suppose given contexts
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{m}:X_{m})
,
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
, and
\textbf{Z}=(z_{1}:Z_{1},...,z_{p}:Z_{p})
, and context morphisms

\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n}):\textbf{X}\longrightarrow\textbf{Y},

\textbf{g}=(g_{1},...,g_{p}):\textbf{Y}\longrightarrow\textbf{Z}.

Their
composite
is the premorphism
(\textbf{X},\textbf{Z},\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{f})
where the tuple of expressions
\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{f}
is given by

(\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{f})_{i}=g_{i}[\textbf{f}]=g_{i}[f_{1}\mid y_{1},...,f_{n}\mid y_{n}]

for
1\leq i\leq p
.

It is true, but not clear at this point, that
\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{f}
is a morphism from
X
to
Z
. This will be verified in Corollary
A.18
as a consequence of Proposition
A.17
, which states that derivable judgments are closed under substitution along context morphisms.

A.4
Properties of the height function

We now verify some properties of the height of derivable judgments, contexts, etc., as defined above.

Proposition A.15
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a pretheory. The following hold:

(a)

Every derivable judgment has height at least
1
. The only context of height
0
is
\emptyset
, and the only context morphism of height
0
is the identity
():\emptyset\rightarrow\emptyset
.

(b)

For a derivable sort judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
we have

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}).

As a consequence, for a length-
n
context
X
we have
\textsf{ht}(\partial_{i}\textbf{X})<\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})
for
0\leq i<n
.

(c)

For a derivable sort equality judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash U=U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}
we have

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}),

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}).

(d)

For a derivable term judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
we have

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}).

(e)

For a derivable term equality judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U
we have

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U),

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}:U).

(f)

If the judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
is derivable, so is
\textbf{X}\vdash u:\mathcal{S}(u)
. Moreover, we have

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)\geq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:\mathcal{S}(u)),

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)\geq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash\mathcal{S}(u)\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort}),

and the first inequality is strict if and only if
U
and
\mathcal{S}(u)
are distinct.

(g)

If
\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term}
is derivable, then
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})=\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:\mathcal{S}(u))
.

(h)

For a derivable judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash T(f_{1},...,f_{k})\;\mathsf{sort}
, we have

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash T(f_{1},...,f_{k})\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}:\mathcal{S}(f_{i}))

for
1\leq i\leq k
. Also, if
\textbf{X}\vdash T(f_{1},...,f_{k})
is not an axiom, then, letting
\textbf{A}\vdash T(a_{1},...,a_{k})\;\mathsf{sort}
be the axiom that introduces
T
and
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{k})
,

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash T(f_{1},...,f_{k})\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}).

(i)

For a derivable judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{k}):U
, we have

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{k}):U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}\;\mathsf{term})

for
1\leq i\leq k
.

(j)

For a context morphism
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
, where
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n})
,

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y})\geq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}\;\mathsf{term})

for
1\leq i\leq n
. Also, for a morphism equality
\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
we have

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y})\geq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}),\;\;\textsf{ht}(\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}).

Proof.

(a)

These follow directly from the definition.

(b)

Note that
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
is the conclusion of an inference step (s-a) or (s-sub). Choosing an initial inference, in either case
X

ctx
is among the premises, so
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\;\textsf{ctx})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X})
.

(c)

We proceed inductively: letting
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})
and
\textsf{ht}(J)=h
, let us prove that

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}),

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})

hold assuming that the analogous inequalities hold for
\textbf{Y}\vdash V\equiv V^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}
whenever
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash V\equiv V^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})<h
. Note that
J
has an initial inference of the form (s1), (s2), (s3), (seq-a), (seq-sub-1), or (seq-sub-2). Let
I
be one such initial inference.

If
I
is (s1), (seq-a), (seq-sub-1) or (seq-sub-2), then it has
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
as a premise, so the desired inequalities hold trivially.

If
I
is (s2), then it has
\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort}
as a premise. But by the induction hypothesis,

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}),\;\;\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U).

If
I
is (s3), it has premises
\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}
and
\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime\prime}\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}),

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime\prime}\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}).

(d)

Let
I
be an initial inference of
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
.

If
I
is (seq/t), it has a premise of the form
\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort}
. Then by item (c) we have

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}).

If
I
is (var), (t-a) or (t-sub), it has
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
as a premise, so the inequality holds trivially.

(e)

We will prove by induction on
h
that the desired inequalities hold for all derivable
\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}
such that
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})=h
.

Given
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})
, assume that the claim holds for
0
, …,
\textsf{ht}(J)-1
, and let
I
be an initial inference of
J
.

If
I
is (t1), (seq/teq), (teq-a), (teq-sub-1) or (teq-sub-2), it has
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
and
\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}:U
among its premises, so the desired inequalities hold trivially.

If
I
is (t2), it has
\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}\equiv u:U
as a premise. But by the induction hypothesis,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}\equiv u:U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}:U)
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)
.

If
I
is (t3), it has premises
\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime\prime}:U
and
\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime\prime}\equiv u^{\prime}:U
, so the induction hypothesis yields

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime\prime}:U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U),

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime\prime}\equiv u^{\prime}:U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}:U).

(f)

Let us prove by induction on
h
that the claim holds whenever
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)=h
.

Given
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)
such that
\textsf{ht}(J)=h
, assume the statement for
0
, …,
\textsf{ht}(J)-1
, and let
I
be an initial inference of
J
.

If
I
is (var), (t-a) or (t-sub), then
U=\mathcal{S}(u)
. Hence the first inequality holds trivially, and the second one follows, using items (c) and (d), from

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}),\qquad\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort})=\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})+1.

If
I
is (seq/t), it has premises
\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort}
and
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U^{\prime}
. Now, the induction hypothesis implies that
\textbf{X}\vdash u:\mathcal{S}(u)
is derivable and

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U^{\prime})\geq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u:\mathcal{S}(u)).

Also, since
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash\mathcal{S}(u)\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort})
,
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash U^{\prime}\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort})<h
, an instance of (s3) yields
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash\mathcal{S}(u)\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort})\leq h
.

(g)

It follows directly from (f) and the definition of
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash u\;\mathsf{term})
.

(h)

Let
I
be an initial inference of
\textbf{X}\vdash T(f_{1},...,f_{k})\;\mathsf{sort}
. Then
I
is of the form (s-a) or (s-sub) and, in either case, it has a premise
\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}:U
for each
i=1
, …,
k
. Now, by item (f) we have

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash T(f_{1},...,f_{k})\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}:U)\geq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}:\mathcal{S}(f_{i})).

If
\textbf{X}\vdash T(f_{1},...,f_{k})\;\mathsf{sort}
is not an axiom, then
I
is (s-sub); if
\textbf{A}\vdash T(a_{1},...,a_{k})\;\mathsf{sort}
is the axiom that introduces
T
, by using the above inequalities and comparing the premises of (s-sub) with the definition of
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A})
we obtain

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash T(f_{1},...,t_{k})\;\mathsf{sort})>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}).

(i)

We will verify by induction on
h
that the claim holds whenever
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{k}):U)=h
.

Given
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{k}):U)
such that
\textsf{ht}(J)=h
, assume the statement for
0
, …,
\textsf{ht}(J)-1
, and let
I
be an initial inference of
J
.

If
I
is (seq/t), then it has a premise
\textbf{X}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{k}):U^{\prime}
, and by the induction hypothesis we obtain

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{k}):U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{k}):U^{\prime})\geq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}:\mathcal{S}(f_{i}))

for
i=1
, …,
k
. If
I
is (t-a) or (t-sub), then it has a premise of the form
\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}:V
for each
i
; by (f) and (g) we conclude that

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{k}):U)>\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}:V)\geq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}:\mathcal{S}(f_{i}))=\textsf{ht}(\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}\;\mathsf{term}).

(j)

Immediate from (f), (g), and the definitions of
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y})
and
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y})
.

∎

A.5
The substitution property

Definition A.16
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a generalized algebraic theory. We say that a derivable standard judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash\blacksquare
has the
substitution property
if for every context morphism
\textbf{f}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{X}
the judgment
\textbf{Y}\vdash\blacksquare[\textbf{f}]
is derivable.
17
17

17

Here, “
\blacksquare
” stands for “
U\;\mathsf{sort}
”, “
u:U
”, “
U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}
”, or “
u\equiv u^{\prime}:U
”, and “
\blacksquare[\textbf{f}]
” denotes “
U[\textbf{f}]\;\mathsf{sort}
”, “
u[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}]
”, “
U[\textbf{f}]\equiv U^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]\;\mathsf{sort}
”, or “
u[\textbf{f}]\equiv u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}]
”, respectively.

The following result is essentially the same as the
substitution lemma
from
[
11
]
:

Proposition A.17
.

Every derivable standard judgment has the substitution property.

Proof sketch.

It suffices to check that for any inference step in the list from Definition
A.8
whose premises are derivable and whose conclusion is a standard judgment (i.e. we do not consider (ctx)), the conclusion has the substitution property provided that the premises do.

We have different approaches depending on the kind of inference rule:

•

For an inference rule among (s1), (s2), (s3), (t1), (t2), (t3), (seq/t), (seq/teq), we apply substitution to each of the premises in order to obtain an inference step of the same kind. For example, suppose that each premise in

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u:U^{\prime}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}:U^{\prime}}{\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U^{\prime}}\text{(seq/teq)}

has the substitution property, and consider a context morphism
\textbf{f}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{X}
. Then the judgments

\textbf{Y}\vdash U[\textbf{f}]\equiv U^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]\;\mathsf{sort}\qquad\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]\equiv u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}]\qquad\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]:U^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]\qquad\textbf{Y}\vdash u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]:U^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]

are derivable, so by (seq/teq) we can derive
\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]\equiv u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]:U^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]
. The other inference rules are treated analogously.

•

For
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
, suppose that the premise of

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash X_{i}\;\mathsf{sort}}{\textbf{X}\vdash x_{i}:X_{i}}\text{(var)}

has the substitution property. For a morphism
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n}):\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{X}
, we have that
\textbf{Y}\vdash x_{i}[\textbf{f}]:X_{i}[\textbf{f}]
is
\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}:X_{i}[\textbf{f}]
, which is derivable by definition of a context morphism.

•

For an inference rule among (s-a), (t-a), (seq-a), (teq-a), applying substitution to its conclusion yields a derivable judgment by (s-sub), (t-sub), (seq-sub-1), (teq-sub-1), respectively. For example, consider a context
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
, an axiom
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U)
, and suppose that each premise in

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash u:U\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}:U}{\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U}\text{(teq-a)}

has the substitution property. Given a context morphism
\textbf{f}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{X}
, we obtain derivable judgments

J^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}]),\qquad J^{\prime\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}]).

Also, for
i=1
, …,
n
let
J_{i}
be the (derivable) judgment
\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}:X_{i}[\textbf{f}]
. Then, by (teq-sub-1), we can use
J
,
J^{\prime}
,
J^{\prime\prime}
,
J_{1}
, …,
J_{n}
to derive
\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]\equiv u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}]
.

The other kinds of axioms are treated similarly.

•

For an inference rule among (s-sub), (t-sub), (seq-sub-1), (seq-sub-2), (teq-sub-1), (teq-sub-2), an application of substitution to its conclusion can be described by substitution of an axiom along a certain composite premorphism.

To illustrate how this is done, let us work out the cases (teq-sub-1) and (teq-sub-2). The argument for (s-sub), (t-sub), (seq-sub-1) and (seq-sub-2) is analogous.

For (teq-sub-1), suppose given an axiom
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U)
, where
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
, and a context morphism
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n}):\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{X}
. For
i=1
, …,
n
, let
J_{i}
be

\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}:X_{i}[\textbf{f}].

Let
J^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}])
and
J^{\prime\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}])
.

Assume that each premise in

\frac{J\quad J^{\prime}\quad J^{\prime\prime}\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]\equiv u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}]}\text{(teq-sub-1)}

has the substitution property, and consider a morphism
\textbf{g}:\textbf{Z}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
. Then substitution along
g
turns

–

J^{\prime}
into a derivable judgment
\textbf{Z}\vdash u[\textbf{f}][\textbf{g}]:U[\textbf{f}][\textbf{g}]
, which is

\textbf{Z}\vdash u[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{g}]:U[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{g}].

–

J^{\prime\prime}
into a derivable judgment
\textbf{Z}\vdash u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}][\textbf{g}]:U[\textbf{f}][\textbf{g}]
, which is

\textbf{Z}\vdash u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{g}]:U[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{g}].

–

J_{i}
into a derivable judgment
\textbf{Z}\vdash f_{i}[\textbf{g}]:X_{i}[\textbf{f}][\textbf{g}]
, which is

\textbf{Z}\vdash(\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{g})_{i}:X_{i}[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{g}].

Now, by (teq-sub-1) we can derive
\textbf{Z}\vdash u[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{g}]\equiv u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{g}]:U[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{g}]
. This proves that
\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]\equiv u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}]
has the substitution property.

For (teq-sub-2), suppose given an axiom
J=(\textbf{X}\vdash u:U)
, where
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
, and premorphisms
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n})
,
\textbf{g}=(g_{1},...,g_{n}):\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{X}
. For
i=1
, …,
n
, let
J_{i}
be

\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}\equiv g_{i}:X_{i}[\textbf{f}].

Let
J^{\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}])
and
J^{\prime\prime}=(\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{g}]:U[\textbf{f}])
.

Assume that each premise in

\frac{J\quad J^{\prime}\quad J^{\prime\prime}\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]\equiv u[\textbf{g}]:U[\textbf{f}]}\text{(teq-sub-2)}

has the substitution property, and consider a context morphism
\textbf{h}:\textbf{Z}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
. Then substitution along
h
turns

–

J^{\prime}
into the derivable judgment
\textbf{Z}\vdash u[\textbf{f}][\textbf{h}]:U[\textbf{f}][\textbf{h}]
, which is
\textbf{Z}\vdash u[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{h}]:U[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{h}]
.

–

J^{\prime\prime}
into the derivable judgment
\textbf{Z}\vdash u[\textbf{g}][\textbf{h}]:U[\textbf{f}][\textbf{h}]
, which is
\textbf{Z}\vdash u[\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{h}]:U[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{h}]
.

–

J_{i}
into the derivable judgment
\textbf{Z}\vdash f_{i}[\textbf{h}]\equiv g_{i}[\textbf{h}]:X_{i}[\textbf{f}][\textbf{h}]
, which is
\textbf{Z}\vdash(\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{h})_{i}\equiv(\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{h})_{i}:X_{i}[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{h}]
.

By (teq-sub-2) we can derive
\textbf{Z}\vdash u[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{h}]\equiv u[\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{h}]:U[\textbf{f}\circ\textbf{h}]
. This proves that
\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]\equiv u[\textbf{g}]:U[\textbf{f}]
has the substitution property.

∎

Corollary A.18
.

Given context morphisms
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
and
\textbf{g}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{Z}
, the premorphism
\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{f}
(see Definition
A.14
) is a morphism from
X
to
Z
.

Proof.

Writing
\textbf{Z}=(z_{1}:Z_{1},...,z_{n}:Z_{n})
and
\textbf{g}=(g_{1},...,g_{n})
, by assumption we have that
\textbf{Y}\vdash g_{i}:Z_{i}[\textbf{g}]
is derivable for
1\leq i\leq n
. Since every derivable judgment has the substitution property,
\textbf{X}\vdash g_{i}[\textbf{f}]:Z_{i}[\textbf{g}][\textbf{f}]
is derivable. As the latter equals
\textbf{X}\vdash(\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{f})_{i}:Z_{i}[\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{f}]
, we conclude that
\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{f}
is a morphism from
X
to
Z
.
∎

Corollary A.19
.

For a context
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
, the judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash X_{i}\;\mathsf{sort}
is derivable for
i=1
, …,
n
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction. Given
i\in\{1,...,n\}
, suppose that
\textbf{X}\vdash X_{j}
is derivable for all
j<i
. By using (var), we derive
\textbf{X}\vdash x_{j}:X_{j}
for each
j<i
. Then we have a context morphism
(x_{1},...,x_{i-1}):\textbf{X}\rightarrow\partial_{i-1}\textbf{X}
and, as
\partial_{i-1}\textbf{X}\vdash X_{i}
has the substitution property, we conclude that
\textbf{X}\vdash X_{i}
is derivable.
∎

A.6
Some results on equality judgments

Proposition A.20
.

For a pretheory
\mathbb{T}
:

(1)

If
\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
is derivable, then, writing
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
, the judgment

\textbf{X}\vdash Y_{i}[\textbf{f}]\equiv Y_{i}[\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{sort}

is derivable for
i=1
, …,
n
.

(2)

If
\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
is derivable, then so are
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
and
\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
.

(3)

If
\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
and
\textbf{Y}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
are derivable, then so is

\textbf{X}\vdash U[\textbf{f}]\equiv U[\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{sort}.

(4)

If
\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
and
\textbf{Y}\vdash u:U
are derivable, then so is

\textbf{X}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]\equiv u[\textbf{g}]:U[\textbf{f}].

(5)

If
\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
and
\textbf{h}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{Z}
are derivable, then so is
\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Z}
.

Proof.

We will prove by well-founded induction (with respect to the poset
\omega\times\omega
) that, for all
h
,
n\geq 0
, the above statements hold whenever all the structures involved have height at most
h
, and
Y
has length-
n
.

Below, for a given
h
we verify each item under the assumption that all items hold with respect to every
h^{\prime}<h
.

(1)

If
\textbf{Y}=\emptyset
, the claim holds vacuously. Otherwise, let
i\in\{1,...,n\}
. Since

\textsf{ht}(\partial_{i-1}\textbf{f}\equiv\partial_{i-1}\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\partial_{i-1}\textbf{Y})\leq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y})

\textsf{ht}(\partial_{i-1}\textbf{Y}\vdash Y_{i})\leq\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}),

\boldsymbol{\ell}(\partial_{i-1}\textbf{Y})<\boldsymbol{\ell}(\textbf{Y}),

by item (1) we can derive
\textbf{X}\vdash Y_{i}[\partial_{i-1}\textbf{f}]\equiv Y_{i}[\partial_{i-1}\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{sort}
, which equals
\textbf{X}\vdash Y_{i}[\textbf{f}]\equiv Y_{i}[\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{sort}
.

(2)

Write
\textbf{Y}=(y_{1}:Y_{1},...,y_{n}:Y_{n})
,
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n})
, and
\textbf{g}=(g_{1},...,g_{n})
. By assumption,
\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}\equiv g_{i}:Y_{i}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid x_{i-1}]
is derivable for
i=1
, …,
n
. By Proposition
A.15
, so are the judgments
\textbf{X}\vdash f_{i}:Y_{i}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid x_{i-1}]
, hence also
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
. On the other hand,
\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
is obtained from
\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
by using item (1) and (seq/teq).

(3)

Express
U=V[\textbf{h}]
for an axiom
\textbf{A}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
and a morphism
\textbf{h}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
. Since
\textsf{ht}(\textbf{h}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{A})<\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort})
, by item (5) we can derive
\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
. By item (2), we have context morphisms
\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}
,
\textbf{g}\circ\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
; it now follows, as
\textbf{A}\vdash V\;\mathsf{sort}
has the substitution property, that
\textbf{X}\vdash V[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]\;\mathsf{sort}
and
\textbf{X}\vdash V[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{sort}
are derivable. This allows us to obtain from (seq-sub-2) the judgment
\textbf{X}\vdash V[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]\equiv V[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{sort}
, which equals
\textbf{X}\vdash U[\textbf{f}]\equiv U[\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{sort}
.

(4)

Express
u=v[\textbf{h}]
for an axiom
\textbf{A}\vdash v:V\;\mathsf{sort}
and a morphism
\textbf{h}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
. Then, by Proposition
A.15
,

\textsf{ht}(\textbf{h}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{A}),\;\;\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash V[\textbf{h}]\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort})<\textsf{ht}(\textbf{Y}\vdash u:U).

By item (5), we can derive
\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
, from which (2) yields morphisms
\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}
,
\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}
. As
\textbf{A}\vdash v:V
has the substitution property, we can derive
\textbf{X}\vdash v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]:V[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]
and
\textbf{Y}\vdash v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}]:V[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}]
.

Also, by applying (3) to
\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
and
\textbf{Y}\vdash V[\textbf{h}]\;\mathsf{sort}
we derive
\textbf{X}\vdash V[\textbf{h}][\textbf{f}]\equiv V[\textbf{h}][\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{sort}
; and from the latter, by (s2) and (seq/t), we obtain
\textbf{X}\vdash v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}]:V[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]
.

Now, note that
\textbf{Y}\vdash V[\textbf{h}]\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort}
having the substitution property yields
\textbf{Y}\vdash V[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]\equiv U[\textbf{f}]\;\mathsf{sort}
; hence the desired judgment is derived via

\frac{\textbf{A}\vdash v:V\hskip 9.24994pt\textbf{X}\vdash v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]:V[\textbf{h}][\textbf{f}]\hskip 9.24994pt\textbf{X}\vdash v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}]:V[\textbf{h}][\textbf{f}]\hskip 9.24994pt\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{A}}{\textbf{X}\vdash v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]\equiv v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}]:V[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]}\text{(teq-sub-2)},

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash V[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]\equiv U[\textbf{f}]\;\mathsf{sort}\hskip 9.24994pt\textbf{X}\vdash v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]\equiv v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}]:V[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]\hskip 9.24994pt\textbf{X}\vdash v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}]\hskip 9.24994pt\textbf{X}\vdash v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}]:U[\textbf{f}]}{\textbf{X}\vdash v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]\equiv v[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}]:U[\textbf{f}]}\text{(seq/teq)}.

(5)

Let
\textbf{Z}=(z_{1}:Z_{1},...,z_{p}:Z_{p})
and
\textbf{h}=(h_{1},...,h_{p})
. Then for
i=1
, …,
p
, the judgment
\textbf{Y}\vdash h_{i}:Z_{i}[\textbf{h}]
is derivable and its height is at most that of
\textbf{h}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{Z}
. By item (4) we obtain
\textbf{X}\vdash h_{i}[\textbf{f}]\equiv h_{i}[\textbf{g}]:Z_{i}[\textbf{h}][\textbf{f}]
, which equals
\textbf{X}\vdash(\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f})_{i}\equiv(\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g})_{i}:Z_{i}[\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}]
. This means, in turn, that
\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{h}\circ\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Z}
is derivable.

∎

A.7
Comparing the derivation rules with Cartmell’s

We now provide the deferred proof that, in a pretheory, a judgment is derivable from the inference rules described above precisely when it is derivable from the ones given in
[
11
]
.

Written in a different but equivalent form, Cartmell considers the following inference rules (where, when a rule is already in the list considered previously, we only write its name):

(ctx)

(s1)

(s2)

(s3)

(t1)

(t2)

(t3)

(seq/t)

(s-a)

(t-a)

(seq-a)

(teq-a)

(s-sub)

(seq/teq)’

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U}{\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U^{\prime}}.

(var)’

\frac{x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\;\textsf{ctx}}{x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash x_{i}:X_{i}}

where
n\geq 1
and
1\leq i\leq n
.

(t-sub)’

Suppose given a term axiom
J
, say
\textbf{X}\vdash t(x_{1},...,x_{n}):U
where
X
is
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}
(possibly with
n=0
), a precontext
Y
, and term expressions
f_{1}
, …,
f_{n}
. For
i=1
, …,
n
, let
J_{i}
be the judgment

\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}:X_{i}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid x_{i-1}].

We consider

\frac{J\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{n}):U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]}.

(seq-sub)’

Suppose given a sort equality judgment
J
, say
\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}
where
\textbf{X}=(x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n})
. Suppose given a precontext
Y
, term expressions
f_{1}
, …,
f_{n}
,
g_{1}
, …,
g_{n}
, and, for
i=1
