, …,
n
, let
J_{i}
be the judgment

\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}\equiv g_{i}:X_{i}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid x_{i-1}].

Then we consider

\frac{J\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]\equiv U^{\prime}[g_{1}\mid x_{1},...,g_{n}\mid x_{n}]\;\mathsf{sort}}.

(teq-sub)’

Suppose given a term equality judgment
J
, say
\Gamma\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U
where
\Gamma
is
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}
. Suppose given a precontext
Y
, term expressions
f_{1}
, …,
f_{n}
,
g_{1}
, …,
g_{n}
, and, for
i=1
, …,
n
, let
J_{i}
be the judgment

\textbf{Y}\vdash f_{i}\equiv g_{i}:X_{i}[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{i-1}\mid x_{i-1}].

Then we consider

\frac{J\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash u[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]\equiv u^{\prime}[g_{1}\mid x_{1},...,g_{n}\mid x_{n}]:U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]}.

The idea is that by considering (seq/teq), (var), (t-sub) instead of (seq/teq)’, (var)’, (t-sub)’, more premises are required to derive a given judgment, but the extra premises are themselves proved, inductively, to be derivable. Also, the role of (seq-sub)’ is played by (seq-sub-1)’ and (seq-sub-2), and that of (teq-sub)’ is played by (teq-sub-1) and (teq-sub-2).

In what follows, we will say that a judgment is
C-derivable
if it is derivable from Cartmell’s inference rules as listed above. When we say that a judgment is
derivable
we will mean so in the sense of Definition
A.8
.

Proposition A.21
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a pretheory. Then a judgment is C-derivable if and only if it is derivable.

Proof.

Let us start by verifying the claim in the simpler direction: that every derivable judgment is C-derivable – in other words, Cartmell’s rules are at least as strong as the ones from Definition
A.8
.

Firstly, each of Cartmell’s rules except for (var)’, (seq-sub)’ and (teq-sub)’ is obtained by removing some (or none) of the premises from a rule in Definition
A.8
.

Also, (seq-sub)’ is at least as strong as both (seq-sub-1) and (seq-sub-2): (seq-sub-1) corresponds to the case where the lists of expressions
(f_{1},...,f_{n})
and
(g_{1},...,g_{n})
are equal, and (seq-sub-2) to the case where
U
and
U^{\prime}
are equal. In the first case, starting from the premises of (seq-sub-1), we use (t1) to derive the sequence of term equalities
J_{1}
, …,
J_{n}
required for (seq-sub)’; in the second case, we use (s1) to derive the sort equality
J^{\prime}=(\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U\;\mathsf{sort})
required for (seq-sub)’.

For (var)’, the premise
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash X_{i}\;\mathsf{sort}
is replaced by
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\;\textsf{ctx}
; but the former being C-derivable implies that so is the latter.

On the other hand, the proof that the rules from Def.
A.8
are as strong as Cartmell’s requires more work.

We will verify that for each of Cartmell’s derivation steps that are not in Def.
A.8
, if all of its premises are derivable, then so is its conclusion. We have the following cases:

(seq/teq)’

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U}{\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U^{\prime}}.

If the premises are derivable, by Proposition
A.15
so is
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U
. Now, by using (seq/t) we derive
\textbf{X}\vdash u:U^{\prime}
. Similarly, we can derive
\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}:U^{\prime}
. The desired judgment is then obtained by

\frac{\textbf{X}\vdash U\equiv U^{\prime}\;\mathsf{sort}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u:U^{\prime}\quad\textbf{X}\vdash u^{\prime}:U^{\prime}}{\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U^{\prime}}\text{(seq/teq)}.

(var)’

\frac{x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\;\textsf{ctx}}{x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash x_{i}:X_{i}}.

Here, by Corollary
A.19
we can derive
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash X_{i}\;\mathsf{sort}
, so from (var) we obtain
x_{1}:X_{1},...,x_{n}:X_{n}\vdash x_{i}:X_{i}
.

(t-sub)’

\frac{J\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{n}):U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]},

where we use the notation of the list above the proposition’s statement. If the premises are derivable, then we have a morphism
\textbf{f}:\textbf{Y}\rightarrow\textbf{X}
and, as
J
has the substitution property,
\textbf{Y}\vdash t(f_{1},...,f_{n}):U[\textbf{f}]
is derivable, as required.

(seq-sub)’

\frac{J\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]\equiv U^{\prime}[g_{1}\mid x_{1},...,g_{n}\mid x_{n}]\;\mathsf{sort}},

where we use the notation of the list above the proposition’s statement. Assume that the premises are derivable. Then, letting
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n})
and
\textbf{g}=(g_{1},...,g_{n})
, we have that
\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
is derivable. On the one hand, since
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
is a morphism (Proposition
A.20
(2)) and
\textbf{X}\vdash U\;\mathsf{sort}
has the substitution property,
\textbf{Y}\vdash U[\textbf{f}]\equiv U^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]
is derivable. On the other hand, Proposition
A.20
(3) yields
\textbf{Y}\vdash U^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]\equiv U^{\prime}[\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{sort}
. These two judgments allow us to derive
\textbf{Y}\vdash U[\textbf{f}]\equiv U^{\prime}[\textbf{g}]\;\mathsf{sort}
via (s3).

(teq-sub)’

\frac{J\quad J_{1}\quad\cdots\quad J_{n}}{\textbf{Y}\vdash u[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]\equiv u^{\prime}[f^{\prime}_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f^{\prime}_{n}\mid x_{n}]:U[f_{1}\mid x_{1},...,f_{n}\mid x_{n}]},

where we use the notation of the list above the proposition’s statement. Assume that the premises are derivable. Then, letting
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n})
and
\textbf{g}=(g_{1},...,g_{n})
, we have that
\textbf{f}\equiv\textbf{g}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
is derivable. On the one hand, since
\textbf{f}:\textbf{X}\rightarrow\textbf{Y}
is a morphism (Proposition
A.20
(2)) and
\textbf{X}\vdash u\equiv u^{\prime}:U
has the substitution property,
\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]\equiv u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]:U[\textbf{f}]
is derivable. On the other hand, Proposition
A.20
(4) yields
\textbf{Y}\vdash u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}]\equiv u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}^{\prime}]:U[\textbf{f}]
. These two judgments allow us to derive
\textbf{Y}\vdash u[\textbf{f}]\equiv u^{\prime}[\textbf{f}^{\prime}]:U[\textbf{f}]
via (t3).

This concludes the proof that if a judgment is C-derivable, then it is derivable.
∎

As a consequence, we can use all known concepts and results related to generalized algebraic theories, such as those in
[
10
]
and
[
11
]
. In particular, we will use the definition of a morphism of generalized algebraic theories, the corresponding category
GAT
, the category
Cont
of contextual categories and contextual functors, and the equivalence of categories
\mathcal{C}:\text{GAT}\rightarrow\text{Cont}
that assigns to each theory
\mathbb{T}
its
syntactic category
.
